\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{k}
for the expectation conditioned on
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{k}
,
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{k}[\,.\,]=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\,.\,|\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{k}]
.
By the tower property,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathsf{Tr}\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i})]={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{0}\dots\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{n-1}\mathsf{Tr}\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{X}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{0}\dots\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{n-2}\mathsf{Tr}\exp\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathbf{X}_{i}+\log\underbrace{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{n-1}e^{\mathbf{X}_{n}}}_{=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{E}e^{\mathbf{X}_{n}}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{\mathbf{X}_{i}}]),

where we applied Lieb’s theorem, Theorem
1.21
on the conditional expectation and then used the independence of
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{n}
.
∎

5.3
Tail bounds for sums of independent matrices

Theorem 1.24

(Master tail bound)
.

For all
t\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i})\geq t)\leq\inf_{\theta>0}\{e^{-\theta t}\mathsf{Tr}\exp(\sum_{i}\log\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}e^{\theta\mathbf{X}_{i}})\}

Proof.

Combine Lemma
1.23
with Proposition
1.20
.
∎

Corollary 1.25
.

Assume that there is a function
g:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty]
and fixed symmetric matrices
\mathbf{A}_{i}
such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{\theta\mathbf{X}_{i}}]\preceq e^{g(\theta)\mathbf{A}_{i}}
for all
i
.
Set

\rho=\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{A}_{k}).

Then, for all
t\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i})\geq t)\leq d\inf_{\theta>0}\{e^{-\theta t+g(\theta)\rho}\}.

Proof.

Using the operator monoticity of
\log
, (
1.9
), and the monotonicity of
\mathsf{Tr}\exp
, (
1.8
), we can plug the estimates for the matrix mgfs into the master inequality, Theorem
1.24
, to obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i})\geq t)\leq{}

\displaystyle e^{-\theta t}\mathsf{Tr}\exp(g(\theta)\sum_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle de^{-\theta t}\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\exp(g(\theta)\sum_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}))

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle de^{-\theta t}\exp(g(\theta)\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i})),

where we estimated
\mathsf{Tr}(\mathbf{X})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\leq d\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}
and used the spectral theorem.
∎

Now we are ready to prove Bernstein’s inequality We just need to come up with a dominating function for Corollary
1.25
.

Lemma 1.26
.

If
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}]=0
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{X})\leq 1
, then

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{\theta\mathbf{X}}]\preceq\exp((e^{\theta}-\theta-1)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2}]).

Proof.

Define

f(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \frac{e^{\theta x}-\theta x-1}{x^{2}},{}\quad&x\neq 0,\\
\frac{\theta^{2}}{2},\quad&x=0,\end{aligned}\right.

and verify that this is a smooth and increasing function on
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
.
Hence,
f(x)\leq f(1)
for
x\leq 1
.
By the transfer rule, (
1.7
),
f(\mathbf{X})\preceq f(I)=f(1)I
.
Therefore,

e^{\theta\mathbf{X}}=I+\theta\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{X}f(\mathbf{X})\mathbf{X}\preceq I+\theta\mathbf{X}+f(1)\mathbf{X}^{2},

and by taking expectations,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{\theta\mathbf{X}}]\preceq I+f(1)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2}]\preceq\exp(f(1)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2}])=\exp((e^{\theta}-\theta-1)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}^{2}]).

∎

Theorem 1.27

(Matrix Bernstein)
.

Assume
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\mathbf{X}_{i}=0
and
\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\mathbf{X}_{i})\leq R
almost surely for all
i
and set

\sigma^{2}=\|\sum_{i}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}^{2}]\|_{\mathrm{op}}.

Then, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i})\geq t)\leq{}

\displaystyle d\exp(-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{R^{2}}h(Rt/\sigma^{2}))

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle d\exp\left(\frac{-t^{2}/2}{\sigma^{2}+Rt/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} d\exp\left(-\frac{3t^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right),\quad&t\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{R},\\
d\exp\left(-\frac{3t}{8R}\right),\quad&t\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{R},\end{aligned}\right.

where
h(u)=(1+u)\log(1+u)-u
,
u>0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, take
R=1
.
By Lemma
1.26
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{\theta\mathbf{X}_{i}}]\preceq\exp(g(\theta)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathbf{X}_{i}^{2}]),\quad\text{with }g(\theta)=e^{\theta}-\theta-1,\quad\theta>0.

By Corollary
1.25
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\sum_{i}\mathbf{X}_{i})\geq t)\leq d\exp(-\theta t+g(\theta)\sigma^{2}).

We can verify that the minimal value is attained at
\theta=\log(1+t/\sigma^{2})
.

The second inequality then follows from the fact that
h(u)\geq h_{2}(u)=\frac{u^{2}/2}{1+u/3}
for
u\geq 0
which can be verified by comparing derivatives.

The third inequality follows from exploiting the properties of
h_{2}
.
∎

With similar techniques, one can also prove a version of Hoeffding’s inequality for matrices, see
[
Tro12
, Theorem 1.3]
.

6
Problem set

Problem 1.1
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be independent random variables such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}(X_{i})=0
and
X_{i}\sim\mathsf{subE}(\lambda)
. For any vector
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})^{\top}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
, define the weighted sum

S(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}X_{i}\,,

Show that for any
t>0
we have

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(|S(a)|>t)\leq 2\exp\left[-C\big(\frac{t^{2}}{\lambda^{2}|a|_{2}^{2}}\wedge\frac{t}{\lambda|a|_{\infty}}\big)\right].

for some positive constant
C
.

Problem 1.2
.

A random variable
X
has
\chi^{2}_{n}
(chi-squared with
n
degrees of freedom) if it has the same distribution as

, where

are i.i.d.

.

(a)

Let

. Show that the moment generating function of

satisfies

\phi(s):=E\big[e^{sY}\big]=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\frac{e^{-s}}{\sqrt{1-2s}}&\text{if }s<1/2\\
\infty&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(b)

,

(c)

[Hint: you can use the convexity inequality
\sqrt{1+u}\leq 1+u/2
]
.

(d)

Show that if

, then, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it holds

X\leq n+2\sqrt{n\log(1/\delta)}+2\log(1/\delta)\,.

.

Let

. Show that for

large enough, we get

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\max_{i\geq 2}X_{i}\big]<\infty\,.

.

Problem 1.5
.

Recover from this bound that

Problem 1.6
.

.

Problem 1.7
.

For any

Problem 1.8
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}

Problem 1.9
.

The goal of this problem is to prove the following theorem:

Theorem 1.28

(Johnson-Lindenstrauss Lemma)
.

Given
n
points denoted by
X=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, let
Q\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{k\times d}
be a random projection operator and set
P:=\sqrt{\frac{d}{k}}Q
.
There is a constant
C>0
such that if

k\geq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log n,

P
is an
\varepsilon
-isometry
for
X
, i.e.,

(1-\varepsilon)\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|Px_{i}-Px_{j}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}^{2},\quad\text{for all }i,j

with propability at least
1-2\exp(-c\varepsilon^{2}k)
.

1.

Convince yourself that if
d>n
, there is a projection
P\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times d}
to an
n
dimensional subspace such that
\|Px_{i}-Px_{j}\|_{2}=\|x_{i}-x_{j}\|_{2}
,
i
.
e
., pairwise distances are exactly preserved.

Let
k\leq d
be two integers,
Y=(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d\times d})
independent and identically distributed Gaussians and
Q\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times k}
the projection onto the first
k
coordinates,
i
.
e
.,
Qy=(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})
.
Define
Z=\frac{1}{\|Y\|}QY=\frac{1}{\|Y\|}(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})
and
L=\|Z\|^{2}
.

2.

Show that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[L]=\frac{k}{d}
.

3.

Show that for all
t>0
such that
1-2t(k\beta-d)>0
and
1-2t\beta k>0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}^{2}\leq\beta\frac{k}{d}\sum_{i=1}^{d}y_{i}^{2}\right)\leq(1-2t(k\beta-d))^{-k/2}(1-2t\beta k)^{-(d-k)/2}

(Hint: Show that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\lambda X^{2}}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2\lambda}}
for
\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
if
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.)

4.

Conclude that for
\beta<1
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(L\leq\beta\frac{k}{d}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{k}{2}(1-\beta+\log\beta)\right).

5.

Similarly, show that for
\beta>1
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(L\geq\beta\frac{k}{d}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{k}{2}(1-\beta+\log\beta)\right).

6.

Show that for a random projection operator
Q\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{k\times d}
and a fixed vector

,

(a)

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\|Qx\|^{2}]=\frac{k}{d}\|x\|^{2}
.

(b)

For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
c>0
such that with probability at least

,

(1-\varepsilon)\frac{k}{d}\|x\|^{2}\leq\|Qx\|_{2}^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\frac{k}{d}\|x\|_{2}^{2}.

(Hint: Think about how to apply the previous results in this case and use the inequalities
\log(1-\varepsilon)\leq-\varepsilon-\varepsilon^{2}/2
and

.)

Prove Theorem
1.28
.

Chapter 2
Linear Regression Model

In this chapter, we consider the following regression model:

Y_{i}=f(X_{i})+\varepsilon_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n\,,

(2.1)

where
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}
is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}
and such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\varepsilon]=0
. Our goal is to estimate the function
f
under a linear assumption. Namely, we assume that
x\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
and
f(x)=x^{\top}\theta^{*}
for some unknown
\theta^{*}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
.

7
Fixed design linear regression

Depending on the nature of the
design
points
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, we will favor a different measure of risk. In particular, we will focus either on
fixed
or
random
design.

7.1
Random design

The case of random design corresponds to the statistical learning setup. Let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n+1},Y_{n+1})
be
n+1
i.i.d. random couples. Given the pairs
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
, the goal is construct a function
\hat{f}_{n}
such that
\hat{f}_{n}(X_{n+1})
is a good predictor of
Y_{n+1}
. Note that when
\hat{f}_{n}
is constructed,
X_{n+1}
is still unknown and we have to account for what value it is likely to take.

Consider the following example from
[
HTF01
, Section 3.2]
. The response variable
Y
is the log-volume of a cancerous tumor, and the goal is to predict it based on
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{6}
, a collection of variables that are easier to measure (age of patient, log-weight of prostate, …). Here the goal is clearly to construct
f
for
prediction
purposes. Indeed, we want to find an automatic mechanism that outputs a good prediction of the log-weight of the tumor given certain inputs for a new (unseen) patient.

A natural measure of performance here is the
L_{2}
-risk employed in the introduction:

R(\hat{f}_{n})=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y_{n+1}-\hat{f}_{n}(X_{n+1})]^{2}=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y_{n+1}-f(X_{n+1})]^{2}+\|\hat{f}_{n}-f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}\,,

where
P_{X}
denotes the marginal distribution of
X_{n+1}
. It measures how good the prediction of
Y_{n+1}
is in average over realizations of
X_{n+1}
. In particular, it does not put much emphasis on values of
X_{n+1}
that are not very likely to occur.

Note that if the
\varepsilon_{i}
are random variables with variance
\sigma^{2}
, then one simply has
R(\hat{f}_{n})=\sigma^{2}+\|\hat{f}_{n}-f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}
. Therefore, for random design, we will focus on the squared
L_{2}
norm
\|\hat{f}_{n}-f\|^{2}_{L^{2}(P_{X})}
as a measure of accuracy. It measures how close
\hat{f}_{n}
is to the unknown
f

in average
over realizations of
X_{n+1}
.

7.2
Fixed design

In fixed design, the points (or vectors)
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are
deterministic
. To emphasize this fact, we use lowercase letters
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
to denote fixed design. Of course, we can always think of them as realizations of a random variable but the distinction between fixed and random design is deeper and significantly affects our measure of performance. Indeed, recall that for random design, we look at the performance
in average
over realizations of
X_{n+1}
. Here, there is no such thing as a marginal distribution of
X_{n+1}
.
Rather, since the design points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are considered deterministic, our goal is to estimate
f

only
at these points. This problem is sometimes called
denoising
since our goal is to recover
f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})
given noisy observations of these values.

In many instances, fixed designs exhibit particular structures.
A typical example is the
regular design
on
[0,1]
, given by
x_{i}=i/n
,
i=1,\ldots,n
. Interpolation between these points is possible under smoothness assumptions.

Note that in fixed design, we observe
\mu^{*}+\varepsilon
, where
\mu^{*}=\big(f(x_{1}),\ldots,f(x_{n})\big)^{\top}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}
. Instead of a functional estimation problem, it is often simpler to view this problem as a vector problem in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
. This point of view will allow us to leverage the Euclidean geometry of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
.

In the case of fixed design, we will focus on the
Mean Squared Error
(MSE) as a measure of performance. It is defined by

\mathsf{MSE}(\hat{f}_{n})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\hat{f}_{n}(x_{i})-f(x_{i})\big)^{2}\,.

Equivalently, if we view our problem as a vector problem, it is defined by

\mathsf{MSE}(\hat{\mu})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\hat{\mu}_{i}-\mu^{*}_{i}\big)^{2}=\frac{1}{n}|\hat{\mu}-\mu^{*}|_{2}^{2}\,.

Often, the design vectors
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
are stored in an
n\times d
design matrix
\mathbb{X}
, whose
j
th row is given by
x_{j}^{\top}
. With this notation, the linear regression model can be written as

Y=\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}+\varepsilon\,,

(2.2)

where
Y=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n})^{\top}
and
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}
. Moreover,

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta})=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*})|_{2}^{2}=(\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*})^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*})\,.

(2.3)

A natural example of fixed design regression is image denoising. Assume that
\mu^{*}_{i},i\in 1,\ldots,n
is the grayscale value of pixel
i
of an image. We do not get to observe the image
\mu^{*}
but rather a noisy version of it
Y=\mu^{*}+\varepsilon
. Given a library of
d
images
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}\},x_{j}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
, our goal is to recover the original image
\mu^{*}
using linear combinations of the images
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
. This can be done fairly accurately (see Figure
2.5
).

Figure 2.5:
Reconstruction of the digit “6”: Original (left), Noisy (middle) and Reconstruction (right). Here
n=16\times 16=256
pixels. Source
[
RT11
]
.

As we will see in Remark
2.3
, properly choosing the design also ensures that if
\mathsf{MSE}(\hat{f})
is small for some linear estimator
\hat{f}(x)=x^{\top}\hat{\theta}
, then
|\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}
is also small.

In this chapter we only consider the fixed design case.

8
Least squares estimators

Throughout this section, we consider the regression model (
2.2
) with fixed design.

8.1
Unconstrained least squares estimator

Define the (unconstrained)
least squares estimator

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
to be any vector such that

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}\,.

Note that we are interested in estimating
\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}
and not
\theta^{*}
itself, so by extension, we also call
\hat{\mu}^{\textsc{ls}}=\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
least squares estimator. Observe that
\hat{\mu}^{\textsc{ls}}
is the projection of
Y
onto the column span of
\mathbb{X}
.

It is not hard to see that least squares estimators of
\theta^{*}
and
\mu^{*}=\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}
are maximum likelihood estimators when
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The least squares estimator
\hat{\mu}^{\textsc{ls}}=\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
satisfies

\mathbb{X}^{\top}\hat{\mu}^{\textsc{ls}}=\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y\,.

Moreover,
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
can be chosen to be

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}=(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})^{\dagger}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y\,,

where
(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})^{\dagger}
denotes the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse of
\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}
.

Proof.

The function
\theta\mapsto|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}
is convex so any of its minima satisfies

\nabla_{\theta}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}=0,

where
\nabla_{\theta}
is the gradient operator. Using matrix calculus, we find

\nabla_{\theta}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}=\nabla_{\theta}\big\{|Y|_{2}^{2}-2Y^{\top}\mathbb{X}\theta+\theta^{\top}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}\theta\big\}=-2(Y^{\top}\mathbb{X}-\theta^{\top}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})^{\top}\,.

Therefore, solving
\nabla_{\theta}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}=0
yields

\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}\theta=\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y\,.

It concludes the proof of the first statement. The second statement follows from the definition of the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse.
∎

We are now going to prove our first result on the finite sample performance of the least squares estimator for fixed design.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds where
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Then the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
satisfies

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}})\big]=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{r}{n}\,,

where
r=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})
. Moreover, for any
\delta>0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it holds

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}})\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{r+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,.

Proof.

Note that by definition

|Y-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}|_{2}^{2}\leq|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}=|\varepsilon|^{2}_{2}\,.

(2.4)

Moreover,

|Y-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}|_{2}^{2}=|\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}+\varepsilon-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}|_{2}^{2}=|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}-2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})+|\varepsilon|_{2}^{2}\,.

Therefore, we get

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})=2|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})}{|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})|_{2}}

(2.5)

Note that it is difficult to control

\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})}{|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})|_{2}}

as
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
depends on
\varepsilon
and the dependence structure of this term may be complicated. To remove this dependency, a traditional technique is to “sup-out”
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
. This is typically where maximal inequalities are needed. Here we have to be a bit careful.

Let
\Phi=[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{r}]\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times r}
be an orthonormal basis of the column span of
\mathbb{X}
. In particular, there exists
\nu\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{r}
such that
\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})=\Phi\nu
. It yields

\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})}{|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*})|_{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi\nu}{|\Phi\nu|_{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi\nu}{|\nu|_{2}}=\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}\frac{\nu}{|\nu|_{2}}\leq\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}}\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u\,,

where
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is the unit ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{r}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}=\Phi^{\top}\varepsilon
. Thus

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}\,,

Next, let
u\in\mathcal{S}^{r-1}
and note that
|\Phi u|_{2}^{2}=u^{\top}\Phi^{\top}\Phi u=u^{\top}u=1
, so that for any
s\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
, we have

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{s\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u}]=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[e^{s\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi u}]\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{s^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2}}\,.

Therefore,
\tilde{\varepsilon}\sim\mathsf{subG}_{r}(\sigma^{2})
.

To conclude the bound in expectation, observe that Lemma
1.4
yields

4\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}\big]=4\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}]\leq 16\sigma^{2}r\,.

Moreover, with probability
1-\delta
, it follows from the last step in the proof
6
6

6

we could use Theorem
1.19
directly here but at the cost of a factor 2 in the constant.
of Theorem
1.19
that

\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}\leq 8\log(6)\sigma^{2}r+8\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)\,.

∎

Remark 2.3
.

If
d\leq n
and
B:=\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}
has rank
d
, then we have

|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}})}{\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(B)}\,,

and we can use Theorem
2.2
to bound
|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}
directly.
By contrast, in the high-dimensional case, we will need more structural assumptions to come to a similar conclusion.

8.2
Constrained least squares estimator

Let
K\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be a symmetric convex set. If we know
a priori
that
\theta^{*}\in K
, we may prefer a
constrained least squares
estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}
defined by

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in K}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}\,.

The fundamental inequality (
2.4
) would still hold and the bounds on the MSE may be smaller. Indeed, (
2.5
) can be replaced by

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})\leq 2\sup_{\theta\in K-K}(\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}\theta)\,,

where
K-K=\{x-y\,:\,x,y\in K\}
. It is easy to see that if
K
is symmetric (
x\in K\Leftrightarrow-x\in K
) and convex, then
K-K=2K
so that

2\sup_{\theta\in K-K}(\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}\theta)=4\sup_{v\in\mathbb{X}K}(\varepsilon^{\top}v)

where
\mathbb{X}K=\{\mathbb{X}\theta\,:\,\theta\in K\}\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
. This is a measure of the size (width) of
\mathbb{X}K
. If
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
, the expected value of the above supremum is actually called
Gaussian width
of
\mathbb{X}K
. While
\varepsilon
is not Gaussian but sub-Gaussian here, similar properties will still hold.

8.2.1
\ell_{1}
constrained least squares

Assume here that
K=\mathcal{B}_{1}
is the unit
\ell_{1}
ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
. Recall that it is defined by

\mathcal{B}_{1}=\Big\{x\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\,:\,\sum_{i=1}^{d}|x_{i}|\leq 1\big\}\,,

and it has exactly
2d
vertices
\mathcal{V}=\{e_{1},-e_{1},\ldots,e_{d},-e_{d}\}
, where
e_{j}
is the
j
-th vector of the canonical basis of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
and is defined by

e_{j}=(0,\ldots,0,\underbrace{1}_{j\text{th position}},0,\ldots,0)^{\top}\,.

It implies that the set
\mathbb{X}K=\{\mathbb{X}\theta,\theta\in K\}\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
is also a polytope with at most
2d
vertices that are in the set
\mathbb{X}\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbb{X}_{1},-\mathbb{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathbb{X}_{d},-\mathbb{X}_{d}\}
where
\mathbb{X}_{j}
is the
j
-th column of
\mathbb{X}
. Indeed,
\mathbb{X}K
is obtained by rescaling and embedding (resp. projecting) the polytope
K
when
d\leq n
(resp.,
d\geq n
). Note that some columns of
\mathbb{X}
might not be vertices of
\mathbb{X}K
so that
\mathbb{X}\mathcal{V}
might be a strict superset of the set of vertices of
\mathbb{X}K
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
K=\mathcal{B}_{1}
be the unit
\ell_{1}
ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d},d\geq 2
and assume that
\theta^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Moreover, assume the conditions of Theorem
2.2
and that the columns of
\mathbb{X}
are normalized in such a way that
\max_{j}|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
. Then the constrained least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
satisfies

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}})\big]=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\,,

Moreover, for any
\delta>0
, with probability
1-\delta
, it holds

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}})\lesssim\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\,.

Proof.

From the considerations preceding the theorem, we get that

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\sup_{v\in\mathbb{X}K}(\varepsilon^{\top}v).

Observe now that since
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
, for any column
\mathbb{X}_{j}
such that
|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
, the random variable
\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}_{j}\sim\mathsf{subG}(n\sigma^{2})
. Therefore, applying Theorem
1.16
, we get the bound on
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K})\big]
and for any
t\geq 0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K})>t\big]\leq\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big[\sup_{v\in\mathbb{X}K}(\varepsilon^{\top}v)>nt/4\big]\leq 2de^{-\frac{nt^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}}

To conclude the proof, we find
t
such that

2de^{-\frac{nt^{2}}{32\sigma^{2}}}\leq\delta\ \Leftrightarrow\ t^{2}\geq 32\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(2d)}{n}+32\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,.

∎

Note that the proof of Theorem
2.2
also applies to
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
(exercise!) so that
\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}
benefits from the best of both rates,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}})\big]\lesssim\min\Big(\sigma^{2}\frac{r}{n},\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\Big)\,.

This is called an
elbow effect
. The elbow takes place around
r\simeq\sqrt{n}
(up to logarithmic terms).

8.2.2
\ell_{0}
constrained least squares

By an abuse of notation, we call the number of non-zero coefficients of a vector
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
its
\ell_{0}
norm
(it is not actually a norm). It is denoted by

|\theta|_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(\theta_{j}\neq 0)\,.

We call a vector
\theta
with “small”
\ell_{0}
norm a
sparse
vector. More precisely, if
|\theta|_{0}\leq k
, we say that
\theta
is a
k
-sparse vector. We also call
support
of
\theta
the set

\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta)=\big\{j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\,:\,\theta_{j}\neq 0\big\}

so that
|\theta|_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{card}}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta))=:|\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta)|
.

Remark 2.5
.

The
\ell_{0}
terminology and notation comes from the fact that

\lim_{q\to 0^{\tiny+}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\theta_{j}|^{q}=|\theta|_{0}

Therefore it is really
\lim_{q\to 0^{\tiny+}}|\theta|_{q}^{q}
but the notation
|\theta|_{0}^{0}
suggests too much that it is always equal to 1.

By extension, denote by
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
the
\ell_{0}
ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, i.e., the set of
k
-sparse vectors, defined by

\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)=\{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\,:\,|\theta|_{0}\leq k\}\,.

In this section, our goal is to control the
\mathsf{MSE}
of
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}
when
K=\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
. Note that computing
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)}
essentially requires computing
\binom{d}{k}
least squares estimators, which is an exponential number in
k
. In practice this will be hard (or even impossible) but it is interesting to understand the statistical properties of this estimator and to use them as a benchmark.

Theorem 2.6
.

Fix a positive integer
k\leq d/2
. Let
K=\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
be set of
k
-sparse vectors of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
and assume that
\theta^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
. Moreover, assume the conditions of Theorem
2.2
. Then, for any
\delta>0
, with probability
1-\delta
, it holds

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\log\binom{d}{2k}+\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(1/\delta)\,.

Proof.

We begin as in the proof of Theorem
2.2
to get (
2.5
):

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})=2|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})}{|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})|_{2}}\,.

We know that both
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}
and
\theta^{*}
are in
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
so that
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*}\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(2k)
. For any
S\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}
, let
\mathbb{X}_{S}
denote the
n\times|S|
submatrix of
\mathbb{X}
that is obtained from the columns of
\mathbb{X}_{j},j\in S
of
\mathbb{X}
. Denote by
r_{S}\leq|S|
the rank of
\mathbb{X}_{S}
and let
\Phi_{S}=[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{r_{S}}]\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times r_{S}}
be an orthonormal basis of the column span of
\mathbb{X}_{S}
. Moreover, for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, define
\theta(S)\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{|S|}
to be the vector with coordinates
\theta_{j},j\in S
. If we denote by
\hat{S}=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})
, we have
|\hat{S}|\leq 2k
and there exists
\nu\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{r_{\hat{S}}}
such that

\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})=\mathbb{X}_{\hat{S}}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}(\hat{S})-\theta^{*}(\hat{S}))=\Phi_{\hat{S}}\nu\,.

Therefore,

\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})}{|\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\theta^{*})|_{2}}=\frac{\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{\hat{S}}\nu}{|\nu|_{2}}\leq\max_{|S|=2k}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}}[\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{S}]u

where
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}
is the unit ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{r_{S}}
.
It yields

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 4\max_{|S|=2k}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}_{S}^{\top}u)^{2}\,,

with
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{S}=\Phi_{S}^{\top}\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{r_{S}}(\sigma^{2})
.

Using a union bound, we get for any
t>0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\max_{|S|=2k}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}>t\big)\leq\sum_{|S|=2k}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}>t\big)

It follows from the proof of Theorem
1.19
that for any
|S|\leq 2k
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S}}}(\tilde{\varepsilon}^{\top}u)^{2}>t\big)\leq 6^{|S|}e^{-\frac{t}{8\sigma^{2}}}\leq 6^{2k}e^{-\frac{t}{8\sigma^{2}}}\,.

Together, the above three displays yield

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}>4t)\leq\binom{d}{2k}6^{2k}e^{-\frac{t}{8\sigma^{2}}}\,.

(2.6)

To ensure that the right-hand side of the above inequality is bounded by
\delta
, we need

t\geq C\sigma^{2}\Big\{\log\binom{d}{2k}+k\log(6)+\log(1/\delta)\Big\}\,.

∎

How large is
\log\binom{d}{2k}
? It turns out that it is not much larger than
k
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For any integers
1\leq k\leq n
, it holds

\binom{n}{k}\leq\Big(\frac{en}{k}\Big)^{k}.

Proof.

Observe first that if
k=1
, since
n\geq 1
, it holds,

\binom{n}{1}=n\leq en=\Big(\frac{en}{1}\Big)^{1}

Next, we proceed by induction and assume that it holds for some
k\leq n-1
that

\binom{n}{k}\leq\Big(\frac{en}{k}\Big)^{k}.

Observe that

\binom{n}{k+1}=\binom{n}{k}\frac{n-k}{k+1}\leq\Big(\frac{en}{k}\Big)^{k}\frac{n-k}{k+1}\leq\frac{e^{k}n^{k+1}}{(k+1)^{k+1}}\Big(1+\frac{1}{k}\Big)^{k}\,,

where we used the induction hypothesis in the first inequality. To conclude, it suffices to observe that

\left(1+\frac{1}{k}\right)^{k}\leq e.\qed

It immediately leads to the following corollary:

Corollary 2.8
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
, for any
\delta>0
, with probability at least
1-\delta
, it holds

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{2k}\Big)+\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log(6)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(1/\delta)\,.

Note that for any fixed
\delta
, there exits a constant
C_{\delta}>0
such that for any
n\geq 2k
, with high probability,

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\leq C_{\delta}\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{2k}\Big)\,.

Comparing this result with Theorem
2.2
with
r=k
, we see that the price to pay for not knowing the support of
\theta^{*}
but only its size, is a logarithmic factor in the dimension
d
.

This result immediately leads to the following bound in expectation.

Corollary 2.9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.6
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\big]\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{k}\Big)\,.

Proof.

It follows from (
2.6
) that for any
H\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}>nu)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq H+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}>n(u+H))\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq H+\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\binom{d}{j}6^{2k}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{n(u+H)}{32\sigma^{2}}}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=H+\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\binom{d}{j}6^{2k}e^{-\frac{nH}{32\sigma^{2}}}\frac{32\sigma^{2}}{n}\,.

Next, take
H
to be such that

\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\binom{d}{j}6^{2k}e^{-\frac{nH}{32\sigma^{2}}}=1\,.

This yields

H\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{k}\Big)\,,

which completes the proof.
∎

9
The Gaussian Sequence Model

The Gaussian Sequence Model is a toy model that has received a lot of attention, mostly in the eighties. The main reason for its popularity is that it carries already most of the insight of nonparametric estimation. While the model looks very simple it allows to carry deep ideas that extend beyond its framework and in particular to the linear regression model that we are interested in. Unfortunately, we will only cover a small part of these ideas and the interested reader should definitely look at the excellent books by A. Tsybakov
[
Tsy09
, Chapter 3]
and I. Johnstone
[
Joh11
]
.

The model is as follows:

Y_{i}=\theta^{*}_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\,,\qquad i=1,\ldots,d\,,

(2.7)

where
\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{d}
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
random variables. Note that often,
d
is taken equal to
\infty
in this sequence model and we will also discuss this case. Its links to nonparametric estimation will become clearer in Chapter
3
. The goal here is to estimate the unknown vector
\theta^{*}
.

9.1
The sub-Gaussian Sequence Model

Note first that the model (
2.7
) is a special case of the linear model with fixed design (
2.1
) with
n=d
and
f(x_{i})=x_{i}^{\top}\theta^{*}
, where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
form the canonical basis
e_{1},\dots,e_{n}
of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
and
\varepsilon
has a Gaussian distribution. Therefore,
n=d
is both the dimension of the parameter
\theta
and the number of observations and it looks like we have chosen to index this problem by
d
rather than
n
somewhat arbitrarily. We can bring
n
back into the picture, by observing that this model encompasses slightly more general choices for the design matrix
\mathbb{X}
as long as it satisfies the following assumption.

The design matrix satisfies

\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}=I_{d}\,,

where
I_{d}
denotes the identity matrix of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
.
Assumption
ORT
allows for cases where
d\leq n
but not
d>n
(high dimensional case) because of obvious rank constraints. In particular, it means that the
d
columns of
\mathbb{X}
are orthogonal in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
and all have norm
\sqrt{n}
.

Under this assumption, it follows from the linear regression model (
2.2
) that

\displaystyle y:=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}\theta^{*}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\theta^{*}+\xi\,,

where
\xi=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\sigma^{2}/n)
(If
\varepsilon
is Gaussian, we even have
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\sigma^{2}/n)
). As a result, under the assumption
ORT
, when
\xi
is Gaussian, the linear regression model (
2.2
) is equivalent to the Gaussian Sequence Model (
2.7
) up to a transformation of the data
Y
and a rescaling of the variance. Moreover, for any estimator
\hat{\theta}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, under
ORT
, it follows from (
2.3
) that

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta})=(\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*})^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}(\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*})=|\hat{\theta}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\,.

Furthermore, for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, the assumption
ORT
yields,

\displaystyle|y-\theta|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=|\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y-\theta|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=|\theta|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{n}\theta^{\top}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y+\frac{1}{n^{2}}Y^{\top}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}-\frac{2}{n}(\mathbb{X}\theta)^{\top}Y+\frac{1}{n}|Y|_{2}^{2}+Q

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+Q\,,

(2.8)

where
Q
is a constant that does not depend on
\theta
and is defined by

Q=\frac{1}{n^{2}}Y^{\top}\mathbb{X}\mathbb{X}^{\top}Y-\frac{1}{n}|Y|_{2}^{2}

This implies in particular that the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
is equal to
y
.

We introduce a sightly more general model called
sub-Gaussian sequence model
:

y=\theta^{*}+\xi\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\,,

(2.9)

where
\xi\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\sigma^{2}/n)
.

In this section, we can actually completely “forget” about our original model (
2.2
). In particular we can define this model independently of Assumption
ORT
and thus for any values of
n
and
d
.

The sub-Gaussian sequence model and the Gaussian sequence model are called
direct
(observation) problems as opposed to
inverse problems
where the goal is to estimate the parameter
\theta^{*}
only from noisy observations of its image through an operator. The linear regression model is one such inverse problem where the matrix
\mathbb{X}
plays the role of a linear operator. However, in these notes, we never try to invert the operator. See
[
Cav11
]
for an interesting survey on the statistical theory of inverse problems.

9.2
Sparsity adaptive thresholding estimators

If we knew a priori that
\theta
was
k
-sparse, we could directly employ Corollary
2.8
to obtain that with probability at least
1-\delta
, we have

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)})\leq C_{\delta}\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{2k}\Big)\,.

As we will see, the assumption
ORT
gives us the luxury to not know
k
and yet
adapt
to its value. Adaptation means that we can construct an estimator that does not require the knowledge of
k
(the smallest such that
|\theta^{*}|_{0}\leq k
) and yet perform as well as
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)}
, up to a multiplicative constant.

Let us begin with some heuristic considerations to gain some intuition. Assume the sub-Gaussian sequence model (
2.9
). If nothing is known about
\theta^{*}
it is natural to estimate it using the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}=y
. In this case,

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}})=|y-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}=|\xi|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{\delta}\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n}\,,

where the last inequality holds with probability at least
1-\delta
. This is actually what we are looking for if
k=Cd
for some positive constant
C\leq 1
. The problem with this approach is that it does not use the fact that
k
may be much smaller than
d
, which happens when
\theta^{*}
has many zero coordinates.

If
\theta^{*}_{j}=0
, then,
y_{j}=\xi_{j}
, which is a sub-Gaussian random variable with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}/n
. In particular, we know from Lemma
1.3
that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

|\xi_{j}|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2/\delta)}{n}}=\tau\,.

(2.10)

The consequences of this inequality are interesting. One the one hand, if we observe
|y_{j}|\gg\tau
, then it must correspond to
\theta_{j}^{*}\neq 0
. On the other hand, if
|y_{j}|\leq\tau
is smaller, then,
\theta_{j}^{*}
cannot be very large. In particular, by the triangle inequality,
|\theta^{*}_{j}|\leq|y_{j}|+|\xi_{j}|\leq 2\tau
. Therefore, we loose at most
2\tau
by choosing
\hat{\theta}_{j}=0
. It leads us to consider the following estimator.

Definition 2.10
.

The
hard thresholding
estimator with threshold
2\tau>0
is denoted by
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}
and has coordinates

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}y_{j}&\text{if}\ |y_{j}|>2\tau\,,\\
0&\text{if}\ |y_{j}|\leq 2\tau\,,\end{array}\right.

for
j=1,\ldots,d
. In short, we can write
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}=y_{j}\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|y_{j}|>2\tau)
.

From our above consideration, we are tempted to choose
\tau
as in (
2.10
). Yet, this threshold is not large enough. Indeed, we need to choose
\tau
such that
|\xi_{j}|\leq\tau

simultaneously
for all
j
. This can be done using a maximal inequality. Namely, Theorem
1.14
ensures that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|\xi_{j}|\leq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2d/\delta)}{n}}.

It yields the following theorem.

Theorem 2.11
.

Consider the linear regression model (
2.2
) under the assumption
ORT
or, equivalenty, the sub-Gaussian sequence model (
2.9
). Then the hard thresholding estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}
with threshold

2\tau=2\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2d/\delta)}{n}}

(2.11)

enjoys the following two properties on the same event
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{A})\geq 1-\delta
:

(i)

If
|\theta^{*}|_{0}=k
,

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}})=|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{k\log(2d/\delta)}{n}\,.

(ii)

if
\min_{j\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})}|\theta^{*}_{j}|>3\tau
, then

\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}})=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})\,.

Proof.

Define the event

\mathcal{A}=\Big\{\max_{j}|\xi_{j}|\leq\tau\big\}\,,

and recall that Theorem
1.14
yields
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{A})\geq 1-\delta
. On the event
\mathcal{A}
, the following holds for any
j=1,\ldots,d
.

First, observe that

|y_{j}|>2\tau\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\theta^{*}_{j}|\geq|y_{j}|-|\xi_{j}|>\tau

(2.12)

and

|y_{j}|\leq 2\tau\quad\Rightarrow\quad|\theta^{*}_{j}|\leq|y_{j}|+|\xi_{j}|\leq 3\tau.

(2.13)

It yields

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}-\theta^{*}_{j}|

\displaystyle=|y_{j}-\theta^{*}_{j}|\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|y_{j}|>2\tau)+|\theta^{*}_{j}|\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|y_{j}|\leq 2\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|y_{j}|>2\tau)+|\theta^{*}_{j}|\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|y_{j}|\leq 2\tau)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|\theta^{*}_{j}|>\tau)+|\theta^{*}_{j}|\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|\theta^{*}_{j}|\leq 3\tau)

by

(
2.12
)
and

(
2.13
)

\displaystyle\leq 4\min(|\theta^{*}_{j}|,\tau)

It yields

\displaystyle|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}-\theta^{*}_{j}|^{2}\leq 16\sum_{j=1}^{d}\min(|\theta^{*}_{j}|^{2},\tau^{2})\leq 16|\theta^{*}|_{0}\tau^{2}\,.

This completes the proof of (i).

To prove (ii), note that if
\theta^{*}_{j}\neq 0
, then
|\theta^{*}_{j}|>3\tau
so that

|y_{j}|=|\theta^{*}_{j}+\xi_{j}|>3\tau-\tau=2\tau\,.

Therefore,
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}\neq 0
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})\subset\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}})
.

Next, if
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}\neq 0
, then
|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}|=|y_{j}|>2\tau
. It yields

|\theta^{*}_{j}|\geq|y_{j}|-\tau>\tau.

Therefore,
|\theta^{*}_{j}|\neq 0
and
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}})\subset\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})
.
∎

Similar results can be obtained for the
soft thresholding
estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}
defined by

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}y_{j}-2\tau&\text{if}\ y_{j}>2\tau\,,\\
y_{j}+2\tau&\text{if}\ y_{j}<-2\tau\,,\\
0&\text{if}\ |y_{j}|\leq 2\tau\,,\end{array}\right.

In short, we can write

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}_{j}=\Big(1-\frac{2\tau}{|y_{j}|}\Big)_{+}y_{j}

Figure 2.6:
Transformation applied to
y_{j}
with
2\tau=1
to obtain the hard (left) and soft (right) thresholding estimators

10
High-dimensional linear regression

10.1
The BIC and Lasso estimators

It can be shown (see Problem
2.6
) that the hard and soft thresholding estimators are solutions of the following penalized empirical risk minimization problems:

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{|y-\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{|y-\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau|\theta|_{1}\Big\}

In view of (
2.8
), under the assumption
ORT
, the above variational definitions can be written as

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau|\theta|_{1}\Big\}

When the assumption
ORT
is not satisfied, they no longer correspond to thresholding estimators but can still be defined as above. We change the constant in the threshold parameters for future convenience.

Definition 2.12
.

Fix
\tau>0
and assume the linear regression model (
2.2
). The BIC
7
7

7

Note that it minimizes the Bayes Information Criterion (BIC) employed in the traditional literature of asymptotic statistics if
\tau=\sqrt{\log(d)/n}
. We will use the same value below, up to multiplicative constants (it’s the price to pay to get non-asymptotic results).
estimator of
\theta^{*}
is defined by any
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}
such that

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}.\\

Moreover, the Lasso estimator of
\theta^{*}
is defined by any
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
such that

\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\theta|_{1}\Big\}.\\

Remark 2.13

(Numerical considerations)
.

Computing the BIC estimator can be proved to be NP-hard in the worst case. In particular, no computational method is known to be significantly faster than the brute force search among all
2^{d}
sparsity patterns. Indeed, we can rewrite:

\min_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}=\min_{0\leq k\leq d}\Big\{\min_{\theta\,:\,|\theta|_{0}=k}\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}k\Big\}

To compute
\min_{\theta\,:\,|\theta|_{0}=k}\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}
, we need to compute
\binom{d}{k}
least squares estimators on a space of size
k
. Each costs
O(k^{3})
(matrix inversion). Therefore the total cost of the brute force search is

C\sum_{k=0}^{d}\binom{d}{k}k^{3}=Cd^{3}2^{d}\,.

By contrast, computing the Lasso estimator is a convex problem and there exist many efficient algorithms to solve it. We will not describe this optimization problem in detail but only highlight a few of the best known algorithms:

1.

Probably the most popular method among statisticians relies on coordinate gradient descent. It is implemented in the
glmnet
package in
R

[
FHT10
]
,

2.

An interesting method called LARS
[
EHJT04
]
computes the entire
regularization path
, i.e., the solution of the convex problem for all values of
\tau
. It relies on the fact that, as a function of
\tau
, the solution
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is a piecewise linear function (with values in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
). Yet this method proved to be too slow for very large problems and has been replaced by
glmnet
which computes solutions for values of
\tau
on a grid much faster.

3.

The optimization community has made interesting contributions to this field by using proximal methods to solve this problem. These methods exploit the structure of the objective function which is of the form smooth (sum of squares) + simple (
\ell_{1}
norm). A good entry point to this literature is perhaps the FISTA algorithm
[
BT09
]
.

4.

Recently there has been a lot of interest around this objective for very large
d
and very large
n
. In this case, even computing
|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}
may be computationally expensive and solutions based on stochastic gradient descent are flourishing.

Note that by Lagrange duality, computing
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
is equivalent to solving an
\ell_{1}

constrained
least squares. Nevertheless, the radius of the
\ell_{1}
constraint is unknown. In general, it is hard to relate Lagrange multipliers to the size constraints. The name “Lasso” was originally given to the constrained version this estimator in the original paper of Robert Tibshirani
[
Tib96
]
.

10.2
Analysis of the BIC estimator

While computationally hard to implement, the BIC estimator gives us a good benchmark for sparse estimation. Its performance is similar to that of
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}
but without requiring the assumption
ORT
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds, where
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
and that
|\theta^{*}|_{0}\geq 1
. Then, the BIC estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}
with regularization parameter

\tau^{2}=16\log(6)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+32\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(ed)}{n}\,.

(2.14)

satisfies

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}})=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim|\theta^{*}|_{0}\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(ed/\delta)}{n}

(2.15)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

We begin as usual by noting that

\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}\leq\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}\,.

It implies

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq n\tau^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}+2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta^{*})-n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}\,.

First, note that

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta^{*})

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon^{\top}\Big(\frac{\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}}{|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}}\Big)|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Big(\frac{\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}}{|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}}\Big)\Big]^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\,,

where we use the inequality
2ab\leq 2a^{2}+\frac{1}{2}b^{2}
. Together with the previous display, it yields

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2n\tau^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}+4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\mathcal{U}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta^{*})\big]^{2}-2n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}\,

(2.16)

where

\mathcal{U}(z)=\frac{z}{|z|_{2}}

Next, we need to “sup out”
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}
. To that end, we decompose the sup into a max over cardinalities as follows:

\sup_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}=\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\max_{|S|=k}\sup_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta)=S}\,.

Applied to the above inequality, it yields

\displaystyle 4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta^{*})\big]^{2}-2n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\big\{\max_{|S|=k}\sup_{\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta)=S}4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\mathcal{U}(\theta-\theta^{*})\big]^{2}-2n\tau^{2}k\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\big\{\max_{|S|=k}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S,*}}}4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{S,*}u\big]^{2}-2n\tau^{2}k\big\}\,,

where
\Phi_{S,*}=[\phi_{1},\ldots,\phi_{r_{S,*}}]
is an orthonormal basis of the set
\{\mathbb{X}_{j},j\in S\cup\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})\}
of columns of
\mathbb{X}
and
r_{S,*}\leq|S|+|\theta^{*}|_{0}
is the dimension of this column span.

Using union bounds, we get for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle\Big(\max_{1\leq k\leq d}\big\{\max_{|S|=k}\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S,*}}}4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{S,*}u\big]^{2}-2n\tau^{2}k\big\}\geq t\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{|S|=k}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big(\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S,*}}}\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{S,*}u\big]^{2}\geq\frac{t}{4}+\frac{1}{2}n\tau^{2}k\Big)

Moreover, using the
\varepsilon
-net argument from Theorem
1.19
, we get for
|S|=k
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big(\sup_{u\in\mathcal{B}_{2}^{r_{S,*}}}

\displaystyle\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Phi_{S,*}u\big]^{2}\geq\frac{t}{4}+\frac{1}{2}n\tau^{2}k\Big)\leq 2\cdot 6^{r_{S,*}}\exp\Big(-\frac{\frac{t}{4}+\frac{1}{2}n\tau^{2}k}{8\sigma^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}-\frac{n\tau^{2}k}{16\sigma^{2}}+(k+|\theta^{*}|_{0})\log(6)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}-2k\log(ed)+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)

where, in the last inequality, we used the definition (
2.14
) of
\tau
.

Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}

\displaystyle\Big(|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\geq 2n\tau^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}+t\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{|S|=k}\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}-2k\log(ed)+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\binom{d}{k}\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}-2k\log(ed)+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{d}\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}-k\log(ed)+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)

by Lemma
2.7

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{d}(ed)^{-k}\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(-\frac{t}{32\sigma^{2}}+|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)\Big)\,.

To conclude the proof, choose
t=32\sigma^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}\log(12)+32\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)
and observe that combined with (
2.16
), it yields with probability
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\tau^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}+t

\displaystyle=64\sigma^{2}\log(ed)|\theta^{*}|_{0}+64\log(12)\sigma^{2}|\theta^{*}|_{0}+32\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle\leq 224|\theta^{*}|_{0}\sigma^{2}\log(ed)+32\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)\,.

∎

It follows from Theorem
2.14
that
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}

adapts
to the unknown sparsity of
\theta^{*}
, just like
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}
. Moreover, this holds under no assumption on the design matrix
\mathbb{X}
.

10.3
Analysis of the Lasso estimator

10.3.1
Slow rate for the Lasso estimator

The properties of the BIC estimator are quite impressive. It shows that under no assumption on
\mathbb{X}
, one can mimic two oracles: (i) the oracle that knows the support of
\theta^{*}
(and computes least squares on this support), up to a
\log(ed)
term and (ii) the oracle that knows the sparsity
|\theta^{*}|_{0}
of
\theta^{*}
, up to a smaller logarithmic term
\log(ed/|\theta^{*}|_{0})
, which is subsumed by
\log(ed)
. Actually, the
\log(ed)
can even be improved to
\log(ed/|\theta^{\ast}|_{0})
by using a modified BIC estimator (see Problem
2.7
).

The Lasso estimator is a bit more difficult to analyze because, by construction, it should more naturally adapt to the unknown
\ell_{1}
-norm of
\theta^{*}
. This can be easily shown as in the next theorem, analogous to Theorem
2.4
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds where
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Moreover, assume that the columns of
\mathbb{X}
are normalized in such a way that
\max_{j}|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
. Then, the Lasso estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with regularization parameter

2\tau=2\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2d)}{n}}+2\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

(2.17)

satisfies

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}})=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 4|\theta^{*}|_{1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2d)}{n}}+4|\theta^{*}|_{1}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
, it holds

\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}\,.

Using Hölder’s inequality, it implies

\displaystyle|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*})+2n\tau\big(|\theta^{*}|_{1}-|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq 2|\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon|_{\infty}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}+2|\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon|_{\infty}|\theta^{*}|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}

\displaystyle=2(|\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon|_{\infty}-n\tau)|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}+2(|\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon|_{\infty}+n\tau)|\theta^{*}|_{1}

Observe now that for any
t>0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(|\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon|_{\infty}\geq t)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\max_{1\leq j\leq d}|\mathbb{X}_{j}^{\top}\varepsilon|>t)\leq 2de^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2n\sigma^{2}}}

Therefore, taking
t=\sigma\sqrt{2n\log(2d)}+\sigma\sqrt{2n\log(1/\delta)}=n\tau
, we get that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 4n\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}\,.\qed

Notice that the regularization parameter (
2.17
) depends on the confidence level
\delta
. This is not the case for the BIC estimator (see (
2.14
)).

The rate in Theorem
2.15
is of order
\sqrt{(\log d)/n}
(
slow rate
), which is much slower than the rate of order
(\log d)/n
(
fast rate
) for the BIC estimator. Hereafter, we show that fast rates can also be achieved by the computationally efficient Lasso estimator, but at the cost of a much stronger condition on the design matrix
\mathbb{X}
.

10.3.2
Incoherence

We say that the design matrix
\mathbb{X}
has incoherence
k
for some integer
k>0
if

\big|\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}-I_{d}\big|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{32k}

where the
|A|_{\infty}
denotes the largest element of
A
in absolute value.
Equivalently,

1.

For all
j=1,\ldots,d
,

\left|\frac{|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}^{2}}{n}-1\right|\leq\frac{1}{32k}\,.

2.

For all
1\leq i,j\leq d
,
i\neq j
, we have

\frac{|\mathbb{X}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{X}_{j}|}{n}\leq\frac{1}{32k}\,.

Note that Assumption
ORT
arises as the limiting case of
\mathsf{INC}(k)
as
k\to\infty
. However, while Assumption
ORT
requires
d\leq n
, here we may have
d\gg n
as illustrated in Proposition
2.16
below. To that end, we simply have to show that there exists a matrix that satisfies
\mathsf{INC}(k)
even for
d>n
. We resort to the
probabilistic method

[
AS08
]
. The idea of this method is that if we can find a probability measure that assigns positive probability to objects that satisfy a certain property, then there must exist objects that satisfy said property.

In our case, we consider the following probability distribution on random matrices with entries in
\{\pm 1\}
. Let the design matrix
\mathbb{X}
have entries that are i.i.d. Rademacher
(\pm 1)
random variables. We are going to show that most realizations of this random matrix satisfy Assumption
\mathsf{INC}(k)
for large enough
n
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
\mathbb{X}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times d}
be a random matrix with entries
X_{ij},i=1,\ldots,n,j=1,\ldots,d
, that are i.i.d. Rademacher
(\pm 1)
random variables. Then,
\mathbb{X}
has incoherence
k
with probability
1-\delta
as soon as

n\geq 2^{11}k^{2}\log(1/\delta)+2^{13}k^{2}\log(d)\,.

It implies that there exist matrices that satisfy Assumption
\mathsf{INC}(k)
for

n\geq Ck^{2}\log(d)\,,

for some numerical constant
C
.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{ij}\in\{-1,1\}
denote the Rademacher random variable that is on the
i
th row and
j
th column of
\mathbb{X}
.

Note first that the
j
th diagonal entries of
\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}/n
are given by

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i,j}^{2}=1.

Moreover, for
j\neq k
, the
(j,k)
th entry of the
d\times d
matrix
\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}/n
is given by

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varepsilon_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,k}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{(j,k)}\,,

where for each pair
(j,k)
,
\xi_{i}^{(j,k)}=\varepsilon_{i,j}\varepsilon_{i,k}
, so that
\xi_{1}^{(j,k)},\ldots,\xi_{n}^{(j,k)}
are i.i.d. Rademacher random variables.

Therefore, we get that for any
t>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(\left|\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}-I_{d}\right|_{\infty}>t\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big(\max_{j\neq k}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{(j,k)}\Big|>t\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\neq k}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big(\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}^{(j,k)}\Big|>t\Big)

(Union bound)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\neq k}2e^{-\frac{nt^{2}}{2}}

(Hoeffding: Theorem
1.9
)

\displaystyle\leq 2d^{2}e^{-\frac{nt^{2}}{2}}.

Taking now
t=1/(32k)
yields

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\big|\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}-I_{d}\big|_{\infty}>\frac{1}{32k}\big)\leq 2d^{2}e^{-\frac{n}{2^{11}k^{2}}}\leq\delta

for

n\geq 2^{11}k^{2}\log(1/\delta)+2^{13}k^{2}\log(d)\,.\qed

For any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
,
S\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}
, define
\theta_{S}
to be the vector with coordinates

\theta_{S,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta_{j}&\text{if}\ j\in S\,,\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{array}\right.

In particular
|\theta|_{1}=|\theta_{S}|_{1}+|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}
.

The following lemma holds

Lemma 2.17
.

Fix a positive integer
k\leq d
and assume that
\mathbb{X}
satisfies assumption
\mathsf{INC}(k)
. Then, for any
S\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that
|S|\leq k
and any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
that satisfies the
cone condition

|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}\leq 3|\theta_{S}|_{1}\,,

(2.18)

it holds

|\theta|^{2}_{2}\leq 2\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}}{n}.

Proof.

We have

\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}}{n}=\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}}{n}+\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}}{n}+2\theta_{S}^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}\theta_{S^{c}}.

We bound each of the three terms separately.

(i)

First, if follows from the incoherence condition that

\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}}{n}=\theta_{S}^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}\theta_{S}=|\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}+\theta_{S}^{\top}\left(\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}-I_{d}\right)\theta_{S}\geq|\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{|\theta_{S}|_{1}^{2}}{32k}\,,

(ii)

Similarly,

\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}}{n}\geq|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}^{2}}{32k}\geq|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{9|\theta_{S}|_{1}^{2}}{32k}\,,

where, in the last inequality, we used the cone condition (
2.18
).

(iii)

Finally,

2\Big|\theta_{S}^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}\theta_{S^{c}}\Big|\leq\frac{2}{32k}|\theta_{S}|_{1}|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}\leq\frac{6}{32k}|\theta_{S}|_{1}^{2}.

where, in the last inequality, we used the cone condition (
2.18
).

Observe now that it follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

|\theta_{S}|_{1}^{2}\leq|S||\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}.

Thus for
|S|\leq k
,

\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}}{n}\geq|\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}+|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{16|S|}{32k}|\theta_{S}|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\theta|_{2}^{2}.\qed

10.3.3
Fast rate for the Lasso

Theorem 2.18
.

Fix
n\geq 2
. Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds where
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Moreover, assume that
|\theta^{*}|_{0}\leq k
and that
\mathbb{X}
satisfies assumption
INC
(k)
. Then the Lasso estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with regularization parameter defined by

2\tau=8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(2d)}{n}}+8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

satisfies

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}})=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim k\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(2d/\delta)}{n}

(2.19)

and

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim k\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(2d/\delta)}{n}\,.

(2.20)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
, it holds

\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}-2\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\,.

Adding
\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}
on each side and multiplying by
n
, we get

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*})+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\,.

Applying Hölder’s inequality and using the same steps as in the proof of Theorem
2.15
, we get that with probability
1-\delta
, we get

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*})

\displaystyle\leq|\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}|_{\infty}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n\tau}{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}\,,

where we used the fact that
|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}^{2}\leq n+1/(32k)\leq 2n
.
Therefore, taking
S=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta^{*})
to be the support of
\theta^{*}
, we get

\displaystyle|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}

\displaystyle=2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta^{*}|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta^{*}|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 4n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta^{*}|_{1}.

(2.21)

In particular, it implies that

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S^{c}}-\theta_{S^{c}}^{*}|_{1}\leq 3|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta_{S}^{*}|_{1}\,.

(2.22)

so that
\theta=\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}
satisfies the cone condition (
2.18
).
Using now the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
2.17
respectively, we get, since
|S|\leq k
,

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta^{*}|_{1}\leq\sqrt{|S|}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta^{*}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{|S|}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2k}{n}}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}\,.

Combining this result with (
2.21
), we find

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 32nk\tau^{2}\,.

This concludes the proof of the bound on the MSE.
To prove (
2.20
), we use Lemma
2.17
once again to get

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}})\leq 64k\tau^{2}.\qed

Note that all we required for the proof was not really incoherence but the conclusion of Lemma
2.17
:

\inf_{|S|\leq k}\inf_{\theta\in\mathcal{C}_{S}}\frac{|\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}}{n|\theta|^{2}_{2}}\geq\kappa,

(2.23)

where
\kappa=1/2
and
\mathcal{C}_{S}
is the cone defined by

\mathcal{C}_{S}=\big\{|\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}\leq 3|\theta_{S}|_{1}\big\}\,.

Condition (
2.23
) is sometimes called
restricted eigenvalue (RE) condition
. Its name comes from the following observation. Note that all
k
-sparse vectors
\theta
are in a cone
\mathcal{C}_{S}
with
|S|\leq k
so that the RE condition implies that the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbb{X}_{S}
satisfies

\lambda_{\textrm{min}}(\mathbb{X}_{S})\geq n\kappa
for all
S
such that
|S|\leq k
. Clearly, the RE condition is weaker than incoherence and it can actually be shown that a design matrix
\mathbb{X}
of i.i.d. Rademacher random variables satisfies the RE conditions as soon as
n\geq Ck\log(d)
with positive probability.

10.4
The Slope estimator

We noticed that the BIC estimator can actually obtain a rate of
k\log(ed/k)
, where
k=|\theta^{\ast}|_{0}
, while the LASSO only achieves
k\log(ed)
.
This begs the question whether the same rate can be achieved by an efficiently computable estimator.
The answer is yes, and the estimator achieving this is a slight modification of the LASSO, where the entries in the
\ell_{1}
-norm are weighted differently according to their size in order to get rid of a slight inefficiency in our bounds.

Definition 2.19

(Slope estimator)
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})
be a non-increasing sequence of positive real numbers,
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{d}>0
.
For
\theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{d})\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, let
(\theta^{\ast}_{1},\dots,\theta^{\ast}_{d})
be a non-increasing rearrangement of the modulus of the entries,
|\theta_{1}|,\dots,|\theta_{d}|
.

We define the
sorted
\ell_{1}
norm of
\theta
as

|\theta|_{\ast}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\theta^{\ast}_{j},

(2.24)

or equivalently as

|\theta|_{\ast}=\max_{\phi\in S_{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}|\theta_{\phi(j)}|.

(2.25)

The
Slope estimator
is then given by

\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\theta|_{\ast}\}

(2.26)

for a choice of tuning parameters
\lambda
and
\tau>0
.

Slope stands for Sorted L-One Penalized Estimation and was introduced in
[
BvS+15
]
, motivated by the quest for a penalized estimation procedure that could offer a control of false discovery rate for the hypotheses
H_{0,j}:\theta^{\ast}_{j}=0
.
We can check that
|\,.\,|_{\ast}
is indeed a norm and that
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}
can be computed efficiently, for example by proximal gradient algorithms, see
[
BvS+15
]
.

In the following, we will consider

\lambda_{j}=\sqrt{\log(2d/j)},\quad j=1,\dots,d.

(2.27)

With this choice, we will exhibit a scaling in
\tau
that leads to the desired high probability bounds, following the proofs in
[
BLT16
]
.

We begin with refined bounds on the suprema of Gaussians.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
g_{1},\dots,g_{d}
be zero-mean Gaussian variables with variance at most
\sigma^{2}
. Then, for any
k\leq d
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(\frac{1}{k\sigma^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}(g_{j}^{\ast})^{2}>t\log\left(\frac{2d}{k}\right)\right)\leq\left(\frac{2d}{k}\right)^{1-3t/8}.

(2.28)

Proof.

We consider the moment generating function to apply a Chernoff bound.

Use Jensen’s inequality on the sum to get

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\exp\left(\frac{3}{8k\sigma^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{k}(g^{\ast}_{j})^{2}\right)\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\exp\left(\frac{3(g_{j}^{\ast})^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\exp\left(\frac{3g_{j}^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{2d}{k},

where we used the bound on the moment generating function of the
\chi^{2}
distribution from Problem
1.2
to get that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\exp(3g^{2}/8)]=2
for
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

The rest follows from a Chernoff bound.
∎

Lemma 2.21
.

Define
[d]:=\{1,\ldots,d\}
. Under the same assumptions as in Lemma
2.20
,

\sup_{k\in[d]}\frac{(g^{\ast}_{k})}{\sigma\lambda_{k}}\leq 4\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)},

(2.29)

with probability at least
1-\delta
, for
\delta<\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

We can estimate
(g^{\ast}_{k})^{2}
by the average of all larger variables,

(g^{\ast}_{k})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}(g^{\ast}_{j})^{2}.

(2.30)

Applying Lemma
2.20
yields

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(\frac{(g^{\ast}_{k})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\lambda_{k}^{2}}>t\right)\leq\left(\frac{2d}{k}\right)^{1-3t/8}

(2.31)

For
t>8
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\left(\sup_{k\in[d]}\frac{(g^{\ast}_{k})^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\lambda_{k}^{2}}>t\right)\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\frac{2d}{j}\right)^{1-3t/8}

(2.32)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle(2d)^{1-3t/8}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{j^{2}}

(2.33)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 4\cdot 2^{-3t/8}.

(2.34)

In turn, this means that

\sup_{k\in[d]}\frac{(g^{\ast}_{k})}{\sigma\lambda_{k}}\leq 4\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)},

(2.35)

for
\delta\leq 1/2
.
∎

Theorem 2.22
.

Fix
n\geq 2
. Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n})
. Moreover, assume that
|\theta^{*}|_{0}\leq k
and that
\mathbb{X}
satisfies assumption
INC
(k^{\prime})
with
k^{\prime}\geq 4k\log(2de/k)
. Then the Slope estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}
with regularization parameter defined by

\tau=8\sqrt{2}\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

(2.36)

satisfies

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}})=\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{k\log(2d/k)\log(1/\delta)}{n}

(2.37)

and

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{k\log(2d/k)\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,.

(2.38)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Remark 2.23
.

The multplicative depedence on
\log(1/\delta)
in (
2.37
) is an artifact of the simplified proof we give here and can be improved to an additive one similar to the lasso case, (
2.19
), by appealing to stronger and more general concentration inequalities.
For more details, see
[
BLT16
]
.

Proof.

By minimality of
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}
in (
2.26
), adding
n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{\ast}|_{\ast}
on both sides,

|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{*}|_{\ast}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{*})+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{*}|_{\ast}+2\tau n|\theta^{*}|_{\ast}-2\tau n|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}|_{\ast}\,.

(2.39)

Set

u:=\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\theta^{\ast},\quad g_{j}=(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon)_{j},

(2.40)

By Lemma
2.21
, we can estimate

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{\top}\mathbb{X}u={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\varepsilon)_{j}u_{j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}g^{\ast}_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.41)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{g^{\ast}_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}(\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j})

(2.42)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sup_{j}\left\{\frac{g^{\ast}_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}\right\}|u|_{\ast}

(2.43)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{2}\sigma\sqrt{n\log(1/\delta)}|u|_{\ast}=\frac{n\tau}{2}|u|_{\ast},

(2.44)

where we used that
|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2n
.

Pick a permutation
\phi
such that
|\theta^{\ast}|_{\ast}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}|\theta_{\phi(j)}|
and
|u_{\phi(k+1)}|\geq\dots\geq|u_{\phi(d)}|
.
Then, noting that
\lambda_{j}
is monotonically decreasing,

\displaystyle|\theta^{\ast}|_{\ast}-|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}|_{\ast}={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}|\theta^{\ast}_{\phi(j)}|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}(\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}})^{\ast}_{j}

(2.45)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}(|\theta^{\ast}_{\phi(j)}|-|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\phi(j)}|)-\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}_{\phi(j)}|

(2.46)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}|u_{\phi(j)}|-\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.47)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}-\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}.

(2.48)

Combined with
\tau=8\sqrt{2}\sigma\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)/n}
and the basic inequality (
2.39
), we have that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}-\mathbb{X}\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|u|_{\ast}\leq{}

\displaystyle 2n\tau|u|_{\ast}+2\tau n|\theta^{*}|_{\ast}-2\tau n|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{S}}|_{\ast}

(2.49)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 4n\tau\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j},

(2.50)

whence

\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}\leq 3\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}.

(2.51)

We can now repeat the incoherence arguments from Lemma
2.17
, with
S
being the
k
largest entries of
|u|
, to get the same conclusion under the restriction
\textsf{INC}(k^{\prime})
.
First, by exactly the same argument as in Lemma
2.17
, we have

\frac{|\mathbb{X}u_{S}|_{2}^{2}}{n}\geq|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{|u_{S}|_{1}^{2}}{32k^{\prime}}\geq|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{k}{32k^{\prime}}|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}.

(2.52)

Next, for the cross terms, we have

\displaystyle 2\Big|u_{S}^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}u_{S^{c}}\Big|\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{32k^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}u^{\ast}_{i}\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.53)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{32k^{\prime}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}u^{\ast}_{i}\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.54)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{32k^{\prime}\lambda_{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}u^{\ast}_{i}\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.55)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{6}{32k^{\prime}\lambda_{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}u^{\ast}_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}u^{\ast}_{j}

(2.56)

\displaystyle\leq{}
