\displaystyle\frac{6\sqrt{k}}{32k^{\prime}\lambda_{d}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}(u^{\ast}_{i})^{2}

(2.57)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{6k}{32k^{\prime}\lambda_{d}}\sqrt{\log(2de/k)}|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}.

(2.58)

where we estimated the sum by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}\log\left(\frac{2d}{j}\right)={}

\displaystyle k\log(2d)-\log(k!)\leq k\log(2d)-k\log(k/e)

(2.59)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle k\log(2de/k),

(2.60)

using Stirling’s inequality,
k!\geq(k/e)^{k}
.

Finally, from a similar calculation, using the cone condition twice,

\frac{|\mathbb{X}u_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}}{n}\geq|u_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{9k}{32k^{\prime}\lambda_{d}^{2}}\log(2de/k)|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}.

(2.61)

Concluding, if
\textsf{INC}(k^{\prime})
holds with
k^{\prime}\geq 4k\log(2de/k)
, taking into account that
\lambda_{d}\geq 1/2
, we have

\frac{|\mathbb{X}u|_{2}^{2}}{n}\geq|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}+|u_{S^{c}}|_{2}^{2}-\frac{36+12+1}{128}|u_{S}|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|u|_{2}^{2}.

(2.62)

Hence, from (
2.50
),

\displaystyle|\mathbb{X}u|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|u|_{\ast}\leq{}

\displaystyle 4n\tau\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}(u^{\ast}_{j})^{2}\right)^{1/2}

(2.63)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 4\sqrt{2}\tau\sqrt{nk\log(2de/k)}|\mathbb{X}u|_{2}

(2.64)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle 2^{6}\sigma\sqrt{\log(1/\delta)}\sqrt{k\log(2de/k)}|\mathbb{X}u|_{2},

(2.65)

which combined yields

\frac{1}{n}|\mathbb{X}u|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{12}\sigma^{2}\frac{k}{n}\log(2de/k)\log(1/\delta),

(2.66)

and

|u|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{13}\frac{k}{n}\sigma^{2}\log(2de/k)\log(1/\delta).\qed

(2.67)

11
Problem set

Problem 2.1
.

Consider the linear regression model with fixed design with
d\leq n
. The
ridge
regression estimator is employed when
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})<d
but we are interested in estimating
\theta^{*}
. It is defined for a given parameter
\tau>0
by

\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{ridge}}_{\tau}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+\tau|\theta|_{2}^{2}\Big\}\,.

(a)

Show that for any
\tau
,
\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{ridge}}_{\tau}
is uniquely defined and give its closed form expression.

(b)

Compute the bias of
\hat{\theta}^{\mathrm{ridge}}_{\tau}
and show that it is bounded in absolute value by
|\theta^{*}|_{2}
.

Problem 2.2
.

Let
X=(1,Z,\ldots,Z^{d-1})^{\top}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be a random vector
where
Z
is a random variable. Show that the matrix
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}(XX^{\top})
is
positive definite if
Z
admits a probability density with
respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
.

Problem 2.3
.

Let
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}
be the hard thresholding estimator defined in Definition
2.10
.

1.

Show that

|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}|_{0}\leq|\theta^{*}|_{0}+\frac{|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}}{4\tau^{2}}

2.

Conclude that if
\tau
is chosen as in Theorem
2.11
, then

|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}|_{0}\leq C|\theta^{*}|_{0}

with probability
1-\delta
for some constant
C
to be made explicit.

Problem 2.4
.

In the proof of Theorem
2.11
, show that
4\min(|\theta^{*}_{j}|,\tau)
can be replaced by
3\min(|\theta^{*}_{j}|,\tau)
, i.e., that on the event
\mathcal{A}
, it holds

|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}_{j}-\theta^{*}_{j}|\leq 3\min(|\theta^{*}_{j}|,\tau)\,.

Problem 2.5
.

For any
q>0
, a vector
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
is said to be in a weak
\ell_{q}
ball of radius
R
if the decreasing rearrangement
|\theta_{[1]}|\geq|\theta_{[2]}|\geq\dots
satisfies

|\theta_{[j]}|\leq Rj^{-1/q}\,.

Moreover, we define the weak
\ell_{q}
norm of
\theta
by

|\theta|_{w\ell_{q}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq d}j^{1/q}|\theta_{[j]}|.

(a)

Give examples of
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
such that

|\theta+\theta^{\prime}|_{w\ell_{1}}>|\theta|_{w\ell_{1}}+|\theta^{\prime}|_{w\ell_{1}}

What do you conclude?

(b)

Show that
|\theta|_{w\ell_{q}}\leq|\theta|_{q}
.

(c)

Given a sequence
\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\dots
, show that if
\lim_{d\to\infty}|\theta_{\{1,\dots,d\}}|_{w\ell_{q}}<\infty
, then
\lim_{d\to\infty}|\theta_{\{1,\dots,d\}}|_{q^{\prime}}<\infty
for all
q^{\prime}>q
.

(d)

Show that, for any
q\in(0,2)
if
\lim_{d\to\infty}|\theta_{\{1,\dots,d\}}|_{w\ell_{q}}=C
, there exists a constant
C_{q}>0
that depends on
q
but not on
d
such that under the assumptions of Theorem
2.11
, it holds

|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq C_{q}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}\log 2d}{n}\Big)^{1-\frac{q}{2}}

with probability .99.

Problem 2.6
.

Show that

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{hrd}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{|y-\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}

\displaystyle\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{sft}}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{|y-\theta|_{2}^{2}+4\tau|\theta|_{1}\Big\}

Problem 2.7
.

Assume the linear model (
2.2
) with
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
and
\theta^{*}\neq 0
. Show that the modified BIC estimator
\hat{\theta}
defined by

\hat{\theta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}|Y-\mathbb{X}\theta|_{2}^{2}+\lambda|\theta|_{0}\log\Big(\frac{ed}{|\theta|_{0}}\Big)\Big\}\\

satisfies

\mathsf{MSE}(\mathbb{X}\hat{\theta})\lesssim|\theta^{*}|_{0}\sigma^{2}\frac{\log\big(\frac{ed}{|\theta^{*}|_{0}}\big)}{n}\,.

with probability .99, for appropriately chosen
\lambda
. What do you conclude?

Problem 2.8
.

Assume that the linear model (
2.2
) holds where
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Moreover, assume the conditions of Theorem
2.2
and that the columns of
X
are normalized in such a way that
\max_{j}|\mathbb{X}_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
. Then the Lasso estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with regularization parameter

2\tau=8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2d)}{n}}\,,

satisfies

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\leq C|\theta^{*}|_{1}

with probability
1-(2d)^{-1}
for some constant
C
to be specified.

Chapter 3
Misspecified Linear Models

Arguably, the strongest assumption that we made in Chapter
2
is that the regression function
f(x)
is of the form
f(x)=x^{\top}\theta^{*}
. What if this assumption is violated? In reality, we do not really believe in the linear model and we hope that good statistical methods should be
robust
to deviations from this model. This is the problem of model misspecification, and in particular misspecified linear models.

Throughout this chapter, we assume the following model:

Y_{i}=f(X_{i})+\varepsilon_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n\,,

(3.1)

where
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})^{\top}
is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy
\sigma^{2}
. Here
X_{i}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
.
When dealing with fixed design, it will be convenient to consider the vector
g\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
defined for any function
g\,:\,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
by
g=(g(X_{1}),\ldots,g(X_{n}))^{\top}
. In this case, we can write for any estimator
\hat{f}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
of
f
,

\mathsf{MSE}(\hat{f})=\frac{1}{n}|\hat{f}-f|_{2}^{2}\,.

Even though the model may not be linear, we are interested in studying the statistical properties of various linear estimators introduced in the previous chapters:
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}},\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K},\tilde{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{X},\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}},\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
. Clearly, even with an infinite number of observations, we have no chance of finding a consistent estimator of
f
if we don’t know the correct model. Nevertheless, as we will see in this chapter, something can still be said about these estimators using
oracle inequalities
.

12
Oracle inequalities

12.1
Oracle inequalities

As mentioned in the introduction, an oracle is a quantity that cannot be constructed without the knowledge of the quantity of interest, here: the regression function. Unlike the regression function itself, an oracle is constrained to take a specific form. For all matter of purposes, an oracle can be viewed as an estimator (in a given family) that can be constructed with an infinite amount of data. This is exactly what we should aim for in misspecified models.

When employing the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
, we constrain ourselves to estimating functions that are of the form
x\mapsto x^{\top}\theta
, even though
f
itself may not be of this form. Therefore, the oracle
\hat{f}
is the linear function that is the closest to
f
.

Rather than trying to approximate
f
by a linear function
f(x)\approx\theta^{\top}x
, we make the model a bit more general and consider a dictionary
\mathcal{H}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{M}\}
of functions where
\varphi_{j}\,:\,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
. In this case, we can actually remove the assumption that
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
. Indeed, the goal is now to estimate
f
using a linear combination of the functions in the dictionary:

f\approx\varphi_{\theta}:=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\theta_{j}\varphi_{j}\,.

Remark 3.1
.

If
M=d
and
\varphi_{j}(X)=X^{(j)}
returns the
j
th coordinate of
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
then the goal is to approximate
f(x)
by
\theta^{\top}x
. Nevertheless, the use of a dictionary allows for a much more general framework.

Note that the use of a dictionary does not affect the methods that we have been using so far, namely penalized/constrained least squares. We use the same notation as before and define

1.

The least squares estimator:

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{i}-\varphi_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)^{2}

(3.2)

2.

The least squares estimator constrained to
K\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
:

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{K}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in K}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{i}-\varphi_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)^{2}

3.

The BIC estimator:

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{i}-\varphi_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)^{2}+\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\Big\}

(3.3)

4.

The Lasso estimator:

\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{i}-\varphi_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)^{2}+2\tau|\theta|_{1}\Big\}

(3.4)

Definition 3.2
.

Let
R(\cdot)
be a risk function and let
\mathcal{H}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{M}\}
be a dictionary of functions from
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
to
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
. Let
K
be a subset of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
. The
oracle
on
K
with respect to
R
is defined by
\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}
, where
\bar{\theta}\in K
is such that

R(\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})\leq R(\varphi_{\theta})\,,\qquad\forall\,\theta\in K\,.

Moreover,
R_{K}=R(\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})
is called
oracle risk
on
K
. An estimator
\hat{f}
is said to satisfy an oracle inequality (over
K
) with remainder term
\phi
in expectation (resp. with high probability) if there exists a constant
C\geq 1
such that

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}R(\hat{f})\leq C\inf_{\theta\in K}R(\varphi_{\theta})+\phi_{n,M}(K)\,,

or

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big\{R(\hat{f})\leq C\inf_{\theta\in K}R(\varphi_{\theta})+\phi_{n,M,\delta}(K)\big\}\geq 1-\delta\,,\qquad\forall\ \delta>0

respectively. If
C=1
, the oracle inequality is sometimes called
exact
.

Our goal will be to mimic oracles. The finite sample performance of an estimator at this task is captured by an oracle inequality.

12.2
Oracle inequality for the least squares estimator

While our ultimate goal is to prove sparse oracle inequalities for the BIC and Lasso estimator in the case of misspecified model, the difficulty of the extension to this case for linear models is essentially already captured by the analysis for the least squares estimator. In this simple case, can even obtain an exact oracle inequality.

Theorem 3.3
.

Assume the general regression model (
3.1
) with
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Then, the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
satisfies for some numerical constant
C>0
,

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\frac{\sigma^{2}M}{n}\log(1/\delta)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Note that by definition

|Y-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}|_{2}^{2}\leq|Y-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}

where
\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}
denotes the orthogonal projection of
f
onto the linear span of
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{M}
. Since
Y=f+\varepsilon
, we get

|f-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}|_{2}^{2}\leq|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}+2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})

Moreover, by Pythagoras’s theorem, we have

|f-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}|_{2}^{2}-|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}=|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}\,.

It yields

|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})\,.

Using the same steps as the ones following equation (
2.5
) for the well specified case, we get

|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}M}{n}\log(1/\delta)

with probability
1-\delta
. The result of the lemma follows.
∎

12.3
Sparse oracle inequality for the BIC estimator

The analysis for more complicated estimators such as the BIC in Chapter
2
allows us to derive oracle inequalities for these estimators.

Theorem 3.4
.

Assume the general regression model (
3.1
) with
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Then, the BIC estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}
with regularization parameter

\tau^{2}=16\log(6)\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}+32\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(ed)}{n}

(3.5)

satisfies for some numerical constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{3\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+

\displaystyle\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM/\delta)\Big\}+\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(1/\delta)

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Recall that the proof of Theorem
2.14
for the BIC estimator begins as follows:

\frac{1}{n}|Y-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}\leq\frac{1}{n}|Y-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}+\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\,.

This is true for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
.
It implies

|f-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}|_{2}^{2}+n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}\leq|f-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}+2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta})+n\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}\,.

Note that if
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}=\theta
, the result is trivial. Otherwise,

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta})

\displaystyle=2\varepsilon^{\top}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}}{|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}}\Big)|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}\Big[\varepsilon^{\top}\Big(\frac{\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}}{|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}}\Big)\Big]^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}\,,

where we used Young’s inequality
2ab\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}a^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}b^{2}
, which is valid for
a,b\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
and
\alpha>0
.
Next, since

\frac{\alpha}{2}|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}\leq\alpha|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-f|_{2}^{2}+\alpha|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}\,,

we get for
\alpha=1/2
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-f|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+n\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}+4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\mathcal{U}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}-\varphi_{\theta})\big]^{2}-n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}|_{0}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+2n\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}+4\big[\varepsilon^{\top}\mathcal{U}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta})\big]^{2}-n\tau^{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}-\theta|_{0}.

We conclude as in the proof of Theorem
2.14
.
∎

The interpretation of this theorem is enlightening. It implies that the BIC estimator will mimic the best tradeoff between the approximation error
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})
and the complexity of
\theta
as measured by its sparsity. In particular, this result, which is sometimes called
sparse oracle inequality
, implies the following oracle inequality. Define the oracle
\bar{\theta}
to be such that

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})=\min_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta}).

Then, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}})\leq 3\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})+\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}\Big[|\bar{\theta}|_{0}\log(eM)+\log(1/\delta)\Big]\Big\}

If the linear model happens to be correct, then we simply have
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\bar{\theta}})=0
.

12.4
Sparse oracle inequality for the Lasso

To prove an oracle inequality for the Lasso, we need additional assumptions on the design matrix, such as incoherence. Here, the design matrix is given by the
n\times M
matrix
\Phi
with elements
\Phi_{i,j}=\varphi_{j}(X_{i})
.

Theorem 3.5
.

Assume the general regression model (
3.1
) with
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
. Moreover, assume that there exists an integer
k
such that the matrix
\Phi
satisfies assumption
INC
(k)
. Then, the Lasso estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with regularization parameter given by

2\tau=8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(2M)}{n}}+8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{n}}

(3.6)

satisfies for some numerical constant
C
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}})\leq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{0}\leq k\end{subarray}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})

\displaystyle+\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{n}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM/\delta)\Big\}

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
, for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}|Y-\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{n}|Y-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}+2\tau|\theta|_{1}-2\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\,.

Expanding the squares, adding
\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}
on each side and multiplying by
n
, we get

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-f|_{2}^{2}-|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-\varphi_{\theta})+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}\,.

(3.7)

Next, note that
INC
(k)
for any
k\geq 1
implies that
|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{n}
for all
j=1,\ldots,M
. Applying Hölder’s inequality, we get that with probability
1-\delta
, it holds that

2\varepsilon^{\top}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-\varphi_{\theta})\leq\frac{n\tau}{2}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}.

Therefore, taking
S=\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(\theta)
to be the support of
\theta
, we get that the right-hand side of (
3.7
) is bounded by

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-f|_{2}^{2}-|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}|_{1}

\displaystyle=2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta|_{1}+2n\tau|\theta|_{1}-2n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 4n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta|_{1}

(3.8)

with probability
1-\delta
.

It implies that either
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}})\leq\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})
or that

|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S^{c}}-\theta_{S^{c}}|_{1}\leq 3|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta_{S}|_{1}\,.

so that
\theta=\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}-\theta
satisfies the cone condition (
2.18
).
Using now the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Lemma
2.17
, respectively, assuming that
|\theta|_{0}\leq k
, we get

4n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta|_{1}\leq 4n\tau\sqrt{|S|}|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta|_{2}\leq 4\tau\sqrt{2n|\theta|_{0}}|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}\,.

Using now the inequality
2ab\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}a^{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2}b^{2}
, we get

\displaystyle 4n\tau|\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}_{S}-\theta|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\tau^{2}n|\theta|_{0}}{\alpha}+\frac{\alpha}{2}|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\tau^{2}n|\theta|_{0}}{\alpha}+\alpha|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}-f|_{2}^{2}+\alpha|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}

Combining this result with (
3.7
) and (
3.8
), we find

(1-\alpha)\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}})\leq(1+\alpha)\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+\frac{16\tau^{2}|\theta|_{0}}{\alpha}\,.

To conclude the proof, it only remains to divide by
1-\alpha
on both sides of the above inequality and take
\alpha=1/2
.
∎

12.5
Maurey’s argument

In there is no sparse
\theta
such that
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})
is small, Theorem
3.4
is useless whereas the Lasso may still enjoy slow rates. In reality, no one really believes in the existence of sparse vectors but rather of approximately sparse vectors. Zipf’s law would instead favor the existence of vectors
\theta
with absolute coefficients that decay polynomially when ordered from largest to smallest in absolute value. This is the case for example if
\theta
has a small
\ell_{1}
norm but is not sparse. For such
\theta
, the Lasso estimator still enjoys slow rates as in Theorem
2.15
, which can be easily extended to the misspecified case (see Problem
3.2
). As a result, it seems that the Lasso estimator is strictly better than the BIC estimator as long as incoherence holds since it enjoys both fast and slow rates, whereas the BIC estimator seems to be tailored to the fast rate. Fortunately, such vectors can be well approximated by sparse vectors in the following sense: for any vector
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
such that
|\theta|_{1}\leq 1
, there exists a vector
\theta^{\prime}
that is sparse and for which
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})
is not much larger than
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})
. The following theorem quantifies exactly the tradeoff between sparsity and
\mathsf{MSE}
. It is often attributed to B. Maurey and was published by Pisier
[
Pis81
]
. This is why it is referred to as
Maurey’s argument
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{M}\}
be a dictionary normalized in such a way that

\max_{1\leq j\leq M}|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq D\sqrt{n}\,.

Then for any integer
k
such that
1\leq k\leq M
and any positive
R
, we have

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{0}\leq k\end{subarray}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{1}\leq R\end{subarray}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+\frac{D^{2}R^{2}}{k}\,.

Proof.

Define

\bar{\theta}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{1}\leq R\end{subarray}}|\varphi_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}

and assume without loss of generality that
|\bar{\theta}_{1}|\geq|\bar{\theta}_{2}|\geq\ldots\geq|\bar{\theta}_{M}|
.
Let now
U\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
be a random vector with values in
\{0,\pm R\varphi_{1},\ldots,\pm R\varphi_{M}\}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(U=R\,\mathrm{sign}(\bar{\theta}_{j})\varphi_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{|\bar{\theta}_{j}|}{R}\,,

\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,M,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(U=0)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{|\bar{\theta}|_{1}}{R}\,.

Note that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[U]=\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}
and
|U|_{2}\leq RD\sqrt{n}
. Let now
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}
be
k
independent copies of
U
and define their average

\bar{U}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}\,.

Note that
\bar{U}=\varphi_{\tilde{\theta}}
for some
\tilde{\theta}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
such that
|\tilde{\theta}|_{0}\leq k
. Moreover, using the Pythagorean Theorem,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|f-\bar{U}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}+\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}-\bar{U}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}+|\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}-\bar{U}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|U-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[U]|_{2}^{2}}{k}

\displaystyle\leq|f-\varphi_{\bar{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}+\frac{(RD\sqrt{n})^{2}}{k}

To conclude the proof, note that

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|f-\bar{U}|_{2}^{2}=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|f-\varphi_{\tilde{\theta}}|_{2}^{2}\geq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{0}\leq k\end{subarray}}|f-\varphi_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}

and divide by
n
.
∎

Maurey’s argument implies the following corollary.

Corollary 3.7
.

Assume that the assumptions of Theorem
3.4
hold and that the dictionary
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{M}\}
is normalized in such a way that

\max_{1\leq j\leq M}|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}\,.

Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that the BIC estimator satisfies

\displaystyle\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{2\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\Big[

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\wedge\sigma|\theta|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(eM)}{n}}\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle+C\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n}

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Choosing
\alpha=1/3
in Theorem
3.4
yields

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}})\leq 2\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\Big\}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{n}

Let
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
.
It follows from Maurey’s argument that for any
k\in[M]
, there exists
\theta=\theta(\theta^{\prime},k)\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
such that
|\theta|_{0}=k
and

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})\leq\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+\frac{2|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}

It implies that

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\leq\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+\frac{2|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}

and furthermore that

\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\leq\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+\frac{2|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}

Since the above bound holds for any
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and
k\in[M]
, we can take an infimum with respect to both
\theta^{\prime}
and
k
on the right-hand side to get

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}

\displaystyle\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+C\min_{k}\Big(\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}\Big)\Big\}\,.

To control the minimum over
k
, we need to consider three cases for the quantity

\bar{k}=\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}}{\sigma}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(eM)}}.

1.

If
1\leq\bar{k}\leq M
, then we get

\min_{k}\Big(\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}\Big)\leq C\sigma|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(eM)}{n}}

2.

If
\bar{k}\leq 1
, then

|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}\leq C\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(eM)}{n}\,,

which yields

\min_{k}\Big(\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}\Big)\leq C\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(eM)}{n}

3.

If
\bar{k}\geq M
, then

\frac{\sigma^{2}M\log(eM)}{n}\leq C\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{M}\,.

Therefore, on the one hand, if
M\geq\frac{|\theta|_{1}}{\sigma\sqrt{\log(eM)/n}}
, we get

\min_{k}\Big(\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}\Big)\leq C\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{M}\leq C\sigma|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(eM)}{n}}\,.

On the other hand, if
M\leq\frac{|\theta|_{1}}{\sigma\sqrt{\log(eM)/n}}
, then for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{M}
, we have

\frac{\sigma^{2}|\theta|_{0}\log(eM)}{n}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}M\log(eM)}{n}\leq C\sigma|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(eM)}{n}}\,.\qed

Combined,

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\inf_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+C\min_{k}\Big(\frac{|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}^{2}}{k}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}k\log(eM)}{n}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\inf_{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta^{\prime}})+C\sigma|\theta^{\prime}|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log(eM}{n}}+C\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(eM)}{n}\Big\},

which together with Theorem 3.4 yields the claim.

Note that this last result holds for any estimator that satisfies an oracle inequality with respect to the
\ell_{0}
norm as in Theorem
3.4
. In particular, this estimator need not be the BIC estimator. An example is the Exponential Screening estimator of
[
RT11
]
.

Maurey’s argument allows us to enjoy the best of both the
\ell_{0}
and the
\ell_{1}
world. The rate adapts to the sparsity of the problem and can be even generalized to
\ell_{q}
-sparsity (see Problem
3.3
). However, it is clear from the proof that this argument is limited to squared
\ell_{2}
norms such as the one appearing in
\mathsf{MSE}
and extension to other risk measures is non-trivial. Some work has been done for non-Hilbert spaces
[
Pis81
,
DDGS97
]
using more sophisticated arguments.

13
Nonparametric regression

So far, the oracle inequalities that we have derived do not deal with the approximation error
\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})
. We kept it arbitrary and simply hoped that it was small. Note also that in the case of linear models, we simply assumed that the approximation error was zero. As we will see in this section, this error can be quantified under natural smoothness conditions if the dictionary of functions
\mathcal{H}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{M}\}
is chosen appropriately. In what follows, we assume for simplicity that
d=1
so that
f\,:\,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
and
\varphi_{j}\,:\,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
.

13.1
Fourier decomposition

Historically, nonparametric estimation was developed before high-dimensional statistics and most results hold for the case where the dictionary
\mathcal{H}=\{\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{M}\}
forms an orthonormal system of
L_{2}([0,1])
:

\int_{0}^{1}\varphi_{j}^{2}(x)\mathrm{d}x=1\,,\quad\int_{0}^{1}\varphi_{j}(x)\varphi_{k}(x)\mathrm{d}x=0,\ \forall\,j\neq k\,.

We will also deal with the case where
M=\infty
.

When
\mathcal{H}
is an orthonormal system, the coefficients
\theta_{j}^{*}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
defined by

\theta_{j}^{*}=\int_{0}^{1}f(x)\varphi_{j}(x)\mathrm{d}x\,,

are called
Fourier coefficients
of
f
.

Assume now that the regression function
f
admits the following decomposition

f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\theta_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\,.

There exists many choices for the orthonormal system and we give only two as examples.

Example 3.8
.

Trigonometric basis
. This is an orthonormal basis of
L_{2}([0,1])
. It is defined by

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\varphi_{2k}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\cos(2\pi kx)\,,

\displaystyle\varphi_{2k+1}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}\sin(2\pi kx)\,,

for
k=1,2,\dots
and
x\in[0,1]
. The fact that it is indeed an orthonormal system can be easily check using trigonometric identities.

The next example has received a lot of attention in the signal (sound, image, …) processing community.

Example 3.9
.

Wavelets
.
Let
\psi\,:\,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
be a sufficiently smooth and compactly supported function, called “
mother wavelet
”. Define the system of functions

\psi_{jk}(x)=2^{j/2}\psi(2^{j}x-k)\,,\quad j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

It can be shown that for a suitable
\psi
, the dictionary
\{\psi_{j,k},j,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
forms an orthonormal system of
L_{2}([0,1])
and sometimes a basis. In the latter case, for any function
g\in L_{2}([0,1])
, it holds

g=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\theta_{jk}\psi_{jk}\,,\quad\theta_{jk}=\int_{0}^{1}g(x)\psi_{jk}(x)\mathrm{d}x\,.

The coefficients
\theta_{jk}
are called
wavelet coefficients
of
g
.

The simplest example is given by the
Haar system
obtained by taking
\psi
to be the following piecewise constant function (see Figure
3.7
). We will not give more details about wavelets here but simply point the interested reader to
[
Mal09
]
.

\psi(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&0\leq x<1/2\\
-1&1/2\leq x\leq 1\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Figure 3.7:
The Haar mother wavelet

13.2
Sobolev classes and ellipsoids

We begin by describing a class of smooth functions where smoothness is understood in terms of its number of derivatives. Recall that
f^{(k)}
denotes the
k
-th derivative of
f
.

Definition 3.10
.

Fix parameters
\beta\in\{1,2,\dots\}
and
L>0
. The Sobolev class of functions
W(\beta,L)
is defined by

\displaystyle W(\beta,L)=\Big\{f\,:\,

\displaystyle[0,1]\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}\ :\ f\in L_{2}([0,1])\,,f^{(\beta-1)}\text{ is absolutely continuous and }

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}[f^{(\beta)}]^{2}\leq L^{2}\,,f^{(j)}(0)=f^{(j)}(1),j=0,\ldots,\beta-1\Big\}

Any function
f\in W(\beta,L)
can represented
8
8

8

In the sense that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}|f(t)-\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}\varphi_{j}(t)|^{2}\mathrm{d}t=0

as its Fourier expansion along the trigonometric basis:

f(x)=\theta_{1}^{*}\varphi_{1}(x)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\big(\theta_{2k}^{*}\varphi_{2k}(x)+\theta_{2k+1}^{*}\varphi_{2k+1}(x)\big)\,,\quad\forall\ x\in[0,1]\,,

where
\theta^{*}=\{\theta_{j}^{*}\}_{j\geq 1}
is in the space of squared summable sequences
\ell_{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{N})
defined by

\ell_{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{N})=\Big\{\theta\,:\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\theta_{j}^{2}<\infty\Big\}\,.

For any
\beta>0
, define the coefficients

a_{j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}j^{\beta}&\text{for $j$ even}\\
(j-1)^{\beta}&\text{for $j$ odd}\end{array}\right.

(3.9)

With these coefficients, we can define the Sobolev class of functions in terms of Fourier coefficients.

Theorem 3.11
.

Fix
\beta\geq 1
and
L>0
and let
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
denote the trigonometric basis of
L_{2}([0,1])
. Moreover, let
\{a_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
be defined as in (
3.9
). A function
f\in W(\beta,L)
can be represented as

f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\theta_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\,,

where the sequence
\{\theta_{j}^{*}\}_{j\geq 1}
belongs to Sobolev ellipsoid of
\ell_{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{N})
defined by

\Theta(\beta,Q)=\Big\{\theta\in\ell_{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{N})\,:\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\theta_{j}^{2}\leq Q\Big\}

for
Q=L^{2}/\pi^{2\beta}
.

Proof.

Let us first define the Fourier coefficients
\{s_{k}(j)\}_{k\geq 1}
of the
j
th derivative
f^{(j)}
of
f
for
j=1,\ldots,\beta
:

\displaystyle s_{1}(j)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}f^{(j)}(t)\mathrm{d}t=f^{(j-1)}(1)-f^{(j-1)}(0)=0\,,

\displaystyle s_{2k}(j)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(j)}(t)\cos(2\pi kt)\mathrm{d}t\,,

\displaystyle s_{2k+1}(j)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(j)}(t)\sin(2\pi kt)\mathrm{d}t\,,

The Fourier coefficients of
f
are given by
\theta_{k}=s_{k}(0)
.

Using integration by parts, we find that

\displaystyle s_{2k}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}f^{(\beta-1)}(t)\cos(2\pi kt)\Big|_{0}^{1}+(2\pi k)\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\beta-1)}(t)\sin(2\pi kt)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}[f^{(\beta-1)}(1)-f^{(\beta-1)}(0)]+(2\pi k)\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\beta-1)}(t)\sin(2\pi kt)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=(2\pi k)s_{2k+1}(\beta-1)\,.

Moreover,

\displaystyle s_{2k+1}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}f^{(\beta-1)}(t)\sin(2\pi kt)\Big|_{0}^{1}-(2\pi k)\sqrt{2}\int_{0}^{1}f^{(\beta-1)}(t)\cos(2\pi kt)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=-(2\pi k)s_{2k}(\beta-1)\,.

In particular, it yields

s_{2k}(\beta)^{2}+s_{2k+1}(\beta)^{2}=(2\pi k)^{2}\big[s_{2k}(\beta-1)^{2}+s_{2k+1}(\beta-1)^{2}\big]

By induction, we find that for any
k\geq 1
,

s_{2k}(\beta)^{2}+s_{2k+1}(\beta)^{2}=(2\pi k)^{2\beta}\big(\theta_{2k}^{2}+\theta_{2k+1}^{2}\big)

Next, it follows for the definition (
3.9
) of
a_{j}
that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2\pi k)^{2\beta}\big(\theta_{2k}^{2}+\theta_{2k+1}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\pi^{2\beta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{2k}^{2}\theta_{2k}^{2}+\pi^{2\beta}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}a_{2k+1}^{2}\theta_{2k+1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\pi^{2\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\theta_{j}^{2}\,.

Together with the Parseval identity, it yields

\int_{0}^{1}\big(f^{(\beta)}(t)\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}t=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}s_{2k}(\beta)^{2}+s_{2k+1}(\beta)^{2}=\pi^{2\beta}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\theta_{j}^{2}\,.

To conclude, observe that since
f\in W(\beta,L)
, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\big(f^{(\beta)}(t)\big)^{2}\mathrm{d}t\leq L^{2}\,,

so that
\theta\in\Theta(\beta,L^{2}/\pi^{2\beta})
.
∎

It can actually be shown that the reciprocal is true, that is, any function with Fourier coefficients in
\Theta(\beta,Q)
belongs to if
W(\beta,L)
, but we will not be needing this.

In what follows, we will define smooth functions as functions with Fourier coefficients (with respect to the trigonometric basis) in a Sobolev ellipsoid. By extension, we write
f\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
in this case and consider any real value for
\beta
.

Proposition 3.12
.

The Sobolev ellipsoids enjoy the following properties

(i)

For any
Q>0
,

0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta\ \Rightarrow\ \Theta(\beta,Q)\subset\Theta(\beta^{\prime},Q).

(ii)

For any
Q>0
,

\beta>\frac{1}{2}\ \Rightarrow\ f\ \text{is continuous}.

The proof is left as an exercise (Problem
3.6
).

It turns out that the first
n
functions in the trigonometric basis are not only orthonormal with respect to the inner product of
L_{2}
, but also with respect to the inner predictor associated with fixed design,
\langle f,g\rangle:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})g(X_{i})
, when the design is chosen to be
regular
, i.e.,
X_{i}=(i-1)/n
,
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Lemma 3.13
.

Assume that
\{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\}
is the regular design, i.e.,
X_{i}=(i-1)/n
. Then, for any
M\leq n-1
, the design matrix
\Phi=\{\varphi_{j}(X_{i})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq M\end{subarray}}
satisfies the
ORT
condition.

Proof.

Fix
j,j^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,
j\neq j^{\prime}
, and consider the inner product
\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j^{\prime}}
. Write
k_{j}=\lfloor j/2\rfloor
for the integer part of
j/2
and define the vectors
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
with coordinates such that
e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j}s}{n}}=a_{s+1}+\mathsf{i}b_{s+1}
and
e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j^{\prime}}s}{n}}=a^{\prime}_{s+1}+\mathsf{i}b^{\prime}_{s+1}
for
s\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
. It holds that

\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j^{\prime}}\in\{a^{\top}a^{\prime}\,,\,b^{\top}b^{\prime}\,,\,b^{\top}a^{\prime}\,,\,a^{\top}b^{\prime}\}\,,

depending on the parity of
j
and
j^{\prime}
.

On the one hand, observe that if
k_{j}\neq k_{j^{\prime}}
, we have for any
\sigma\in\{-1,+1\}
,

\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j}s}{n}}e^{\sigma\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j^{\prime}}s}{n}}=\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi(k_{j}+\sigma k_{j^{\prime}})s}{n}}=0\,.

On the other hand

\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j}s}{n}}e^{\sigma\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j^{\prime}}s}{n}}=(a+\mathsf{i}b)^{\top}(a^{\prime}+\sigma\mathsf{i}b^{\prime})=a^{\top}a^{\prime}-\sigma b^{\top}b^{\prime}+\mathsf{i}\big[b^{\top}a^{\prime}+\sigma a^{\top}b^{\prime}\big]

so that
a^{\top}a^{\prime}=\pm b^{\top}b^{\prime}=0
and
b^{\top}a^{\prime}=\pm a^{\top}b^{\prime}=0
whenever,
k_{j}\neq k_{j^{\prime}}
. It yields
\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j^{\prime}}=0
.

Next, consider the case where
k_{j}=k_{j^{\prime}}
so that

\sum_{s=0}^{n-1}e^{\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j}s}{n}}e^{\sigma\frac{\mathsf{i}2\pi k_{j^{\prime}}s}{n}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{if $\sigma=1$}\\
n&\text{if $\sigma=-1$}\\
\end{array}\right.\,.

On the one hand if
j\neq j^{\prime}
, it can only be the case that
\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j^{\prime}}\in\{b^{\top}a^{\prime}\,,\,a^{\top}b^{\prime}\}
but the same argument as above yields
b^{\top}a^{\prime}=\pm a^{\top}b^{\prime}=0
since the imaginary part of the inner product is still
0
. Hence, in that case,
\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j^{\prime}}=0
.
On the other hand, if
j=j^{\prime}
, then
a=a^{\prime}
and
b=b^{\prime}
so that it yields
a^{\top}a^{\prime}=|a|_{2}^{2}=n
and
b^{\top}b^{\prime}=|b|_{2}^{2}=n
which is equivalent to
\varphi_{j}^{\top}\varphi_{j}=|\varphi_{j}|_{2}^{2}=n
.
Therefore, the design matrix
\Phi
is such that

\Phi^{\top}\Phi=nI_{M}\,.\qed

13.3
Integrated squared error

As mentioned in the introduction of this chapter, the smoothness assumption allows us to control the approximation error. Before going into the details, let us gain some insight. Note first that if
\theta\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
, then
a_{j}^{2}\theta_{j}^{2}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
so that
|\theta_{j}|=o(j^{-\beta})
. Therefore, the
\theta_{j}
s decay polynomially to zero and it makes sense to approximate
f
by its truncated Fourier series

\sum_{j=1}^{M}\theta_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}=:\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}

for any fixed
M
. This truncation leads to a systematic error that vanishes as
M\to\infty
. We are interested in understanding the rate at which this happens.

The Sobolev assumption allows us to control precisely this error as a function of the tunable parameter
M
and the smoothness
\beta
.

Lemma 3.14
.

For any integer
M\geq 1
, and
f\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
,
\beta>1/2
, it holds

\|\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}-f\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{j>M}|\theta^{*}_{j}|^{2}\leq QM^{-2\beta}\,.

(3.10)

and for
M=n-1
, we have

|\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{n-1}-f|_{2}^{2}\leq 2n\Big(\sum_{j\geq n}|\theta^{*}_{j}|\Big)^{2}\lesssim Qn^{2-2\beta}\,.

(3.11)

Proof.

Note that for any
\theta\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
, if
\beta>1/2
, then

\displaystyle\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}|\theta_{j}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}a_{j}|\theta_{j}|\frac{1}{a_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}a_{j}^{2}\theta_{j}^{2}\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{a_{j}^{2}}}\quad\text{by Cauchy-Schwarz}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{Q\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j^{2\beta}}}<\infty

Since
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j}
forms an orthonormal system in
L_{2}([0,1])
, we have

\min_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\|\varphi_{\theta}-f\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\|\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}-f\|_{L_{2}}^{2}=\sum_{j>M}|\theta^{*}_{j}|^{2}\,.

When
\theta^{*}\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
, we have

\sum_{j>M}|\theta_{j}^{*}|^{2}=\sum_{j>M}a_{j}^{2}|\theta_{j}^{*}|^{2}\frac{1}{a_{j}^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{a_{M+1}^{2}}Q\leq\frac{Q}{M^{2\beta}}\,.

To prove the second part of the lemma, observe that

|\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{n-1}-f|_{2}=\big|\sum_{j\geq n}\theta_{j}^{*}\varphi_{j}\big|_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{2n}\sum_{j\geq n}|\theta_{j}^{*}|\,,

where in the last inequality, we used the fact that for the trigonometric basis
|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2n},j\geq 1
regardless of the choice of the design
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.
When
\theta^{*}\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
, we have

\sum_{j\geq n}|\theta^{*}_{j}|=\sum_{j\geq n}a_{j}|\theta^{*}_{j}|\frac{1}{a_{j}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{j\geq n}a_{j}^{2}|\theta^{*}_{j}|^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{j\geq n}\frac{1}{a_{j}^{2}}}\lesssim\sqrt{Q}n^{\frac{1}{2}-\beta}\,.\qed

Note that the truncated Fourier series
\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}
is an oracle: this is what we see when we view
f
through the lens of functions with only low frequency harmonics.

To estimate
\varphi_{\theta^{*}}=\varphi_{\theta^{\ast}}^{M}
, consider the estimator
\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}
where

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(Y_{i}-\varphi_{\theta}(X_{i})\big)^{2}\,,

which should be such that
\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}
is close to
\varphi_{\theta^{*}}
. For this estimator, we have proved (Theorem
3.3
) an oracle inequality for the
\mathsf{MSE}
that is of the form

|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-f|_{2}^{2}\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}|\varphi_{\theta}^{M}-f|_{2}^{2}+C\sigma^{2}M\log(1/\delta)\,,\qquad C>0\,.

It yields

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M})^{\top}(f-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M})+C\sigma^{2}M\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle=2(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M})^{\top}(\sum_{j>M}\theta^{*}_{j}\varphi_{j})+C\sigma^{2}M\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle=2(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M})^{\top}(\sum_{j\geq n}\theta^{*}_{j}\varphi_{j})+C\sigma^{2}M\log(1/\delta)\,,

where we used Lemma
3.13
in the last equality. Together with (
3.11
) and Young’s inequality
2ab\leq\alpha a^{2}+b^{2}/\alpha,a,b\geq 0
for any
\alpha>0
, we get

2(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M})^{\top}(\sum_{j\geq n}\theta^{*}_{j}\varphi_{j})\leq\alpha|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}|_{2}^{2}+\frac{C}{\alpha}Qn^{2-2\beta}\,,

for some positive constant
C
when
\theta^{*}\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
. As a result,

|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}Qn^{2-2\beta}+\frac{\sigma^{2}M}{1-\alpha}\log(1/\delta)

(3.12)

for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Since, Lemma
3.13
implies,
|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}|_{2}^{2}=n\|\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}^{M}-\varphi_{\theta^{*}}^{M}\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}
, we have proved the following theorem.

Theorem 3.15
.

Fix
\beta\geq(1+\sqrt{5})/4\simeq 0.81,Q>0,\delta>0
and assume the general regression model (
3.1
) with
f\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
and
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2}),\sigma^{2}\leq 1
. Moreover, let
M=\lceil n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\rceil
and
n
be large enough so that
M\leq n-1
. Then the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
defined in (
3.2
) with
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{M}
being the trigonometric basis, satisfies with probability
1-\delta
, for
n
large enough,

\|\varphi^{M}_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-f\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1+\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta))\,.

where the constant factors may depend on
\beta,Q
and
\sigma
.

Proof.

Choosing
\alpha=1/2
for example and absorbing
Q
in the constants, we get from (
3.12
) and Lemma
3.13
that
for
M\leq n-1
,

\|\varphi^{M}_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-\varphi^{M}_{\theta^{*}}\|^{2}_{L_{2}([0,1])}\lesssim n^{1-2\beta}+\sigma^{2}\frac{M\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,.

Using now Lemma
3.14
and
\sigma^{2}\leq 1
, we get

\|\varphi^{M}_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-f\|^{2}_{L_{2}([0,1])}\lesssim M^{-2\beta}+n^{1-2\beta}+\sigma^{2}\frac{M\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,.

Taking
M=\lceil n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\rceil\leq n-1
for
n
large enough yields

\|\varphi^{M}_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}-f\|^{2}_{L_{2}([0,1])}\lesssim n^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}+n^{1-2\beta}\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)\,.

To conclude the proof, simply note that for the prescribed
\beta
, we have
n^{1-2\beta}\leq n^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}
.
∎

13.4
Adaptive estimation

The rate attained by the projection estimator
\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}}
with
M=\lceil n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\rceil
is actually optimal so, in this sense, it is a good estimator. Unfortunately, its implementation requires the knowledge of the smoothness parameter
\beta
which is typically unknown, to determine the level
M
of truncation. The purpose of
adaptive estimation
is precisely to adapt to the unknown
\beta
, that is to build an estimator that does not depend on
\beta
and yet, attains a rate of the order of
Cn^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}
(up to a logarithmic lowdown). To that end, we will use the oracle inequalities for the BIC and Lasso estimator defined in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) respectively. In view of Lemma
3.13
, the design matrix
\Phi
actually satisfies the assumption
ORT
when we work with the trigonometric basis. This has two useful implications:

1.

Both estimators are actually thresholding estimators and can therefore be implemented efficiently

2.

The condition
INC(
k
)
is automatically satisfied for any
k\geq 1
.

These observations lead to the following corollary.

Corollary 3.16
.

Fix
\beta>(1+\sqrt{5})/4\simeq 0.81,Q>0,\delta>0
and
n
large enough to ensure
n-1\geq\lceil n^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\rceil
assume the general regression model (
3.1
) with
f\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
and
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2}),\sigma^{2}\leq 1
. Let
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n-1}
be the trigonometric basis. Denote by
\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}}}
(resp.
\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}}
) the BIC (resp. Lasso) estimator defined in (
3.3
) (resp. (
3.4
)) over
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n-1}
with regularization parameter given by (
3.5
) (resp. (
3.6
)). Then
\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}
, where
\hat{\theta}\in\{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}},\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\}
satisfies with probability
1-\delta
,

\|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-f\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}\lesssim(n/\log n)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}(1+\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta))\,,

where the constants may depend on
\beta
and
Q
.

Proof.

For
\hat{\theta}\in\{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{bic}},\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}\}
, adapting the proofs of Theorem
3.4
for the BIC estimator and Theorem
3.5
for the Lasso estimator, for any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n-1}
, with probability
1-\delta

|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-f|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1+\alpha}{1-\alpha}|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+R(|\theta|_{0})\,.

where

R(|\theta|_{0}):=\frac{C\sigma^{2}}{\alpha(1-\alpha)}|\theta_{0}|\log(en/\delta)

It yields

\displaystyle|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\alpha}{1-\alpha}|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+2(\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta})^{\top}(f-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta})+R(|\theta|_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{2\alpha}{1-\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha}\Big)|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta}-f|_{2}^{2}+\alpha|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta}|_{2}^{2}+R(|\theta|_{0})\,,

where we used Young’s inequality once again. Let
M\in\mathbb{N}
be determined later and choose now
\alpha=1/2
and
\theta=\theta^{*}_{M}
, where
\theta^{*}_{M}
is equal to
\theta^{*}
on its first
M
coordinates and
0
otherwise so that
\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}=\varphi^{M}_{\theta^{*}}
. It yields

|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}|_{2}^{2}\lesssim|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}-f|_{2}^{2}+R(M)\lesssim|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}}|_{2}^{2}+|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}}-f|_{2}^{2}+R(M)

Next, it follows from (
3.11
) that
|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}}-f|_{2}^{2}\lesssim Qn^{2-2\beta}
. Together with Lemma
3.13
, it yields

\|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}\lesssim\|\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}}-\varphi^{n-1}_{\theta^{*}_{M}}\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}+Qn^{1-2\beta}+\frac{R(M)}{n}\,.

Moreover, using (
3.10
), we find that

\|\varphi^{n-1}_{\hat{\theta}}-f\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}^{2}\lesssim M^{-2\beta}+Qn^{1-2\beta}+\frac{M}{n}\sigma^{2}\log(en/\delta)\,.

To conclude the proof, choose
M=\lceil(n/\log n)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\rceil
and observe that the choice of
\beta
ensures that
n^{1-2\beta}\lesssim M^{-2\beta}
.
∎

While there is sometimes a (logarithmic) price to pay for adaptation, it turns out that the extra logarithmic factor can be removed by a clever use of blocks (see
[
Tsy09
, Chapter 3]
). The reason why we get this extra logarithmic factor here is because we use a hammer that’s too big. Indeed, BIC and Lasso allow for “holes” in the Fourier decomposition, so we only get part of their potential benefits.

14
Problem Set

Problem 3.1
.

Show that the least-squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}
defined in (
3.2
) satisfies the following
exact
oracle inequality:

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\sigma^{2}{\frac{M}{n}}

for some constant
C
to be specified.

Problem 3.2
.

Assume that
\varepsilon\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n}(\sigma^{2})
and the vectors
\varphi_{j}
are normalized in such a way that
\max_{j}|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq\sqrt{n}
. Show that there exists a choice of
\tau
such that the Lasso estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}
with regularization parameter
2\tau
satisfies the following
exact
oracle inequality:

\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\hat{\theta}^{\mathcal{L}}})\leq\inf_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}}\Big\{\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C\sigma|\theta|_{1}\sqrt{\frac{\log M}{n}}\Big\}

with probability at least
1-M^{-c}
for some positive constants
C,c
.

Problem 3.3
.

Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{M}\}
be a dictionary normalized in such a way that
\max_{j}|\varphi_{j}|_{2}\leq D\sqrt{n}
. Show that for any integer
k
such that
1\leq k\leq M
, we have

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{0}\leq 2k\end{subarray}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{M}\\
|\theta|_{w\ell_{q}}\leq 1\end{subarray}}\mathsf{MSE}(\varphi_{\theta})+C_{q}D^{2}\frac{\big(M^{\frac{1}{\bar{q}}}-k^{\frac{1}{\bar{q}}}\big)^{2}}{k}\,,

where
|\theta|_{w\ell_{q}}
denotes the weak
\ell_{q}
norm and
\bar{q}
is such that
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{\bar{q}}=1
, for
q>1
.

Problem 3.4
.

Show that the trigonometric basis and the Haar system indeed form an orthonormal system of
L_{2}([0,1])
.

Problem 3.5
.

Consider the
n\times d
random matrix
\Phi=\{\varphi_{j}(X_{i})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq d\end{subarray}}
where
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are i.i.d uniform random variables on the interval
[0,1]
and
\phi_{j}
is the trigonometric basis as defined in Example
3.8
. Show that
\Phi
satisfies
INC
(k)
with probability at least
.9
as long as
n\geq Ck^{2}\log(d)
for some large enough constant
C>0
.

Problem 3.6
.

If
f\in\Theta(\beta,Q)
for
\beta>1/2
and
Q>0
, then
f
is continuous.

Chapter 4
Minimax Lower Bounds

In the previous chapters, we have proved several upper bounds and the goal of this chapter is to assess their optimality. Specifically, our goal is to answer the following questions:

1.

Can our analysis be improved? In other words: do the estimators that we have studied actually satisfy better bounds?

2.

Can any estimator improve upon these bounds?

Both questions ask about some form of
optimality
. The first one is about optimality of an estimator, whereas the second one is about optimality of a bound.

The difficulty of these questions varies depending on whether we are looking for a positive or a negative answer. Indeed, a positive answer to these questions simply consists in finding a better proof for the estimator we have studied (question 1.) or simply finding a better estimator, together with a proof that it performs better (question 2.). A negative answer is much more arduous. For example, in question 2., it is a statement about
all estimators
. How can this be done? The answer lies in information theory (see
[
CT06
]
for a nice introduction).

In this chapter, we will see how to give a negative answer to question 2. It will imply a negative answer to question 1.

15
Optimality in a minimax sense

Consider the Gaussian Sequence Model (GSM) where we observe
\mathbf{Y}=(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d})^{\top}
, defined by

Y_{i}=\theta^{*}_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\,,\qquad i=1,\ldots,d\,,

(4.1)

where
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{d})^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d})
,
\theta^{*}=(\theta^{*}_{1},\ldots,\theta^{*}_{d})^{\top}\in\Theta
is the parameter of interest and
\Theta\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
is a given set of parameters. We will need a more precise notation for probabilities and expectations throughout this chapter. Denote by
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta^{*}}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta^{*}}
the probability measure and corresponding expectation that are associated to the distribution of
\mathbf{Y}
from the GSM (
4.1
).

Recall that GSM is a special case of the linear regression model when the design matrix satisfies the ORT condition. In this case, we have proved several performance guarantees (
upper bounds
) for various choices of
\Theta
that can be expressed either in the form

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\big]\leq C\phi(\Theta)

(4.2)

or the form

|\hat{\theta}_{n}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq C\phi(\Theta)\,,\quad\text{with prob.}\ 1-d^{-2}

(4.3)

For some constant
C
. The rates
\phi(\Theta)
for different choices of
\Theta
that we have obtained are gathered in Table
1
together with the estimator (and the corresponding result from Chapter
2
) that was employed to obtain this rate.

\Theta

\phi(\Theta)

Estimator

Result

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n}

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}

Theorem
2.2

\mathcal{B}_{1}

\displaystyle\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}

Theorem
2.4

\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log(ed/k)

\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)}

Corollaries
2.8
-
2.9

Table 1:
Rate
\phi(\Theta)
obtained for different choices of
\Theta
.

Can any of these results be improved? In other words, does there exists another estimator
\tilde{\theta}
such that
\sup_{\theta^{*}\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}|\tilde{\theta}-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\ll\phi(\Theta)
?

A first step in this direction is the Cramér-Rao lower bound
[
Sha03
]
that allows us to prove lower bounds in terms of the Fisher information. Nevertheless, this notion of optimality is too stringent and often leads to nonexistence of optimal estimators. Rather, we prefer here the notion of
minimax optimality
that characterizes how fast
\theta^{*}
can be estimated
uniformly
over
\Theta
.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that an estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}
is
minimax optimal over
\Theta
if it satisfies (
4.2
) and there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq C^{\prime}\phi(\Theta)

(4.4)

where the infimum is taker over all estimators (i.e., measurable functions of
\mathbf{Y}
). Moreover,
\phi(\Theta)
is called
minimax rate of estimation over
\Theta
.

Note that minimax rates of convergence
\phi
are defined up to multiplicative constants. We may then choose this constant such that the minimax rate has a simple form such as
\sigma^{2}d/n
as opposed to
7\sigma^{2}d/n
for example.

This definition can be adapted to rates that hold with high probability. As we saw in Chapter
2
(Cf. Table
1
), the upper bounds in expectation and those with high probability are of the same order of magnitude. It is also the case for lower bounds. Indeed, observe that it follows from the Markov inequality that for any
A>0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}\big[\phi^{-1}(\Theta)|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\big]\geq A\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big[\phi^{-1}(\Theta)|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}>A\big]

(4.5)

Therefore, (
4.4
) follows if we prove that

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big[|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}>A\phi(\Theta)\big]\geq C

for some positive constants
A
and
C"
. The above inequality also implies a lower bound with high probability. We can therefore employ the following alternate definition for minimax optimality.

Definition 4.2
.

We say that an estimator
\hat{\theta}
is
minimax optimal over
\Theta
if it satisfies either (
4.2
) or (
4.3
) and there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big[|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}>\phi(\Theta)\big]\geq C^{\prime}

(4.6)

where the infimum is taken over all estimators (i.e., measurable functions of
\mathbf{Y}
). Moreover,
\phi(\Theta)
is called
minimax rate of estimation over
\Theta
.

16
Reduction to finite hypothesis testing

Minimax lower bounds rely on information theory and follow from a simple principle: if the number of observations is too small, it may be hard to distinguish between two probability distributions that are close to each other. For example, given
n
i.i.d. observations, it is impossible to reliably decide whether they are drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
or
\mathcal{N}(\frac{1}{n},1)
. This simple argument can be made precise using the formalism of
statistical hypothesis testing
. To do so, we reduce our estimation problem to a testing problem. The reduction consists of two steps.

1.

Reduction to a finite number of parameters.
In this step the goal is to find the largest possible number of parameters
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}\in\Theta
under the constraint that

|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}\geq 4\phi(\Theta)\,.

(4.7)

This problem boils down to a
packing
of the set
\Theta
.

Then we can use the following trivial observations:

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big[|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}>\phi(\Theta)\big]\geq\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}^{2}>\phi(\Theta)\big]\,.

2.

Reduction to a hypothesis testing problem.
In this second step, the necessity of the constraint (
4.7
) becomes apparent.

For any estimator
\hat{\theta}
, define the minimum distance test
\psi(\hat{\theta})
that is associated to it by

\psi(\hat{\theta})=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{1\leq j\leq M}|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}\,,

with ties broken arbitrarily.

Next observe that if, for some
j=1,\ldots,M
,
\psi(\hat{\theta})\neq j
, then there exists
k\neq j
such that
|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{k}|_{2}\leq|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}
. Together with the reverse triangle inequality it yields

|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}\geq|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}-|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{k}|_{2}\geq|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}-|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}

so that

|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}

Together with constraint (
4.7
), it yields

|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}^{2}\geq\phi(\Theta)

As a result,

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[|\hat{\theta}-\theta_{j}|_{2}^{2}>\phi(\Theta)\big]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[\psi(\hat{\theta})\neq j\big]

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\psi}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[\psi\neq j\big]

where the infimum is taken over all tests
\psi
based on
\mathbf{Y}
and that take values in
\{1,\ldots,M\}
.

Conclusion:
it is sufficient for proving lower bounds to find
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}\in\Theta
such that
|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}\geq 4\phi(\Theta)
and

\inf_{\psi}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[\psi\neq j\big]\geq C^{\prime}\,.

The above quantity is called
minimax probability of error
. In the next sections, we show how it can be bounded from below using arguments from information theory. For the purpose of illustration, we begin with the simple case where
M=2
in the next section.

17
Lower bounds based on two hypotheses

17.1
The Neyman-Pearson Lemma and the total variation distance

Consider two probability measures
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
and observations
X
drawn from either
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
or
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
. We want to know which distribution
X
comes from. It corresponds to the following statistical hypothesis problem:

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle Z\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle Z\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}

A test
\psi=\psi(Z)\in\{0,1\}
indicates which hypothesis should be true. Any test
\psi
can make two types of errors. It can commit either an error of type I (
\psi=1
whereas
Z\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
) or an error of type II (
\psi=0
whereas
Z\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
). Of course, the test may also be correct. The following fundamental result called the
Neyman Pearson Lemma
indicates that any test
\psi
is bound to commit one of these two types of error with positive probability unless
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
have essentially disjoint support.

Let
\nu
be a sigma finite measure satisfying
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}\ll\nu
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}\ll\nu
. For example, we can take
\nu=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
. It follows from the Radon-Nikodym theorem
[
Bil95
]
that both
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
admit probability densities with respect to
\nu
. We denote them by
p_{0}
and
p_{1}
respectively. For any function
f
, we write for simplicity

\int f=\int f(x)\nu(\mathrm{d}x)

Lemma 4.3

(Neyman-Pearson)
.

Let
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
be two probability measures. Then for any test
\psi
, it holds

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi=0)\geq\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})

Moreover, equality holds for the
Likelihood Ratio test

\psi^{\star}=\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(p_{1}\geq p_{0})
.

Proof.

Observe first that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi^{\star}=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi^{\star}=0)

\displaystyle=\int_{\psi^{*}=1}p_{0}+\int_{\psi^{*}=0}p_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{1}\geq p_{0}}p_{0}+\int_{p_{1}<p_{0}}p_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{1}\geq p_{0}}\min(p_{0},p_{1})+\int_{p_{1}<p_{0}}\min(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle=\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})\,.

Next for any test
\psi
, define its rejection region
R=\{\psi=1\}
. Let
R^{\star}=\{p_{1}\geq p_{0}\}
denote the rejection region of the likelihood ratio test
\psi^{\star}
. It holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi=0)

\displaystyle=1+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(R)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(R)

\displaystyle=1+\int_{R}p_{0}-p_{1}

\displaystyle=1+\int_{R\cap R^{\star}}p_{0}-p_{1}+\int_{R\cap(R^{\star})^{c}}p_{0}-p_{1}

\displaystyle=1-\int_{R\cap R^{\star}}|p_{0}-p_{1}|+\int_{R\cap(R^{\star})^{c}}|p_{0}-p_{1}|

\displaystyle=1+\int|p_{0}-p_{1}|\big[\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(R\cap(R^{\star})^{c})-\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(R\cap R^{\star})\big]

The above quantity is clearly minimized for
R=R^{\star}
.
∎

The lower bound in the Neyman-Pearson lemma is related to a well-known quantity: the total variation distance.

Definition-Proposition 4.4
.

The
total variation distance
between two probability measures
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1})

\displaystyle=\sup_{R\in\mathcal{A}}|\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(R)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(R)|

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle=\sup_{R\in\mathcal{A}}\Big|\int_{R}p_{0}-p_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int|p_{0}-p_{1}|

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle=1-\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle(iv)

\displaystyle=1-\inf_{\psi}\big[\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi=0)\big]

\displaystyle(v)

where the infimum above is taken over all tests.

Proof.

Clearly
(i)=(ii)
and the Neyman-Pearson Lemma gives
(iv)=(v)
. Moreover, by identifying a test
\psi
to its rejection region, it is not hard to see that
(i)=(v)
. Therefore it remains only to show that
(iii)
is equal to any of the other expressions. Hereafter, we show that
(iii)=(iv)
. To that end, observe that

\displaystyle\int|p_{0}-p_{1}|

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{1}\geq p_{0}}p_{1}-p_{0}+\int_{p_{1}<p_{0}}p_{0}-p_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{p_{1}\geq p_{0}}p_{1}+\int_{p_{1}<p_{0}}p_{0}-\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle=1-\int_{p_{1}<p_{0}}p_{1}+1-\int_{p_{1}\geq p_{0}}p_{0}-\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})

\displaystyle=2-2\int\min(p_{0},p_{1})

∎

In view of the Neyman-Pearson lemma, it is clear that if we want to prove large lower bounds, we need to find probability distributions that are close in total variation. Yet, this conflicts with constraint (
4.7
) and a tradeoff needs to be achieved. To that end, in the Gaussian sequence model, we need to be able to compute the total variation distance between
\mathcal{N}(\theta_{0},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d})
and
\mathcal{N}(\theta_{1},\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d})
. None of the expression in Definition-Proposition
4.4
gives an easy way to do so. The Kullback-Leibler divergence is much more convenient.

17.2
The Kullback-Leibler divergence

Definition 4.5
.

The Kullback-Leibler divergence between probability measures
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
is given by

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}\,,&\text{if}\ \mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}\ll\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}\\
\infty\,,&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{array}\right.

It can be shown
[
Tsy09
]
that the integral is always well defined when
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}\ll\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
(though it can be equal to
\infty
even in this case). Unlike the total variation distance, the Kullback-Leibler divergence is not a distance. Actually, it is not even symmetric. Nevertheless, it enjoys properties that are very useful for our purposes.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability measures. Then

1.

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\geq 0
.

2.

The function
(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\mapsto\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})
is convex.

3.

If
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are product measures, i.e.,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{Q}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Q}_{i}

then

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i},\mathbb{Q}_{i})\,.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
is not absolutely continuous then the result is trivial. Next, assume that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
and let
X\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
.

1. Observe that by Jensen’s inequality,

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})=-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}(X)\Big)\geq-\log\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}(X)\Big)=-\log(1)=0\,.

2. Consider the function
f:(x,y)\mapsto x\log(x/y)
and compute its Hessian:

\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}=\frac{1}{x}\,,\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial y^{2}}=\frac{x}{y^{2}}\,,\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x\partial y}=-\frac{1}{y}\,,

We check that the matrix
\displaystyle H=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{1}{x}&-\frac{1}{y}\\
-\frac{1}{y}&\frac{x}{y^{2}}\end{array}\right)
is positive semidefinite for all
x,y>0
. To that end, compute its determinant and trace:

\det(H)=\frac{1}{y^{2}}-\frac{1}{y^{2}}=0\,,\mathsf{Tr}(H)=\frac{1}{x}\frac{x}{y^{2}}>0\,.

Therefore,
H
has one null eigenvalue and one positive one and must therefore be positive semidefinite so that the function
f
is convex on
(0,\infty)^{2}
. Since the KL divergence is a sum of such functions, it is also convex on the space of positive measures.

3. Note that if
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(X)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{i}}(X_{i})\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(X_{1})\cdots\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{n}(X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}_{i}}(X_{i})\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i}(X_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{i},\mathbb{Q}_{i})

∎

Point 2. in Proposition
4.6
is particularly useful in statistics where observations typically consist of
n
independent random variables.

Example 4.7
.

For any
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, let
P_{\theta}
denote the distribution of
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
. Then

\mathsf{KL}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{(\theta_{i}-\theta^{\prime}_{i})^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}=\frac{|\theta-\theta^{\prime}|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\,.

The proof is left as an exercise (see Problem
4.1
).

The Kullback-Leibler divergence is easier to manipulate than the total variation distance but only the latter is related to the minimax probability of error. Fortunately, these two quantities can be compared using Pinsker’s inequality. We prove here a slightly weaker version of Pinsker’s inequality that will be sufficient for our purpose. For a stronger statement, see
[
Tsy09
]
, Lemma 2.5.

Lemma 4.8

(Pinsker’s inequality.)
.

Let
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability measures such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
. Then

\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\sqrt{\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})}\,.

Proof.

Note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\int_{pq>0}p\log\Big(\frac{p}{q}\Big)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{pq>0}p\log\Big(\sqrt{\frac{q}{p}}\Big)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{pq>0}p\log\Big(\Big[\sqrt{\frac{q}{p}}-1\Big]+1\Big)

\displaystyle\geq-2\int_{pq>0}p\Big[\sqrt{\frac{q}{p}}-1\Big]\qquad\text{ (by Jensen)}

\displaystyle=2-2\int\sqrt{pq}

Next, note that

\displaystyle\Big(\int\sqrt{pq}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big(\int\sqrt{\max(p,q)\min(p,q)}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\max(p,q)\int\min(p,q)\qquad\text{(by Cauchy-Schwarz)}

\displaystyle=\big[2-\int\min(p,q)\big]\int\min(p,q)

\displaystyle=\big(1+\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\big)\big(1-\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\big)

\displaystyle=1-\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}

The two displays yield

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\geq 2-2\sqrt{1-\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}}\geq\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})^{2}\,,

where we used the fact that
0\leq\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq 1
and
\sqrt{1-x}\leq 1-x/2
for
x\in[0,1]
.
∎

Pinsker’s inequality yields the following theorem for the GSM.

Theorem 4.9
.

Assume that
\Theta
contains two hypotheses
\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{1}
such that
|\theta_{0}-\theta_{1}|_{2}^{2}=8\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}/n
for some
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. Then

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n})\geq\frac{1}{2}-\alpha\,.

Proof.

Write for simplicity
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{j}=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}
,
j=0,1
.
Recall that it follows from the reduction to hypothesis testing that

\displaystyle\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n})

\displaystyle\geq\inf_{\psi}\max_{j=0,1}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{j}(\psi\neq j)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\inf_{\psi}\Big(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi=0)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[1-\mathsf{TV}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1})\Big]

(Prop.-def.
4.4
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\Big[1-\sqrt{\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})}\Big]

(Lemma
4.8
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[1-\sqrt{\frac{n|\theta_{1}-\theta_{0}|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}\Big]

(Example
4.7
)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[1-2\alpha\Big]

Clearly, the result of Theorem
4.9
matches the upper bound for
\Theta=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
only for
d=1
. How about larger
d
? A quick inspection of our proof shows that our technique, in its present state, cannot yield better results. Indeed, there are only two known candidates for the choice of
\theta^{*}
. With this knowledge, one can obtain upper bounds that do not depend on
d
by simply projecting
Y
onto the linear span of
\theta_{0},\theta_{1}
and then solving the GSM in two dimensions. To obtain larger lower bounds, we need to use more than two hypotheses. In particular, in view of the above discussion, we need a set of hypotheses that spans a linear space of dimension proportional to
d
. In principle, we should need at least order
d
hypotheses but we will actually need much more.

18
Lower bounds based on many hypotheses

The reduction to hypothesis testing from Section
16
allows us to use more than two hypotheses. Specifically, we should find
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
such that
