\inf_{\psi}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[\psi\neq j\big]\geq C^{\prime}\,,

for some positive constant
C^{\prime}
. Unfortunately, the Neyman-Pearson Lemma no longer exists for more than two hypotheses. Nevertheless, it is possible to relate the minimax probability of error directly to the Kullback-Leibler divergence, without involving the total variation distance. This is possible using a well known result from information theory called
Fano’s inequality
.

Theorem 4.10

(Fano’s inequality)
.

Let
P_{1},\ldots,P_{M},M\geq 2
be probability distributions such that
P_{j}\ll P_{k}
,
\forall\,j,k
. Then

\inf_{\psi}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}P_{j}\big[\psi(X)\neq j\big]\geq 1-\frac{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{M}\mathsf{KL}(P_{j},P_{k})+\log 2}{\log M}

where the infimum is taken over all tests with values in
\{1,\ldots,M\}
.

Proof.

Define

p_{j}=P_{j}(\psi=j)\qquad\text{and}\qquad q_{j}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{k=1}^{M}P_{k}(\psi=j)

so that

\bar{p}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}p_{j}\in(0,1)\,,\qquad\bar{q}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}q_{j}\,.

Moreover, define the KL divergence between two Bernoulli distributions:

\mathsf{kl}(p,q)=p\log\big(\frac{p}{q}\big)+(1-p)\log\big(\frac{1-p}{1-q}\big)\,.

Note that
\bar{p}\log\bar{p}+(1-\bar{p})\log(1-\bar{p})\geq-\log 2
, which yields

\bar{p}\leq\frac{\mathsf{kl}(\bar{p},\bar{q})+\log 2}{\log M}\,.

Next, observe that by convexity (Proposition
4.6
, 2.), it holds

\mathsf{kl}(\bar{p},\bar{q})\leq\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathsf{kl}(p_{j},q_{j})\leq\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j.k=1}^{M}\mathsf{kl}(P_{j}(\psi=j),P_{k}(\psi=j))\,.

It remains to show that

\mathsf{kl}(P_{j}(\psi=j),P_{k}(\psi=j))\leq\mathsf{KL}(P_{j},P_{k})\,.

This result can be seen to follow directly from a well-known inequality often referred to as
data processing inequality
but we are going to prove it directly.

Denote by
\mathrm{d}P_{k}^{=j}
(resp.
\mathrm{d}P_{k}^{\neq j}
) the conditional density of
P_{k}
given
\psi(X)=j
(resp.
\psi(X)\neq j
) and recall that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(P_{j},P_{k})=\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{j}}{\mathrm{d}P_{k}}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{\psi=j}\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{j}}{\mathrm{d}P_{k}}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{j}+\int_{\psi\neq j}\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{j}}{\mathrm{d}P_{k}}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{j}

\displaystyle=P_{j}(\psi=j)\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}P_{j}^{=j}}{\mathrm{d}P_{k}^{=j}}\frac{P_{j}(\psi=j)}{P_{k}(\psi=j)}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{j}^{=j}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+P_{j}(\psi\neq j)\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{\neq j}}{\mathrm{d}P_{k}^{\neq j}}\frac{P_{j}(\psi\neq j)}{P_{k}(\psi\neq j)}\Big)\mathrm{d}P_{j}^{\neq j}

\displaystyle=P_{j}(\psi=j)\Big(\log\Big(\frac{P_{j}(\psi=j)}{P_{k}(\psi=j)}\Big)+\mathsf{KL}(P_{j}^{=j},P_{j}^{=j})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+P_{j}(\psi\neq j)\Big(\log\Big(\frac{P_{j}(\psi\neq j)}{P_{k}(\psi\neq j)}\Big)+\mathsf{KL}(P_{j}^{\neq j},P_{j}^{\neq j})\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{kl}(P_{j}(\psi=j),P_{k}(\psi=j))\,.

where we used Proposition
4.6
, 1.

We have proved that

\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}P_{j}(\psi=j)\leq\frac{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{M}\mathsf{KL}(P_{j},P_{k})+\log 2}{\log M}\,.

Passing to the complemetary sets completes the proof of Fano’s inequality.
∎

Fano’s inequality leads to the following useful theorem.

Theorem 4.11
.

Assume that
\Theta
contains
M\geq 5
hypotheses
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
such that for some constant
0<\alpha<1/4
, it holds

(i)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}\geq 4\phi

(ii)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(M)

Then

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq\phi\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha\,.

Proof.

in view of (
i
), it follows from the reduction to hypothesis testing that it is sufficient to prove that

\inf_{\psi}\max_{1\leq j\leq M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta_{j}}\big[\psi\neq j\big]\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha.

If follows from (
ii
) and Example
4.7
that

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{j},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{k})=\frac{n|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\leq\alpha\log(M)\,.

Moreover, since
M\geq 5
,

\frac{\frac{1}{M^{2}}\sum_{j,k=1}^{M}\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{j},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{k})+\log 2}{\log(M-1)}\leq\frac{\alpha\log(M)+\log 2}{\log(M-1)}\leq 2\alpha+\frac{1}{2}\,.

The proof then follows from Fano’s inequality.
∎

Theorem
4.11
indicates that we must take
\phi\leq\frac{\alpha\sigma^{2}}{2n}\log(M)
. Therefore, the larger the
M
, the larger the lower bound can be. However,
M
cannot be arbitrary larger because of the constraint
(i)
. We are therefore facing a
packing
problem where the goal is to “pack” as many Euclidean balls of radius proportional to
\sigma\sqrt{\log(M)/n}
in
\Theta
under the constraint that their centers remain close together (constraint
(ii)
). If
\Theta=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, this the goal is to pack the Euclidean ball of radius
R=\sigma\sqrt{2\alpha\log(M)/n}
with Euclidean balls of radius
R\sqrt{2\alpha/\gamma}
. This can be done using a volume argument (see Problem
4.3
). However, we will use the more versatile lemma below. It gives a a lower bound on the size of a packing of the discrete hypercube
\{0,1\}^{d}
with respect to the
Hamming distance
defined by

\rho(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(\omega_{i}\neq\omega^{\prime}_{j})\,,\qquad\forall\,\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{d}.

Lemma 4.12

(Varshamov-Gilbert)
.

For any
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
, there exist binary vectors
\omega_{1},\ldots\omega_{M}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
such that

(i)

\displaystyle\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq\big(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma\big)d
for all
j\neq k
,

(ii)

\displaystyle M=\lfloor e^{\gamma^{2}d}\rfloor\geq e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}d}{2}}
.

Proof.

Let
\omega_{j,i}
,
1\leq i\leq d,1\leq j\leq M
be i.i.d. Bernoulli random variables with parameter
1/2
and observe that

d-\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})=X\sim\mathsf{Bin}(d,1/2)\,.

Therefore it follows from a union bound that

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big[\exists j\neq k\,,\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})<\big(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma\big)d\big]\leq\frac{M(M-1)}{2}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(X-\frac{d}{2}>\gamma d\big)\,.

Hoeffding’s inequality then yields

\frac{M(M-1)}{2}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(X-\frac{d}{2}>\gamma d\big)\leq\exp\Big(-2\gamma^{2}d+\log\big(\frac{M(M-1)}{2}\big)\Big)<1

as soon as

M(M-1)<2\exp\Big(2\gamma^{2}d\Big).

A sufficient condition for the above inequality to hold is to take
M=\lfloor e^{\gamma^{2}d}\rfloor\geq e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}d}{2}}
. For this value of
M
, we have

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\forall j\neq k\,,\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq\big(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma\big)d\big)>0

and by virtue of the probabilistic method, there exist
\omega_{1},\ldots\omega_{M}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
that satisfy
(i)
and
(ii)

∎

19
Application to the Gaussian sequence model

We are now in a position to apply Theorem
4.11
by choosing
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
based on
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
from the Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma.

19.1
Lower bounds for estimation

Take
\gamma=1/4
and apply the Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma to obtain
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
with
M=\lfloor e^{d/16}\rfloor\geq e^{d/32}
and such that

\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq d/4
for all
j\neq k
. Let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
be such that

\theta_{j}=\omega_{j}\frac{\beta\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

for some
\beta>0
to be chosen later. We can check the conditions of Theorem
4.11
:

(i)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq 4\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}d}{16n}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\leq\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}d}{n}\leq\frac{32\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(M)=\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(M)
,

for
\beta=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha}}{4}
. Applying now Theorem
4.11
yields

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\alpha}{256}\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n}\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha\,.

It implies the following corollary.

Corollary 4.13
.

The minimax rate of estimation of over
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
in the Gaussian sequence model is
\phi(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d})=\sigma^{2}d/n
. Moreover, it is attained by the least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}=\mathbf{Y}
.

Note that this rate is minimax over sets
\Theta
that are strictly smaller than
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
(see Problem
4.4
). Indeed, it is minimax over any subset of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
that contains
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
.

19.2
Lower bounds for sparse estimation

It appears from Table
1
that when estimating sparse vectors, we have to pay for an extra logarithmic term
\log(ed/k)
for not knowing the sparsity pattern of the unknown
\theta^{*}
. In this section, we show that this term is unavoidable as it appears in the minimax optimal rate of estimation of sparse vectors.

Note that the vectors
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
employed in the previous subsection are not guaranteed to be sparse because the vectors
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
obtained from the Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma may themselves not be sparse. To overcome this limitation, we need a sparse version of the Varhsamov-Gilbert lemma.

Lemma 4.14

(Sparse Varshamov-Gilbert)
.

There exist positive constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
such that the following holds for any two integers
k
and
d
such that
1\leq k\leq d/8
.
There exist binary vectors
\omega_{1},\ldots\omega_{M}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
such that

(i)

\displaystyle\rho(\omega_{i},\omega_{j})\geq\frac{k}{2}
for all
i\neq j
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\log(M)\geq\frac{k}{8}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})
.

(iii)

\displaystyle|\omega_{j}|_{0}=k
for all
j
.

Proof.

Take
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
independently and uniformly at random from the set

C_{0}(k)=\{\omega\in\{0,1\}^{d}\,:\,|\omega|_{0}=k\}

of
k
-sparse binary random vectors. Note that
C_{0}(k)
has cardinality
\binom{d}{k}
. To choose
\omega_{j}
uniformly from
C_{0}(k)
, we proceed as follows. Let
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
be
k
random variables such that
U_{1}
is drawn uniformly at random from
\{1,\ldots,d\}
and for any
i=2,\ldots,k
, conditionally on
U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}
, the random variable
U_{i}
is drawn uniformly at random from
\{1,\ldots,d\}\setminus\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{i-1}\}
. Then define

\omega=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\text{if}\ i\in\{U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}\}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{array}\right.

Clearly, all outcomes in
C_{0}(k)
are equally likely under this distribution and therefore,
\omega
is uniformly distributed on
C_{0}(k)
. Observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\exists\,\omega_{j}\neq\omega_{k}\,:\,\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})<k\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{d}\\
|x|_{0}=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\exists\,\omega_{j}\neq x\,:\,\rho(\omega_{j},x)<\frac{k}{2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\{0,1\}^{d}\\
|x|_{0}=k\end{subarray}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\omega_{j}\neq x\,:\,\rho(\omega_{j},x)<\frac{k}{2}\big)

\displaystyle=M\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\omega\neq x_{0}\,:\,\rho(\omega,x_{0})<\frac{k}{2}\big),

where
\omega
has the same distribution as
\omega_{1}
and
x_{0}
is any
k
-sparse vector in
\{0,1\}^{d}
.
The last equality holds by symmetry since (i) all the
\omega_{j}
s have the same distribution and (ii) all the outcomes of
\omega_{j}
are equally likely.

Note that

\rho(\omega,x_{0})\geq k-\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\,,

where
Z_{i}=\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(U_{i}\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(x_{0}))
. Indeed the left hand side is the number of coordinates on which the vectors
\omega,x_{0}
disagree and the right hand side is the number of coordinates in
\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(x_{0})
on which the two vectors disagree. In particular, we have that
Z_{1}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(k/d)
and for any
i=2,\ldots,d
, conditionally on
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{i-i}
, we have
Z_{i}\sim\mathsf{Ber}(Q_{i})
, where

Q_{i}=\frac{k-\sum_{l=1}^{i-1}Z_{l}}{p-(i-1)}\leq\frac{k}{d-k}\leq\frac{2k}{d},

since
k\leq d/2
.

Next we apply a Chernoff bound to get that for any
s>0
,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\omega\neq x_{0}\,:\,\rho(\omega,x_{0})<\frac{k}{2}\big)\leq\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}>\frac{k}{2}\big)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big[\exp\big(s\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\big)\Big]e^{-\frac{sk}{2}}

The above MGF can be controlled by induction on
k
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big[\exp\big(s\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big[\exp\big(s\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}Z_{i}\big)\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\exp\big(sZ_{k}\big|Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k=1}\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big[\exp\big(s\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}Z_{i}\big)(Q_{k}(e^{s}-1)+1)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big[\exp\big(s\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}Z_{i}\big)\Big]\big(\frac{2k}{d}(e^{s}-1)+1\big)

\displaystyle\quad\vdots

\displaystyle\leq\big(\frac{2k}{d}(e^{s}-1)+1\big)^{k}

\displaystyle=2^{k}

For
s=\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})
. Putting everything together, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\exists\,\omega_{j}\neq\omega_{k}\,:\,\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})<k\big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(\log M+k\log 2-\frac{sk}{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\log M+k\log 2-\frac{k}{2}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(\log M+k\log 2-\frac{k}{2}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\Big(\log M-\frac{k}{4}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\Big)\qquad\text{(for $d\geq 8k$)}

\displaystyle<1\,,

if we take
M
such that

\log M<\frac{k}{4}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k}).\qed

Apply the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert lemma to obtain
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
with
\log(M)\geq\frac{k}{8}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})
and such that

\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq k/2
for all
j\neq k
. Let
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{M}
be such that

\theta_{j}=\omega_{j}\frac{\beta\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})}\,,

for some
\beta>0
to be chosen later. We can check the conditions of Theorem
4.11
:

(i)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\geq 4\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{8n}k\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})
;

(ii)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\leq\frac{2k\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\leq\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(M)
,

for
\beta=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha}{8}}
. Applying now Theorem
4.11
yields

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\\
|\theta|_{0}\leq k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{64n}k\log(1+\frac{d}{2k})\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha\,.

It implies the following corollary.

Corollary 4.15
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
denotes the set of all
k
-sparse vectors of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
.
The minimax rate of estimation over
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
in the Gaussian sequence model is
\phi(\mathcal{B}_{0}(k))=\frac{\sigma^{2}k}{n}\log(ed/k)
. Moreover, it is attained by the constrained least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)}
.

Note that the modified BIC estimator of Problem
2.7
and the Slope (
2.26
) are also minimax optimal over
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
.
However, unlike
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)}
and the modified BIC, the Slope is also adaptive to
k
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
, the Lasso estimator and the BIC estimator are minimax optimal for sets of parameters such that
k\leq d^{1-\varepsilon}
.

19.3
Lower bounds for estimating vectors in
\ell_{1}
balls

Recall that in Maurey’s argument, we approximated a vector
\theta
such that
|\theta|_{1}=R
by a vector
\theta^{\prime}
such that
|\theta^{\prime}|_{0}=\frac{R}{\sigma}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log d}}
. We can essentially do the same for the lower bound.

Assume that
d\geq\sqrt{n}
and let
\beta\in(0,1)
be a parameter to be chosen later and define
k
to be the smallest integer such that

k\geq\frac{R}{\beta\sigma}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\log(ed/\sqrt{n})}}\,.

Let
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}
be obtained from the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
4.14
with this choice of
k
and define

\theta_{j}=\omega_{j}\frac{R}{k}\,.

Observe that
|\theta_{j}|_{1}=R
for
j=1,\ldots,M
.
We can check the conditions of Theorem
4.11
:

(i)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{R^{2}}{k^{2}}\rho(\omega_{j},\omega_{k})\geq\frac{R^{2}}{2k}\geq 4R\min\big(\frac{R}{8},\beta^{2}\sigma\frac{\log(ed/\sqrt{n})}{8n}\big)
.

(ii)

\displaystyle|\theta_{j}-\theta_{k}|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2R^{2}}{k}\leq 4R\beta\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(ed/\sqrt{n})}{n}}\leq\frac{2\alpha\sigma^{2}}{n}\log(M)
,

for
\beta
small enough if
d\geq Ck
for some constant
C>0
chosen large enough. Applying now Theorem
4.11
yields

\inf_{\hat{\theta}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\theta}\big(|\hat{\theta}-\theta|_{2}^{2}\geq R\min\big(\frac{R}{8},\beta^{2}\sigma^{2}\frac{\log(ed/\sqrt{n})}{8n}\big)\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha\,.

It implies the following corollary.

Corollary 4.16
.

Recall that
\mathcal{B}_{1}(R)\subset\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
denotes the set vectors
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
such that
|\theta|_{1}\leq R
. Then there exist a constant
C>0
such that if
d\geq n^{1/2+\varepsilon}
,
\varepsilon>0
, the minimax rate of estimation over
\mathcal{B}_{1}(R)
in the Gaussian sequence model is

\phi(\mathcal{B}_{0}(k))=\min(R^{2},R\sigma\frac{\log d}{n})\,.

Moreover, it is attained by the constrained least squares estimator
\hat{\theta}^{\textsc{ls}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}(R)}
if
R\geq\sigma\frac{\log d}{n}
and by the trivial estimator
\hat{\theta}=0
otherwise.

Proof.

To complete the proof of the statement, we need to study the risk of the trivial estimator equal to zero for small
R
. Note that if
|\theta^{*}|_{1}\leq R
, we have

|0-\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}=|\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq|\theta^{*}|_{1}^{2}=R^{2}\,.\qed

Remark 4.17
.

Note that the inequality
|\theta^{*}|_{2}^{2}\leq|\theta^{*}|_{1}^{2}
appears to be quite loose. Nevertheless, it is tight up to a multiplicative constant for the vectors of the form
\theta_{j}=\omega_{j}\frac{R}{k}
that are employed in the lower bound. Indeed, if
R\leq\sigma\frac{\log d}{n}
, we have
k\leq 2/\beta

|\theta_{j}|_{2}^{2}=\frac{R^{2}}{k}\geq\frac{\beta}{2}|\theta_{j}|_{1}^{2}\,.

20
Lower bounds for sparse estimation via
\chi^{2}
divergence

In this section, we will show how to derive lower bounds by directly controlling the distance between a simple and a composite hypothesis, which is useful when investigating lower rates for decision problems instead of estimation problems.

Define the
\chi^{2}
divergence between two probability distributions
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q}
as

\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})=\left\{\begin{aligned} \int\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}{d\mathbb{Q}}-1\right)^{2}d\mathbb{Q}\quad&\text{if }\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\ll\mathbb{Q},\\
\infty\quad&\text{otherwise.}\end{aligned}\right.

When we compare the expression in the case where
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
, then we see that both the KL divergence and the
\chi^{2}
divergence can be written as
\int f\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{P}}{d\mathbb{Q}}\right)d\mathbb{Q}
with
f(x)=x\log x
and
f(x)=(x-1)^{2}
, respectively.
Also note that both of these functions are convex in
x
and fulfill
f(1)=0
, which by Jensen’s inequality shows us that they are non-negative and equal to
0
if and only if
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}=\mathbb{Q}
.

Firstly, let us derive another useful expression for the
\chi^{2}
divergence.
By expanding the square, we have

\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})=\int\frac{(d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P})^{2}}{d\mathbb{Q}}-2\int d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}+\int d\mathbb{Q}=\int\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}{d\mathbb{Q}}\right)^{2}d\mathbb{Q}-1

Secondly, we note that we can bound the KL divergence from above by the
\chi^{2}
divergence, via Jensen’s inequality, as

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q})=\int\log\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}{d\mathbb{Q}}\right)d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\leq\log\int\frac{(d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P})^{2}}{d\mathbb{Q}}=\log(1+\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q}))\leq\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P},\mathbb{Q}),

(4.8)

where we used the concavity inequality
\log(1+x)\leq x
for
x>-1
.

Note that this estimate is a priori very rough and that we are transitioning from something at
\log
scale to something on a regular scale.
However, since we are mostly interested in showing that these distances are bounded by a small constant after adjusting the parameters of our models appropriately, it will suffice for our purposes.
In particular, we can combine (
4.8
) with the Neyman-Pearson Lemma
4.3
and Pinsker’s inequality, Lemma
4.8
, to get

\inf_{\psi}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}(\psi=0)\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon})

for distinguishing between two hypothesis with a decision rule
\psi
if
\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1})\leq\varepsilon
.

In the following, we want to apply this observation to derive lower rates for distinguishing between

H_{0}:Y\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}\quad H_{1}:Y\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{u},\text{ for some }u\in B_{0}(k),

where
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{u}
denotes the probability distribution of a Gaussian sequence model
Y=u+\frac{\sigma}{n}\xi
, with
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Consider the detection problem

H_{0}:\theta^{\ast}=0,\quad H_{v}:\theta^{\ast}=\mu v,\;\text{for some }v\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(k),\,\|v\|_{2}=1,

with data given by
Y=\theta^{\ast}+\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\xi
,
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.

There exist
\varepsilon>0
and
c_{\varepsilon}>0
such that if

\mu\leq\frac{\sigma}{2}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\sqrt{\log\left(1+\frac{d\varepsilon}{k^{2}}\right)}

then,

\inf_{\psi}\{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)\vee\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)\\
\|v\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v}(\psi=0)\}\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}).

Proof.

We introduce the mixture hypothesis

\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}=\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subseteq[d]\\
|S|=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S},

with
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S}
being the distribution of
Y=\mu\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}_{S}/\sqrt{k}+\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}\xi
and give lower bounds on distinguishing

H_{0}:Y\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0},\quad H_{1}:Y\sim\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}},

by computing their
\chi^{2}
distance,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})={}

\displaystyle\int\left(\frac{d\bar{p}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\right)^{2}d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{S,T}\int\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{T}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\right)d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}-1.

The first step is to compute
d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S}/(d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})
.
Writing out the corresponding Gaussian densities yields

\displaystyle\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}(X)={}

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}^{d}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2\sigma^{2}}\|X-\mu\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}_{S}/\sqrt{k}\|_{2}^{2})}{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}^{d}}\exp(-\frac{n}{2\sigma^{2}}\|X-0\|_{2}^{2})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{n}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(\frac{2\mu}{\sqrt{k}}\langle X,\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}_{S}\rangle-\mu^{2}\right)\right)

For convenience, we introduce the notation
X=\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}}Z
for a standard normal
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
,
\nu=\mu\sqrt{n}/(\sigma\sqrt{k})
, and write
Z_{S}
for the restriction to the coordinates in the set
S
.
Multiplying two of these densities and integrating with respect to
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
in turn then reduces to computing the mgf of a Gaussian and gives

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{X\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\left[\exp\left(\frac{n}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(2\mu\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{kn}}(Z_{S}+Z_{T})-2\mu^{2}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})}\exp\left(\nu(Z_{S}+Z_{T})-\nu^{2}k\right).

Decomposing
Z_{S}+Z_{T}=2\sum_{i\in S\cap T}Z_{i}+\sum_{i\in S\Delta T}Z_{i}
and noting that
|S\Delta T|\leq 2k
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\left(\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\frac{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{T}}{d\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}}\right)={}

\displaystyle\exp\left(\frac{4}{2}\nu^{2}|S\cap T+\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}|S\Delta T|-\nu^{2}k\right)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\exp\left(2\nu^{2}|S\cap T|\right).

Now, we need to take the expectation over two uniform draws of support sets
S
and
T
, which reduces via conditioning and exploiting the independence of the two draws to

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{S,T}[\exp(2\nu^{2}|S\cap T|]-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{T}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{S}[[\exp(2\nu^{2}|S\cap T|\,|\,T]]-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{S}[\exp(2\nu^{2}|S\cap[k]|)]-1.

Similar to the proof of the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert bound, Lemma
4.14
, the distribution of
|S\cap[k]|
is stochastically dominated by a binomial distribution
\mathsf{Bin}(k,k/d)
, so that

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})\leq{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\exp(2\nu^{2}\mathsf{Bin}(k,\frac{k}{d})]-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\exp(2\nu^{2}\mathsf{Ber}(k/d))]\right)^{k}-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{e}^{2\nu^{2}}\frac{k}{d}+\left(1-\frac{k}{d}\right)\right)^{k}-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{k}{d}(\mathrm{e}^{2\nu^{2}}-1)\right)^{k}-1

Since
(1+x)^{k}-1\approx kx
for
x=o(1/k)
, we can take
\nu^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\log(1+\frac{d}{k^{2}}\varepsilon)
to get a bound of the order
\varepsilon
.
Plugging this back into the definition of
\nu
yields

\mu=\frac{\sigma}{2}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}\sqrt{\log\left(1+\frac{d\varepsilon}{k^{2}}\right)}.

The rate for detection for arbitrary
v
now follows from

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)\vee\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)\\
\|v\|_{2}=1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v}(\psi=0)\geq{}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}(\psi=1)\vee\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}(\psi=0)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(1-\sqrt{\varepsilon}).\qed

Remark 4.19
.

If instead of the
\chi^{2}
divergence, we try to compare the
\mathsf{KL}
divergence between
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}}
, we are tempted to exploit its convexity properties to get

\mathsf{KL}(\bar{\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})\leq\frac{1}{\binom{d}{k}}\sum_{S}\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{S},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0})=\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{[k]},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}),

which is just the distance between two Gaussian distributions.
In turn, we do not see any increase in complexity brought about by increasing the number of competing hypotheses as we did in Section
19
.
By using the convexity estimate, we have lost the granularity of controlling how much the different probability distributions overlap.
This is where the
\chi^{2}
divergence helped.

Remark 4.20
.

This rate for detection implies lower rates for estimation of both
\theta^{\ast}
and
\|\theta^{\ast}\|_{2}
:
If given an estimator
\widehat{T}(X)
for
T(\theta)=\|\theta\|_{2}
that achieves an error less than
\mu/2
for some
X
, then we would get a correct decision rule for those
X
by setting

\psi(X)=\left\{\begin{aligned} 0,&\quad\text{if }\widehat{T}(X)<\frac{\mu}{2},\\
1,&\quad\text{if }\widehat{T}(X)\geq\frac{\mu}{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

Conversely, the lower bound above means that such an error rate can not be achieved with vanishing error probability for
\mu
smaller than the critical value.
Moreover, by the triangle inequality, we can the same lower bound also for estimation of
\theta^{\ast}
itself.

We note that the rate that we obtained is slightly worse in the scaling of the log factor (
d/k^{2}
instead of
d/k
) than the one obtained in Corollary
4.15
.
This is because it applies to estimating the
\ell_{2}
norm as well, which is a strictly easier problem and for which estimators can be constructed that achieve the faster rate of
\frac{k}{n}\log(d/k^{2})
.

21
Lower bounds for estimating the
\ell_{1}
norm via moment matching

So far, we have seen many examples of rates that scale with
n^{-1}
for the squared error.
In this section, we are going to see an example for estimating a functional that can only be estimated at a much slower rate of
1/\log n
, following
[
CL11
]
.

Consider the normalized
\ell_{1}
norm,

T(\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\theta_{i}|,

(4.9)

as the target functional to be estimated, and measurements

Y\sim N(\theta,I_{n}).

(4.10)

We are going to show lower bounds for the estimation if restricted to an
\ell_{\infty}
ball,
\Theta_{n}(M)=\{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}:|\theta_{i}|\leq M\}
, and for a general
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
.

21.1
A constrained risk inequality

We start with a general principle that is in the same spirit as the Neyman-Pearson lemma, (Lemma
4.3
), but deals with expectations rather than probabilities and uses the
\chi^{2}
distance to estimate the closeness of distributions.
Moreover, contrary to what we have seen so far, it allows us to compare two mixture distributions over candidate hypotheses, also known as priors.

In the following, we write
X
for a random observation coming from a distribution indexed by
\theta\in\Theta
,
\widehat{T}=\widehat{T}(X)
an estimator for a function
T(\theta)
, and write
B(\theta)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}\widehat{T}-T(\theta)
for the bias of
\widehat{T}
.

Let
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
be two prior distributions over
\Theta
and denote by
m_{i}
,
v_{i}^{2}
the means and variance of
T(\theta)
under the priors
\mu_{i}
,
i\in\{0,1\}
,

m_{i}=\int T(\theta)d\mu_{i}(\theta),\quad\int v_{i}^{2}=\int(T(\theta)-m_{i})^{2}d\mu_{i}(\theta).

Moreover, write
F_{i}
for the marginal distributions over the priors and denote their density with respect to the average of the two probability distributions by
f_{i}
.
With this, we write the
\chi^{2}
distance between
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
as

I^{2}=\chi^{2}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{f_{0}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{f_{1}})=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}-1\right)^{2}=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}\right)^{2}-1.

Theorem 4.21
.

(i) If
\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)\leq\varepsilon^{2}
,

\left|\int B(\theta)d\mu_{1}(\theta)-\int B(\theta)d\mu_{0}(\theta)\right|\geq|m_{1}-m_{0}|-(\varepsilon+v_{0})I.

(4.11)

(ii) If
|m_{1}-m_{0}|>v_{0}I
and
0\leq\lambda\leq 1
,

\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}d(\lambda\mu_{0}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{1})(\theta)\geq\frac{\lambda(1-\lambda)(|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I)^{2}}{\lambda+(1-\lambda)(I+1)^{2}},

(4.12)

in particular

\max_{i\in\{0,1\}}\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}d\mu_{i}(\theta)\geq\frac{(|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I)^{2}}{(I+2)^{2}},

(4.13)

and

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}\geq\frac{(|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I)^{2}}{(I+2)^{2}}.

(4.14)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume
m_{1}\geq m_{0}
.
We start by considering the term

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}\left[(\widehat{T}(X)-m_{0})\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)-f_{0}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\left[\widehat{T}(X)\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)-f_{0}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{1}}\left[m_{1}+\widehat{T}(X)-m_{1}\right]-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}\left[m_{0}+\widehat{T}(X)-m_{0}\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle m_{1}+\int B(\theta)d\mu_{1}(\theta)-\left(m_{0}+\int B(\theta)d\mu_{0}(\theta)\right).

Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}(\widehat{T}(X)-m_{0})^{2}={}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-m_{0})^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta)+T(\theta)-m_{0})^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle+2\int B(\theta)(T(\theta)-m_{0})d\mu_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle+\int(T(\theta)-m_{0})^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon v_{0}+v_{0}^{2}=(\varepsilon+v_{0})^{2}.

Therefore, by Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{f_{0}}\left[(\widehat{T}(X)-m_{0})\left(\frac{f_{1}(X)-f_{0}(X)}{f_{0}(X)}\right)\right]\leq{}

\displaystyle(\varepsilon+v_{0})I,

and hence

m_{1}+\int B(\theta)d\mu_{1}(\theta)-\left(m_{0}+\int B(\theta)d\mu_{0}(\theta)\right)\leq(\varepsilon+v_{0})I,

whence

\int B(\theta)d\mu_{0}(\theta)-\int B(\theta)d\mu_{1}(\theta)\geq m_{1}-m_{0}-(\varepsilon+v_{0})I,

which gives us (
4.11
).

To estimate the risk under a mixture of
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
, we note that from (
4.11
), we have an estimate for the risk under
\mu_{1}
given an upper bound on the risk under
\mu_{0}
, which means we can reduce this problem to estimating a quadratic from below.
Consider

J(x)=\lambda x^{2}+(1-\lambda)(a-bx)^{2},

with
0<\lambda<1
,
a>0
and
b>0
.

J
is minimized by
x=x_{\mathrm{min}}=\frac{ab(1-\lambda)}{\lambda+b^{2}(1-\lambda)}
with
a-bx_{\mathrm{min}}>0
and
J(x_{\mathrm{min}})=\frac{a^{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)}{\lambda+b^{2}(1-\lambda)}
.
Hence, if we add a cut-off at zero in the second term, we do not change the minimal value and obtain that

\lambda x^{2}+(1-\lambda)((a-bx)_{+})^{2}

has the same minimum.

From (
4.11
) and Jensen’s inequality, setting
\varepsilon^{2}=\int B(\theta)^{2}d\mu_{0}(\theta)
, we get

\displaystyle\int B(\theta)^{2}d\mu_{1}(\theta)\geq{}

\displaystyle\left(\int B(\theta)d\mu_{1}(\theta)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle((m_{1}-m_{0}-(\varepsilon+v_{0})I-\varepsilon)_{+})^{2}.

Combining this with the estimate for the quadratic above yields

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\int\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}d(\lambda\mu_{0}+(1-\lambda)\mu_{1})(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\lambda\varepsilon^{2}+(1-\lambda)((m_{1}-m_{0}-(\varepsilon+v_{0})I-\varepsilon)_{+})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda(1-\lambda)(|m_{1}-m_{0}|-v_{0}I)^{2}}{\lambda+(1-\lambda)(I+1)^{2}},

which is (
4.12
).

Finally, since the minimax risk is bigger than any mixture risk, we can bound it from below by the maximum value of this bound, which is obtained at
\lambda=(I+1)/(I+2)
to get (
4.13
), and by the same argument (
4.14
).
∎

21.2
Unfavorable priors via polynomial approximation

In order to construct unfavorable priors for the
\ell_{1}
norm (
4.9
), we resort to polynomial approximation theory.
For a continuous function
f
on
[-1,1]
, denote the maximal approximation error by polynomials of degree
k
, written as
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, by

\delta_{k}(f)=\inf_{G\in\mathcal{P}_{k}}\max_{x\in[-1,1]}|f(x)-G(x)|,

and the polynomial attaining this error by
G_{k}^{\ast}
.
For
f(t)=|t|
, it is known
[
Riv90
]
that

\beta_{\ast}=\lim_{k\to\infty}2k\delta_{2k}(f)\in(0,\infty),

which is a very slow rate of convergence of the approximation, compared to smooth functions for which we would expect geometric convergence.

We can now construct our priors using some abstract measure theoretical results.

Lemma 4.22
.

For an integer
k>0
, there are two probability measures
\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
on
[-1,1]
that fulfill the following:

1.

\nu_{0}
and
\nu_{1}
are symmetric about 0;

2.

\int t^{l}d\nu_{1}(t)=\int t^{l}d\nu_{0}(t)
for all
l\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
;

3.

\int|t|d\nu_{1}(t)-\int|t|d\nu_{0}(t)=2\delta_{k}
.

Proof.

The idea is to construct the measures via the Hahn-Banach theorem and the Riesz representation theorem.

First, consider
f(t)=|t|
as an element of the space
C([-1,1])
equipped with the supremum norm and define
\mathcal{P}_{k}
as the space of polynomials of degree up to
k
, and
\mathcal{F}_{k}:=\operatorname{span}(\mathcal{P}_{k}\cup\{f\})
.
On
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, define the functional
T(cf+p_{k})=c\delta_{k}
, which is well-defined because
f
is not a polynomial.

Claim:

\|T\|=\sup\{T(g):g\in\mathcal{F}_{k},\|g\|_{\infty}\leq 1\}=1
.

\|T\|\geq 1
: Let
G_{k}^{\ast}
be the best-approximating polynomial to
f
in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
Then,
\|f-G_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\infty}=\delta_{k}
,
\|(f-G^{\ast}_{k})/\delta_{k}\|_{\infty}=1
, and
T((f-G^{\ast}_{k})/\delta_{k})=1
.

\|T\|\leq 1
: Suppose
g=cf+p_{k}
with
p_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
,
\|g\|_{\infty}=1
and
T(g)>1
.
Then
c>1/\delta_{k}
and

\|f-(-p_{k}/c)\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{c}<\delta_{k},

contradicting the definition of
\delta_{k}
.

Now, by the Hahn-Banach theorem, there is a norm-preserving extension of
T
to
C([-1,1])
which we again denote by
T
.
By the Riesz representation theorem, there is a Borel signed measure
\tau
with variation equal to
1
such that

T(g)=\int_{-1}^{1}g(t)d\tau(t),\quad\text{for all }g\in C([-1,1]).

The Hahn-Jordan decomposition gives two positive measures
\tau_{+}
and
\tau_{-}
such that
\tau=\tau_{+}-\tau_{-}
and

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}|t|d(\tau_{+}-\tau_{-})(t)={}

\displaystyle\delta_{k}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}t^{l}d\tau_{+}(t)={}

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}t^{l}d\tau_{-}(t),\quad l\in\{0,\dots,k\}.

Since
f
is a symmetric function, we can symmetrize these measures and hence can assume that they are symmetric about
0
.
Finally, to get a probability measures with the desired properties, set
\nu_{1}=2\tau_{+}
and
\nu_{0}=2\tau_{-}
.
∎

21.3
Minimax lower bounds

Theorem 4.23
.

We have the following bounds on the minimax rate for
T(\theta)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\theta_{i}|
:
For
\theta\in\Theta_{n}(M)=\{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}:|\theta_{i}|\leq M\}
,

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}(M)}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}\geq\beta_{\ast}^{2}M^{2}\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right)^{2}(1+o(1)).

(4.15)

For
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
,

\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{n}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\theta}(\widehat{T}(X)-T(\theta))^{2}\geq\frac{\beta_{\ast}^{2}}{16e^{2}\log n}(1+o(1)).

(4.16)

The last remaining ingredient for the proof are the
Hermite polynomials
, a family of orthogonal polynomials with respect to the Gaussian density

\varphi(y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\mathrm{e}^{-y^{2}/2}.

For our purposes, it is enough to define them by the derivatives of the density,

\frac{d^{k}}{dy^{k}}\varphi(y)=(-1)^{k}H_{k}(y)\varphi(y),

and to observe that they are orthogonal,

\int H_{k}^{2}(y)\varphi(y)dy=k!,\quad\int H_{k}(y)H_{j}(y)\varphi(y)dy=0,\quad\text{for }k\neq j.

Proof.

We want to use Theorem
4.21
.
To construct the prior measures, we scale the measures from Lemma
4.22
appropriately:
Let
k_{n}
be an even integer that is to be determined, and
\nu_{0}
,
\nu_{1}
the two measures given by Lemma
4.22
.
Define
g(x)=Mx
and define the measures
\mu_{i}
by dilating
\nu_{i}
,
\mu_{i}(A)=\nu_{i}(g^{-1}(A))
for every Borel set
A\subseteq[-M,M]
and
i\in\{0,1\}
.
Hence,

1.

\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
are symmetric about 0;

2.

\int t^{l}d\mu_{1}(t)=\int t^{l}d\mu_{0}(t)
for all
l\in\{0,1,\dots,k_{n}\}
;

3.

\int|t|d\mu_{1}(t)-\int|t|d\mu_{0}(t)=2M\delta_{k_{n}}
.

To get priors for
n
i.i.d. samples, consider the product priors
\mu_{i}^{n}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{i}
.
With this, we have

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{1}^{n}}T(\theta)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}^{n}}T(\theta)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{1}}|\theta_{1}|-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}}|\theta_{1}|=2M\delta_{k_{n}},

and

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}^{n}}(T(\theta)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}^{n}}T(\theta))^{2}=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}^{n}}(T(\theta)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\mu_{0}^{n}}T(\theta))^{2}\leq\frac{M^{2}}{n},

since each
\theta_{i}\in[-M,M]
.

It remains to control the
\chi^{2}
distance between the two marginal distributions.
To this end, we will expand the Gaussian density in terms of Hermite polynomials.
First, set
f_{i}(y)=\int\varphi(y-t)d\mu_{i}(t)
.
Since
g(x)=\exp(-x)
is convex and
\mu_{0}
is symmetric,

\displaystyle f_{0}(y)\geq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp\left(-\int\frac{(y-t)^{2}}{2}d\mu_{0}(t)\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\varphi(y)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}M^{2}\int t^{2}d\nu_{0}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\varphi(y)\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}M^{2}\right).

Expand
\varphi
as

\varphi(y-t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}H_{k}(y)\varphi(y)\frac{t^{k}}{k!}.

Then,

\int\frac{(f_{1}(y)-f_{0}(y))^{2}}{f_{0}(y)}dy\leq\mathrm{e}^{M^{2}/2}\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}M^{2k}.

The
\chi^{2}
distance for
n
i.i.d. observations can now be easily bounded by

\displaystyle I_{n}^{2}={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{(\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{1}(y_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(y_{i}))^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(y_{i})}dy_{1}\dotsi dy_{n}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int\frac{(\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{1}(y_{i}))^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}f_{0}(y_{i})}dy_{1}\dotsi dy_{n}-1

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\int\frac{(f_{1}(y_{i}))^{2}}{f_{0}(y_{i})}dy_{i}\right)-1

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{M^{2}/2}\sum_{k=k_{n}+1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}M^{2k}\right)^{n}-1

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{3M^{2}/2}\frac{1}{k_{n}!}M^{2k_{n}}\right)^{n}-1

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left(1+\mathrm{e}^{3M^{2}/2}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}M^{2}}{k_{n}}\right)^{k_{n}}\right)^{n}-1,

(4.17)

where the last step used a Stirling type estimate,
k!>(k/e)^{k}
.

Note that if
x=o(1/n)
, then
(1+x)^{n}-1=o(nx)
by Taylor’s formula, so we can choose
k_{n}\geq\log n/(\log\log n)
to guarantee that for
n
large enough,
I_{n}\to 0
.
With Theorem
4.21
, we have

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{T}}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}(M)}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}(\widehat{T}-T(\theta))^{2}\geq{}

\displaystyle\frac{(2M\delta_{k_{n}}-(M/\sqrt{n})I_{n})^{2}}{(I_{n}+2)^{2}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\beta_{\ast}^{2}M^{2}\left(\frac{\log\log n}{\log n}\right)^{2}(1+o(1)).

To prove the lower bound over
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
, take
M=\sqrt{\log n}
and
k_{n}
to be the smallest integer such that
k_{n}\geq 2\mathrm{e}\log n
and plug this into (
4.17
), yielding

\displaystyle I_{n}^{2}\leq\left(1+n^{3/2}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}\log n}{2\mathrm{e}\log n}\right)^{k_{n}}\right)^{n}-1,

which goes to zero.
Hence, we can conclude just as before.
∎

Note that
[
CL11
]
also complement these lower bounds with upper bounds that are based on polynomial approximation, completing the picture of estimating
T(\theta)
.

22
Problem Set

Problem 4.1
.

(a)

Prove the statement of Example
4.7
.

(b)

Let
P_{\theta}
denote the distribution of
X\sim\mathsf{Ber}(\theta)
. Show that

\mathsf{KL}(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{\prime}})\geq C(\theta-\theta^{\prime})^{2}\,.

Problem 4.2
.

Let
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{0}
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{1}
be two probability measures. Prove that for any test
\psi
, it holds

\max_{j=0,1}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{j}(\psi\neq j)\geq\frac{1}{4}e^{-\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{P}_{0},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{P}_{1})}\,.

Problem 4.3
.

For any
R>0
,
\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
, denote by
\mathcal{B}_{2}(\theta,R)
the (Euclidean) ball of radius
R
and centered at
\theta
. For any
\varepsilon>0
let
N=N(\varepsilon)
be the largest integer such that there exist
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{N}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(0,1)
for which

|\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}|\geq 2\varepsilon

for all
i\neq j
.
We call the set
\{\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{N}\}
an
\varepsilon
-packing of
\mathcal{B}_{2}(0,1)
.

(a)

Show that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
N\leq C_{d}/\varepsilon^{d}
.

(b)

Show that for any
x\in\mathcal{B}_{2}(0,1)
, there exists
i=1,\ldots,N
such that
|x-\theta_{i}|_{2}\leq 2\varepsilon
.

(c)

Use (b) to conclude that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that
N\geq C^{\prime}_{d}/\varepsilon^{d}
.

Problem 4.4
.

Show that the rate
\phi=\sigma^{2}d/n
is the minimax rate of estimation over:

(a)

The Euclidean Ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
with radius
\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d/n}
.

(b)

The unit
\ell_{\infty}
ball of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
defined by

\mathcal{B}_{\infty}(1)=\{\theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}\,:\,\max_{j}|\theta_{j}|\leq 1\}

as long as
\sigma^{2}\leq n
.

(c)

The set of nonnegative vectors of
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
.

(d)

The discrete hypercube
\frac{\sigma}{16\sqrt{n}}\{0,1\}^{d}
.

Problem 4.5
.

Fix
\beta\geq 5/3,Q>0
and prove that the minimax rate of estimation over
\Theta(\beta,Q)
with the
\|\cdot\|_{L_{2}([0,1])}
-norm is given by
n^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}
.

[Hint:Consider functions of the form

f_{j}=\frac{C}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\omega_{ji}\varphi_{i}

where
C
is a constant,
\omega_{j}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
for some appropriately chosen
N
and
\{\varphi_{j}\}_{j\geq 1}
is the trigonometric basis.]

Chapter 5
Matrix estimation

Over the past decade or so, matrices have entered the picture of high-dimensional statistics for several reasons. Perhaps the simplest explanation is that they are the most natural extension of vectors. While this is true, and we will see examples where the extension from vectors to matrices is straightforward, matrices have a much richer structure than vectors allowing “interaction” between their rows and columns. In particular, while we have been describing simple vectors in terms of their sparsity, here we can measure the complexity of a matrix by its
rank
. This feature was successfully employed in a variety of applications ranging from
multi-task learning
to
collaborative filtering
. This last application was made popular by the
Netflix
prize in particular.

In this chapter, we study several statistical problems where the parameter of interest
\theta
is a matrix rather than a vector. These problems include multivariate regression, covariance matrix estimation, and principal component analysis. Before getting to these topics, we begin with a quick reminder on matrices and linear algebra.

23
Basic facts about matrices

Matrices are much more complicated objects than vectors. In particular, while vectors can be identified with linear operators from
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
to
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
, matrices can be identified to linear operators from
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
to
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
for
n\geq 1
. This seemingly simple fact gives rise to a profusion of notions and properties as illustrated by Bernstein’s book
[
Ber09
]
that contains facts about matrices over more than a thousand pages. Fortunately, we will be needing only a small number of such properties, which can be found in the excellent book
[
GVL96
]
, which has become a standard reference on matrices and numerical linear algebra.

23.1
Singular value decomposition

Let
A=\{a_{ij},1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n\}
be a
m\times n
real matrix of rank
r\leq\min(m,n)
. The
Singular Value Decomposition
(SVD) of
A
is given by

A=UDV^{\top}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}\,,

where
D
is an
r\times r
diagonal matrix with positive diagonal entries
\{\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r}\}
,
U
is a matrix with columns
\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{m}
that are orthonormal, and
V
is a matrix with columns
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{r}\}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
that are also orthonormal. Moreover, it holds that

AA^{\top}u_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{2}u_{j}\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad A^{\top}Av_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{2}v_{j}

for
j=1,\ldots,r
. The values
\lambda_{j}>0
are called
singular values
of
A
and are uniquely defined. If rank
r<\min(n,m)
then the singular values of
A
are given by
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r},0,\ldots,0)^{\top}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{\min(n,m)}
where there are
\min(n,m)-r
zeros. This way, the vector
\lambda
of singular values of a
n\times m
matrix is a vector in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{\min(n,m)}
.

In particular, if
A
is an
n\times n
symmetric positive semidefinite (PSD), i.e.
A^{\top}=A
and
u^{\top}Au\geq 0
for all
u\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
, then the singular values of
A
are equal to its eigenvalues.

The largest singular value of
A
denoted by
\lambda_{\max{}}(A)
also satisfies the following variational formulation:

\lambda_{\max{}}(A)=\max_{x\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{n}}\frac{|Ax|_{2}}{|x|_{2}}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{n}\\
y\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{m}\end{subarray}}\frac{y^{\top}Ax}{|y|_{2}|x|_{2}}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}\\
y\in\mathcal{S}^{m-1}\end{subarray}}y^{\top}Ax\,.

In the case of a
n\times n
PSD matrix
A
, we have

\lambda_{\max{}}(A)=\max_{x\in\mathcal{S}^{n-1}}x^{\top}Ax\,.

In these notes, we always assume that eigenvectors and singular vectors have unit norm.

23.2
Norms and inner product

Let
A=\{a_{ij}\}
and
B=\{b_{ij}\}
be two real matrices. Their size will be implicit in the following notation.

23.2.1
Vector norms

The simplest way to treat a matrix is to deal with it as if it were a vector. In particular, we can extend
\ell_{q}
norms to matrices:

|A|_{q}=\Big(\sum_{ij}|a_{ij}|^{q}\Big)^{1/q}\,,\quad q>0\,.

The cases where
q\in\{0,\infty\}
can also be extended matrices:

|A|_{0}=\sum_{ij}\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(a_{ij}\neq 0)\,,\qquad|A|_{\infty}=\max_{ij}|a_{ij}|\,.

The case
q=2
plays a particular role for matrices and
|A|_{2}
is called the
Frobenius
norm of
A
and is often denoted by
\|A\|_{F}
. It is also the Hilbert-Schmidt norm associated to the inner product:

\langle A,B\rangle=\mathsf{Tr}(A^{\top}B)=\mathsf{Tr}(B^{\top}A)\,.

23.2.2
Spectral norms

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{r},0,\ldots,0)
be the singular values of a matrix
A
. We can define spectral norms on
A
as vector norms on the vector
\lambda
. In particular, for any
q\in[1,\infty]
,

\|A\|_{q}=|\lambda|_{q}\,,

is called
Schatten
q
-norm
of
A
. Here again, special cases have special names:

•

q=2
:
\|A\|_{2}=\|A\|_{F}
is the Frobenius norm defined above.

•

q=1
:
\|A\|_{1}=\|A\|_{*}
is called the Nuclear norm (or trace norm) of
A
.

•

q=\infty
:
\|A\|_{\infty}=\lambda_{\max{}}(A)=\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is called the operator norm (or spectral norm) of
A
.

We are going to employ these norms to assess the proximity to our matrix of interest. While the interpretation of vector norms is clear by extension from the vector case, the meaning of “
\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}
is small” is not as transparent. The following subsection provides some inequalities (without proofs) that allow a better reading.

23.3
Useful matrix inequalities

Let
A
and
B
be two
m\times n
matrices with singular values
\lambda_{1}(A)\geq\lambda_{2}(A)\ldots\geq\lambda_{\min(m,n)}(A)
and
\lambda_{1}(B)\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{\min(m,n)}(B)
respectively. Then the following inequalities hold:

\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle\max_{k}\big|\lambda_{k}(A)-\lambda_{k}(B)\big|\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,,&\text{Weyl (1912)}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{k}\big|\lambda_{k}(A)-\lambda_{k}(B)\big|^{2}\leq\|A-B\|_{F}^{2}\,,&\text{Hoffman-Weilandt (1953)}\\
\displaystyle\langle A,B\rangle\leq\|A\|_{p}\|B\|_{q}\,,\ \frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1,p,q\in[1,\infty]\,,&\text{H\"{o}lder}\end{array}

The singular value decomposition is associated to the following useful lemma, known as the
Eckart-Young (or Eckart-Young-Mirsky) Theorem
. It states that for any given
k
the closest matrix of rank
k
to a given matrix
A
in Frobenius norm is given by its truncated SVD.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
A
be a rank-
r
matrix with singular value decomposition

A=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}u_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\,,

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{r}>0
are the ordered singular values of
A
.
For any
k<r
, let
A_{k}
to be the truncated singular value decomposition of
A
given by

A_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}u_{i}v_{i}^{\top}\,.

Then for any matrix
B
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B)\leq k
, it holds

\|A-A_{k}\|_{F}\leq\|A-B\|_{F}\,.

Moreover,

\|A-A_{k}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{j=k+1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{2}\,.

Proof.

Note that the last equality of the lemma is obvious since

A-A_{k}=\sum_{j=k+1}^{r}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}

and the matrices in the sum above are orthogonal to each other.

Thus, it is sufficient to prove that for any matrix
B
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B)\leq k
, it holds

\|A-B\|_{F}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=k+1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{2}\,.

To that end, denote by
\sigma_{1}\geq\sigma_{2}\geq\dots\geq\sigma_{k}\geq 0
the ordered singular values of
B
—some may be equal to zero if
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B)<k
—and observe that it follows from the Hoffman-Weilandt inequality that

\|A-B\|_{F}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{r}\big(\lambda_{j}-\sigma_{j}\big)^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\big(\lambda_{j}-\sigma_{j}\big)^{2}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=k+1}^{r}\lambda_{j}^{2}\,.\qed

24
Multivariate regression

In the traditional regression setup, the response variable
Y
is a scalar. In several applications, the goal is not to predict a variable but rather a vector
Y\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{T}
, still from a covariate
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
. A standard example arises in genomics data where
Y
contains
T
physical measurements of a patient and
X
contains the expression levels for
d
genes. As a result the regression function in this case
f(x)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y|X=x]
is a function from
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
to
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{T}
. Clearly,
f
can be estimated independently for each coordinate, using the tools that we have developed in the previous chapter. However, we will see that in several interesting scenarios, some structure is shared across coordinates and this information can be leveraged to yield better prediction bounds.

24.1
The model

Throughout this section, we consider the following multivariate linear regression model:

\mathbb{Y}=\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}+E\,,

(5.1)

where
\mathbb{Y}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times T}
is the matrix of observed responses,
\mathbb{X}
is the
n\times d
observed design matrix (as before),
\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}
is the matrix of unknown parameters and
E\sim\mathsf{subG}_{n\times T}(\sigma^{2})
is the noise matrix. In this chapter, we will focus on the prediction task, which consists in estimating
\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}
.

As mentioned in the foreword of this chapter, we can view this problem as
T
(univariate) linear regression problems
Y^{(j)}=\mathbb{X}\theta^{*,(j)}+\varepsilon^{(j)},j=1,\ldots,T
, where
Y^{(j)},\theta^{*,(j)}
and
\varepsilon^{(j)}
are the
j
th column of
\mathbb{Y},\Theta^{*}
and
E
respectively. In particular, an estimator for
\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}
can be obtained by concatenating the estimators for each of the
T
problems. This approach is the subject of Problem
5.1
.

The columns of
\Theta^{*}
correspond to
T
different regression tasks. Consider the following example as a motivation. Assume that the
Subway
headquarters want to evaluate the effect of
d
variables (promotions, day of the week, TV ads,…) on their sales. To that end, they ask each of their
T=40,000
restaurants to report their sales numbers for the past
n=200
days. As a result, franchise
j
returns to headquarters a vector
\mathbb{Y}^{(j)}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n}
. The
d
variables for each of the
n
days are already known to headquarters and are stored in a matrix
\mathbb{X}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times d}
. In this case, it may be reasonable to assume that the same subset of variables has an impact of the sales for each of the franchises, though the magnitude of this impact may differ from franchise to franchise. As a result, one may assume that the matrix
\Theta^{*}
has each of its
T
columns that is row sparse and that they
share the same sparsity pattern
, i.e.,
\Theta^{*}
is of the form:

\Theta^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&0&0\\
\bullet&\bullet&\bullet&\bullet\\
\bullet&\bullet&\bullet&\bullet\\
0&0&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0\\
\bullet&\bullet&\bullet&\bullet\end{array}\right)\,,

where
\bullet
indicates a potentially nonzero entry.

It follows from the result of Problem
5.1
that if each task is performed individually, one may find an estimator
\hat{\Theta}
such that

\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\sigma^{2}\frac{kT\log(ed)}{n}\,,

where
k
is the number of nonzero coordinates in each column of
\Theta^{*}
. We remember that the term
\log(ed)
corresponds to the additional price to pay for not knowing where the nonzero components are. However, in this case, when the number of tasks grows, this should become easier. This fact was proved in
[
LPTVDG11
]
. We will see that we can recover a similar phenomenon when the number of tasks becomes large, though larger than in
[
LPTVDG11
]
. Indeed, rather than exploiting sparsity, observe that such a matrix
\Theta^{*}
has rank
k
. This is the kind of structure that we will be predominantly using in this chapter.

Rather than assuming that the columns of
\Theta^{*}
share the same sparsity pattern, it may be more appropriate to assume that the matrix
\Theta^{*}
is low rank or approximately so. As a result, while the matrix may not be sparse at all, the fact that it is low rank still materializes the idea that some structure is shared across different tasks. In this more general setup, it is assumed that the columns of
\Theta^{*}
live in a lower dimensional space. Going back to the
Subway
example this amounts to assuming that while there are 40,000 franchises, there are only a few canonical profiles for these franchises and that all franchises are linear combinations of these profiles.

24.2
Sub-Gaussian matrix model

Recall that under the assumption
ORT
for the design matrix, i.e.,
\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}=nI_{d}
, then the univariate regression model can be reduced to the sub-Gaussian sequence model. Here we investigate the effect of this assumption on the multivariate regression model (
5.1
).

Observe that under assumption
ORT
,

\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{Y}=\Theta^{*}+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\,.

Which can be written as an equation in
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}
called the
sub-Gaussian matrix model (sGMM)
:

y=\Theta^{*}+F\,,

(5.2)

where
y=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{Y}
and
F=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{X}^{\top}E\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d\times T}(\sigma^{2}/n)
.

Indeed, for any
u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1},v\in\mathcal{S}^{T-1}
, it holds

u^{\top}Fv=\frac{1}{n}(\mathbb{X}u)^{\top}Ev=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}w^{\top}Ev\sim\mathsf{subG}(\sigma^{2}/n)\,,

where
w=\mathbb{X}u/\sqrt{n}
has unit norm:
|w|_{2}^{2}=u^{\top}\frac{\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X}}{n}u=|u|_{2}^{2}=1
.

Akin to the sub-Gaussian sequence model, we have a
direct
observation model where we observe the parameter of interest with additive noise. This enables us to use thresholding methods for estimating
\Theta^{*}
when
|\Theta^{*}|_{0}
is small. However, this also follows from Problem
5.1
. The reduction to the vector case in the sGMM is just as straightforward. The interesting analysis begins when
\Theta^{*}
is low-rank, which is equivalent to sparsity in its unknown eigenbasis.

Consider the SVD of
\Theta^{*}
:

\Theta^{*}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}\,.

and recall that
\|\Theta^{*}\|_{0}=|\lambda|_{0}
. Therefore, if we knew
u_{j}
and
v_{j}
, we could simply estimate the
\lambda_{j}
s by hard thresholding. It turns out that estimating these eigenvectors by the eigenvectors of
y
is sufficient.

Consider the SVD of the observed matrix
y
:

y=\sum_{j}\hat{\lambda}_{j}\hat{u}_{j}\hat{v}_{j}^{\top}\,.

Definition 5.2
.

The
singular value thresholding
estimator with threshold
2\tau\geq 0
is defined by

\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}=\sum_{j}\hat{\lambda}_{j}\mathrm{1}\kern-2.40005pt\mathrm{I}(|\hat{\lambda}_{j}|>2\tau)\hat{u}_{j}\hat{v}_{j}^{\top}\,.

Recall that the threshold for the hard thresholding estimator was chosen to be the level of the noise with high probability. The singular value thresholding estimator obeys the same rule, except that the norm in which the magnitude of the noise is measured is adapted to the matrix case. Specifically, the following lemma will allow us to control the operator norm of the matrix
F
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
A
be a
d\times T
random matrix such that
A\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d\times T}(\sigma^{2})
. Then

\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 4\sigma\sqrt{\log(12)(d\vee T)}+2\sigma\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)}

with probability
1-\delta
.

Proof.

This proof follows the same steps as Problem
1.4
. Let
\mathcal{N}_{1}
be a
1/4
-net for
\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
and
\mathcal{N}_{2}
be a
1/4
-net for
\mathcal{S}^{T-1}
. It follows from Lemma
1.18
that we can always choose
|\mathcal{N}_{1}|\leq 12^{d}
and
|\mathcal{N}_{2}|\leq 12^{T}
. Moreover, for any
u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1},v\in\mathcal{S}^{T-1}
, it holds

\displaystyle u^{\top}Av

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}x^{\top}Av+\frac{1}{4}\max_{u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}}u^{\top}Av

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{2}}x^{\top}Ay+\frac{1}{4}\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{S}^{T-1}}x^{\top}Av+\frac{1}{4}\max_{u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}}u^{\top}Av

\displaystyle\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{2}}x^{\top}Ay+\frac{1}{2}\max_{u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}}\max_{v\in\mathcal{S}^{T-1}}u^{\top}Av

It yields

\|A\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq 2\max_{x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{N}_{2}}x^{\top}Ay

So that for any
t\geq 0
, by a union bound,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\|A\|_{\textrm{op}}>t\big)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\mathcal{N}_{1}\\
y\in\mathcal{N}_{2}\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(x^{\top}Ay>t/2\big)

Next, since
A\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d\times T}(\sigma^{2})
, it holds that
x^{\top}Ay\sim\mathsf{subG}(\sigma^{2})
for any
x\in\mathcal{N}_{1},y\in\mathcal{N}_{2}
.Together with the above display, it yields

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\|A\|_{\textrm{op}}>t\big)\leq 12^{d+T}\exp\big(-\frac{t^{2}}{8\sigma^{2}}\big)\leq\delta

for

t\geq 4\sigma\sqrt{\log(12)(d\vee T)}+2\sigma\sqrt{2\log(1/\delta)}\,.

∎

The following theorem holds.

Theorem 5.4
.

Consider the multivariate linear regression model (
5.1
) under the assumption
ORT
or, equivalently, the sub-Gaussian matrix model (
5.2
). Then, the singular value thresholding estimator
\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}
with threshold

2\tau=8\sigma\sqrt{\frac{\log(12)(d\vee T)}{n}}+4\sigma\sqrt{\frac{2\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\,,

(5.3)

satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 144\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\tau^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})}{n}\Big(d\vee T+\log(1/\delta)\Big)\,.

with probability
1-\delta
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that the singular values of
\Theta^{*}
and
y
are arranged in a non increasing order:
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
and
\hat{\lambda}_{1}\geq\hat{\lambda}_{2}\geq\dots
. Define the set
S=\{j\,:\,|\hat{\lambda}_{j}|>2\tau\}
.

Observe first that it follows from Lemma
5.3
that
\|F\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\tau
for
\tau
chosen as in (
5.3
) on an event
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(\mathcal{A})\geq 1-\delta
. The rest of the proof assumes that the event
\mathcal{A}
occurred.

Note that it follows from Weyl’s inequality that
|\hat{\lambda}_{j}-\lambda_{j}|\leq\|F\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\tau
. It implies that
S\subset\{j\,:\,|\lambda_{j}|>\tau\}
and
S^{c}\subset\{j\,:\,|\lambda_{j}|\leq 3\tau\}
.

Next define the oracle
\bar{\Theta}=\sum_{j\in S}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}
and note that

\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{F}^{2}+2\|\bar{\Theta}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}

(5.4)

Using the Hölder inequality, we control the first term as follows

\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{F}^{2}\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta})\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq 2|S|\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

Moreover,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-y\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|y-\Theta^{*}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\Theta^{*}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in S^{c}}|\hat{\lambda}_{j}|+\tau+\max_{j\in S^{c}}|\lambda_{j}|\leq 6\tau\,.

Therefore,

\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\bar{\Theta}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 72|S|\tau^{2}=72\sum_{j\in S}\tau^{2}\,.

The second term in (
5.4
) can be written as

\|\bar{\Theta}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}=\sum_{j\in S^{c}}|\lambda_{j}|^{2}\,.

Plugging the above two displays in (
5.4
), we get

\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 144\sum_{j\in S}\tau^{2}+\sum_{j\in S^{c}}|\lambda_{j}|^{2}

Since on
S
,
\tau^{2}=\min(\tau^{2},|\lambda_{j}|^{2})
and on
S^{c}
,
|\lambda_{j}|^{2}\leq 9\min(\tau^{2},|\lambda_{j}|^{2})
, it yields,

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{svt}}-\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 144\sum_{j}\min(\tau^{2},|\lambda_{j}|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 144\sum_{j=1}^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle=144\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\tau^{2}\,.\qed

In the next subsection, we extend our analysis to the case where
\mathbb{X}
does not necessarily satisfy the assumption
ORT
.

24.3
Penalization by rank

The estimator from this section is the counterpart of the BIC estimator in the spectral domain. However, we will see that unlike BIC, it can be computed efficiently.

Let
\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}
be any solution to the following minimization problem:

\min_{\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}+2\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta)\Big\}\,.

This estimator is called
estimator by rank penalization with regularization parameter
\tau^{2}
. It enjoys the following property.

Theorem 5.5
.

Consider the multivariate linear regression model (
5.1
). Then, the estimator by rank penalization
\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}
with regularization parameter
\tau^{2}
, where
\tau
is defined in (
5.3
)
satisfies

\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 8\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\tau^{2}\lesssim\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})}{n}\Big(d\vee T+\log(1/\delta)\Big)\,.

with probability
1-\delta
.

Proof.

We begin as usual by noting that

\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}\|_{F}^{2}+2n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})\leq\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}+2n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\,,

which is equivalent to

\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 2\langle E,\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\rangle-2n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})+2n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\,.

Next, by Young’s inequality, we have

2\langle E,\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\rangle=2\langle E,U\rangle^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\,,

where

U=\frac{\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}}{\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}}\,.

Write

\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}=\Phi N\,,

where
\Phi
is a
n\times r,r\leq d
matrix whose columns form an orthonormal basis of the column span of
\mathbb{X}
. The matrix
\Phi
can come from the SVD of
\mathbb{X}
for example:
\mathbb{X}=\Phi\Lambda\Psi^{\top}
.
It yields

U=\frac{\Phi N}{\|N\|_{F}}

and

\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\leq 4\langle\Phi^{\top}E,N/\|N\|_{F}\rangle^{2}-4n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})+4n\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\,.

(5.5)

Note that
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(N)\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})+\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})
. Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\langle E,U\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\langle\Phi^{\top}E,N/\|N\|_{F}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi^{\top}E\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\frac{\|N\|_{1}^{2}}{\|N\|_{F}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(N)\|\Phi^{\top}E\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\Phi^{\top}E\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\big[\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})+\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\big]\,.

Next, note that Lemma
5.3
yields
\|\Phi^{\top}E\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}\leq n\tau^{2}
so that

\langle E,U\rangle^{2}\leq n\tau^{2}\big[\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}})+\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})\big]\,.

Together with (
5.5
), this completes the proof.
∎

It follows from Theorem
5.5
that the estimator by rank penalization enjoys the same properties as the singular value thresholding estimator even when
\mathbb{X}
does not satisfy the
ORT
condition. This is reminiscent of the BIC estimator which enjoys the same properties as the hard thresholding estimator. However this analogy does not extend to computational questions. Indeed, while the rank penalty, just like the sparsity penalty, is not convex, it turns out that
\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}
can be computed efficiently.

Note first that

\min_{\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}}\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}+2\tau^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta)=\min_{k}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}\\
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta)\leq k\end{subarray}}\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}+2\tau^{2}k\Big\}\,.

Therefore, it remains to show that

\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}\\
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta)\leq k\end{subarray}}\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}

can be solved efficiently. To that end, let
\bar{\mathbb{Y}}=\mathbb{X}(\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{X})^{\dagger}\mathbb{X}^{\top}\mathbb{Y}
denote the orthogonal projection of
\mathbb{Y}
onto the image space of
\mathbb{X}
: this is a linear operator from
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}
into
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{n\times T}
. By the Pythagorean theorem, we get for any
\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}
,

\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}=\|\mathbb{Y}-\bar{\mathbb{Y}}\|_{F}^{2}+\|\bar{\mathbb{Y}}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}\,.

Next consider the SVD of
\bar{\mathbb{Y}}
:

\bar{\mathbb{Y}}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}

where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots
and define
\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}
by

\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}\,.

By Lemma
5.1
, it holds

\|\bar{\mathbb{Y}}-\tilde{\mathbb{Y}}\|_{F}^{2}=\min_{Z:\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(Z)\leq k}\|\bar{\mathbb{Y}}-Z\|_{F}^{2}\,.

Therefore, any minimizer of
\mathbb{X}\Theta\mapsto\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}
over matrices of rank at most
k
can be obtained by truncating the SVD of
\bar{\mathbb{Y}}
at order
k
.

Once
\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}
has been found, one may obtain a corresponding
\hat{\Theta}^{\textsc{rk}}
by least squares but this is not necessary for our results.

Remark 5.6
.

While the rank penalized estimator can be computed efficiently, it is worth pointing out that a convex relaxation for the rank penalty can also be used. The estimator by nuclear norm penalization
\hat{\Theta}
is defined to be any solution to the minimization problem

\min_{\Theta\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.47495pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times T}}\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{Y}-\mathbb{X}\Theta\|_{F}^{2}+\tau\|\Theta\|_{1}\Big\}

Clearly, this criterion is convex and it can actually be implemented efficiently using semi-definite programming. It has been popularized by matrix completion problems. Let
\mathbb{X}
have the following SVD:

\mathbb{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\lambda_{j}u_{j}v_{j}^{\top}\,,

with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{r}>0
. It can be shown that for some appropriate choice of
\tau
, it holds

\frac{1}{n}\|\mathbb{X}\hat{\Theta}-\mathbb{X}\Theta^{*}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{r}}\frac{\sigma^{2}\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\Theta^{*})}{n}d\vee T

with probability .99. However, the proof of this result is far more involved than a simple adaption of the proof for the Lasso estimator to the matrix case (the readers are invited to see that for themselves). For one thing, there is no assumption on the design matrix (such as
INC
for example). This result can be found in
[
KLT11
]
.

25
Covariance matrix estimation

25.1
Empirical covariance matrix

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
i.i.d. copies of a random vector
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[XX^{\top}\big]=\Sigma
for some unknown matrix
\Sigma\succ 0
called
covariance matrix
. This matrix contains information about the moments of order 2 of the random vector
X
. A natural candidate to estimate
\Sigma
is the
empirical covariance matrix

\hat{\Sigma}
defined by

\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\,.

Using the tools of Chapter
1
, we can prove the following result.

Theorem 5.7
.

Let
Y\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be a random vector such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[YY^{\top}]=I_{d}
and
Y\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(1)
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
independent copies of sub-Gaussian random vector
X=\Sigma^{1/2}Y
. Then
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[X]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma
and
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})
. Moreover,

\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\vee\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\Big)\,,

with probability
1-\delta
.

Proof.

It follows from elementary computations that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[X]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma
and
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})
. To prove the deviation inequality, observe first that without loss of generality, we can assume that
\Sigma=I_{d}
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\frac{\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}-I_{d}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}}

\displaystyle=\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}-I_{d}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,.

Thus, in the rest of the proof, we assume that
\Sigma=I_{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
1/4
-net for
\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
such that
|\mathcal{N}|\leq 12^{d}
. It follows from the proof of Lemma
5.3
that

\|\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d}\|_{\textrm{op}}\leq 2\max_{x,y\in\mathcal{N}}x^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})y.

Hence, for any
t\geq 0
, by a union bound,

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\|\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d}\|_{\textrm{op}}>t\big)\leq\sum_{x,y\in\mathcal{N}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(x^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})y>t/2\big)\,.

(5.6)

It holds,

x^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})y=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)\big]\big\}\,.

Using polarization, we also have

(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)=\frac{Z_{+}^{2}-Z_{-}^{2}}{4}\,,

where
Z_{+}=X_{i}^{\top}(x+y)
and
Z_{-}=X_{i}^{\top}(x-y)
. It yields

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}

\displaystyle\big[\exp\big(s\big((X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)\big]\big)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\exp\big(\frac{s}{4}\big(Z_{+}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{+}^{2}]\big)-\frac{s}{4}\big(Z_{-}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{-}^{2}])\big)\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\exp\big(\frac{s}{2}\big(Z_{+}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{+}^{2}]\big)\big)\big]\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\exp\big(-\frac{s}{2}\big(Z_{-}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{-}^{2}]\big)\big)\big]\Big)^{1/2}\,,

where in the last inequality, we used Cauchy-Schwarz. Next, since
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(1)
, we have
Z_{+},Z_{-}\sim\mathsf{subG}(2)
, and it follows from Lemma
1.12
that

Z_{+}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{+}^{2}]\sim\mathsf{subE}(32)\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad Z_{-}^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z_{-}^{2}]\sim\mathsf{subE}(32).

Therefore, for any
s\leq 1/16
, we have for any
Z\in\{Z_{+},Z_{-}\}
that

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\exp\big(\frac{s}{2}\big(Z^{2}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z^{2}]\big)\big)\big]\leq e^{128s^{2}}\,.

It yields that
