\displaystyle(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[(X_{i}^{\top}x)(X_{i}^{\top}y)\big]\sim\mathsf{subE}(16)\,.

Applying now Bernstein’s inequality (Theorem
1.13
), we get

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(x^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})y>t/2\big)\leq\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{32}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{32})\Big]\,.

(5.7)

Together with (
5.6
), this yields

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\|\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d}\|_{\textrm{op}}>t\big)\leq 144^{d}\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{32}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{32})\Big]\,.

(5.8)

In particular, the right hand side of the above inequality is at most
\delta\in(0,1)
if

\frac{t}{32}\geq\Big(\frac{2d}{n}\log(144)+\frac{2}{n}\log(1/\delta)\Big)\vee\Big(\frac{2d}{n}\log(144)+\frac{2}{n}\log(1/\delta)\Big)^{1/2}.

This concludes our proof.
∎

Theorem
5.7
indicates that for fixed
d
, the empirical covariance matrix is a consistent estimator of
\Sigma
(in any norm as they are all equivalent in finite dimension). However, the bound that we got is not satisfactory in high-dimensions when
d\gg n
. To overcome this limitation, we can introduce sparsity as we have done in the case of regression. The most obvious way to do so is to assume that few of the entries of
\Sigma
are non zero and it turns out that in this case thresholding is optimal. There is a long line of work on this subject (see for example
[
CZZ10
]
and
[
CZ12
]
).

Once we have a good estimator of
\Sigma
, what can we do with it? The key insight is that
\Sigma
contains information about the projection of the vector
X
onto
any
direction
u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
. Indeed, we have that
\mathrm{var}(X^{\top}u)=u^{\top}\Sigma u
, which can be readily estimated by
\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(X^{\top}u)=u^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}u
. Observe that it follows from Theorem
5.7
that

\displaystyle\big|\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}(X^{\top}u)-\mathrm{Var}(X^{\top}u)\big|

\displaystyle=\big|u^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)u\big|

\displaystyle\leq\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\vee\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\Big)

with probability
1-\delta
.

The above fact is useful in the Markowitz theory of portfolio section for example
[
Mar52
]
, where a portfolio of assets is a vector
u\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
such that
|u|_{1}=1
and the risk of a portfolio is given by the variance
\mathrm{Var}(X^{\top}u)
. The goal is then to maximize reward subject to risk constraints. In most instances, the empirical covariance matrix is plugged into the formula in place of
\Sigma
.
(See Problem
5.4
).

26
Principal component analysis

26.1
Spiked covariance model

Estimating the variance in all directions is also useful for
dimension reduction
. In
Principal Component Analysis (PCA)
, the goal is to find one (or more) directions onto which the data
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
can be projected without loosing much of its properties. There are several goals for doing this but perhaps the most prominent ones are data visualization (in few dimensions, one can plot and visualize the cloud of
n
points) and clustering (clustering is a hard computational problem and it is therefore preferable to carry it out in lower dimensions). An example of the output of a principal component analysis is given in Figure
5.8
. In this figure, the data has been projected onto two orthogonal directions
PC1
and
PC2
, that were estimated to have the most variance (among all such orthogonal pairs). The idea is that when projected onto such directions, points will remain far apart and a clustering pattern will still emerge. This is the case in Figure
5.8
where the original data is given by
d=500,000
gene expression levels measured on
n\simeq 1,387
people. Depicted are the projections of these
1,387
points in two dimension. This image has become quite popular as it shows that gene expression levels can recover the structure induced by geographic clustering.

Figure 5.8:
Projection onto two dimensions of
1,387
points from gene expression data. Source: Gene expression blog.

How is it possible to “compress” half a million dimensions into only two? The answer is that the data is intrinsically low dimensional. In this case, a plausible assumption is that all the
1,387
points live close to a two-dimensional linear subspace. To see how this assumption (in one dimension instead of two for simplicity) translates into the structure of the covariance matrix
\Sigma
, assume that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
are Gaussian random variables generated as follows. Fix a direction
v\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
and let
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,I_{d})
so that
v^{\top}Y_{i}
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. In particular, the vectors
(v^{\top}Y_{1})v,\ldots,(v^{\top}Y_{n})v
live in the one-dimensional space spanned by
v
. If one would observe such data the problem would be easy as only two observations would suffice to recover
v
. Instead, we observe
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
where
X_{i}=(v^{\top}Y_{i})v+Z_{i}
, and
Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
are i.i.d. and independent of the
Y_{i}
s, that is we add isotropic noise to every point. If the
\sigma
is small enough, we can hope to recover the direction
v
(See Figure
5.9
). The covariance matrix of
X_{i}
generated as such is given by

\Sigma=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[XX^{\top}\big]=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[((v^{\top}Y)v+Z)((v^{\top}Y)v+Z)^{\top}\big]=vv^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{d}\,.

This model is often called the
spiked covariance model
. By a simple rescaling, it is equivalent to the following definition.

Definition 5.8
.

A covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times d}
is said to satisfy the spiked covariance model if it is of the form

\Sigma=\theta vv^{\top}+I_{d}\,,

where
\theta>0
and
v\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
. The vector
v
is called the
spike
.

This model can be extended to more than one spike but this extension is beyond the scope of these notes.

v

Figure 5.9:
Points are close to a one-dimensional space spanned by
v
.

Clearly, under the spiked covariance model,
v
is the eigenvector of the matrix
\Sigma
that is associated to its largest eigenvalue
1+\theta
. We will refer to this vector simply as
largest eigenvector
. To estimate it, a natural candidate is the largest eigenvector
\hat{v}
of
\tilde{\Sigma}
, where
\tilde{\Sigma}
is any estimator of
\Sigma
. There is a caveat: by symmetry, if
u
is an eigenvector, of a symmetric matrix, then
-u
is also an eigenvector associated to the same eigenvalue. Therefore, we may only estimate
v
up to a sign flip. To overcome this limitation, it is often useful to describe proximity between two vectors
u
and
v
in terms of the principal angle between their linear span. Let us recall that for two unit vectors the principal angle between their linear spans is denoted by
\angle(u,v)
and defined as

\angle(u,v)=\arccos(|u^{\top}v|)\,.

The following result from perturbation theory is known as the Davis-Kahan
\sin(\theta)
theorem as it bounds the sin of the principal angle between eigenspaces. This theorem exists in much more general versions that extend beyond one-dimensional eigenspaces.

Theorem 5.9

(Davis-Kahan
\sin(\theta)
theorem)
.

Let
A,B
be two
d\times d
PSD matrices. Let
(\lambda_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,(\lambda_{d},u_{d})
(resp.
(\mu_{1},v_{1}),\ldots,(\mu_{d},v_{d})
) denote the pairs of eigenvalues–eigenvectors of
A
(resp. B) ordered such that
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots
(resp.
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{2}\geq\dots
). Then

\sin\big(\angle(u_{1},v_{1})\big)\leq\frac{2}{\max(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2},\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})}\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,.

Moreover,

\min_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}|\varepsilon u_{1}-v_{1}|_{2}^{2}\leq 2\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},v_{1})\big)\,.

Proof.

Note that
u_{1}^{\top}Au_{1}=\lambda_{1}
and for any
x\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
, it holds

\displaystyle x^{\top}Ax

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}(u_{j}^{\top}x)^{2}\leq\lambda_{1}(u_{1}^{\top}x)^{2}+\lambda_{2}(1-(u_{1}^{\top}x)^{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\cos^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)+\lambda_{2}\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)\,.

Therefore, taking
x=v_{1}
, we get that on the one hand,

\displaystyle u_{1}^{\top}Au_{1}-v_{1}^{\top}Av_{1}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{1}\cos^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)-\lambda_{2}\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)\,.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle u_{1}^{\top}Au_{1}-v_{1}^{\top}Av_{1}

\displaystyle=u_{1}^{\top}Bu_{1}-v_{1}^{\top}Av_{1}+u_{1}^{\top}(A-B)u_{1}

\displaystyle\leq v_{1}^{\top}Bv_{1}-v_{1}^{\top}Av_{1}+u_{1}^{\top}(A-B)u_{1}

(
v_{1}
is leading eigenvector of
B
)

\displaystyle=\langle A-B,u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}\|_{1}

(Hölder)

\displaystyle\leq\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\sqrt{2}\|u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}\|_{2}

(Cauchy-Schwarz)

where in the last inequality, we used the fact that
\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top})=2
.

It is straightforward to check that

\|u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}\|_{2}^{2}=\|u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}-v_{1}v_{1}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}=2-2(u_{1}^{\top}v_{1})^{2}=2\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},v_{1})\big)\,.

We have proved that

(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)\leq 2\|A-B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\sin\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)\,,

which concludes the first part of the lemma. Note that we can replace
\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
with
\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
since the result is completely symmetric in
A
and
B
.

It remains to show the second part of the lemma. To that end, observe that

\min_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}|\varepsilon u_{1}-v_{1}|_{2}^{2}=2-2|\tilde{u}_{1}^{\top}v_{1}|\leq 2-2(u_{1}^{\top}v_{1})^{2}=2\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u_{1},x)\big)\,.\qed

Combined with Theorem
5.7
, we immediately get the following corollary.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
Y\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be a random vector such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[YY^{\top}]=I_{d}
and
Y\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(1)
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
independent copies of sub-Gaussian random vector
X=\Sigma^{1/2}Y
so that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[X]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma
and
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})
. Assume further that
\Sigma=\theta vv^{\top}+I_{d}
satisfies the spiked covariance model. Then, the largest eigenvector
\hat{v}
of the empirical covariance matrix
\hat{\Sigma}
satisfies,

\min_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}|\varepsilon\hat{v}-v|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1+\theta}{\theta}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\vee\frac{d+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\Big)

with probability
1-\delta
.

This result justifies the use of the empirical covariance matrix
\hat{\Sigma}
as a replacement for the true covariance matrix
\Sigma

when performing PCA in low dimensions, that is when
d\ll n
. In the high-dimensional case, where
d\gg n
, the above result is uninformative. As before, we resort to sparsity to overcome this limitation.

26.2
Sparse PCA

In the example of Figure
5.8
, it may be desirable to interpret the meaning of the two directions denoted by
PC1
and
PC2
. We know that they are linear combinations of the original 500,000 gene expression levels. A natural question to ask is whether only a subset of these genes could suffice to obtain similar results. Such a discovery could have potential interesting scientific applications as it would point to a few genes responsible for disparities between European populations.

In the case of the spiked covariance model this amounts to having a sparse
v
. Beyond interpretability as we just discussed, sparsity should also lead to statistical stability as in the case of sparse linear regression for example. To enforce sparsity, we will assume that
v
in the spiked covariance model is
k
-sparse:
|v|_{0}=k
. Therefore, a natural candidate to estimate
v
is given by
\hat{v}
defined by

\hat{v}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{v}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}\\
|u|_{0}=k\end{subarray}}u^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}u\,.

It is easy to check that
\lambda^{k}_{\max}(\hat{\Sigma})=\hat{v}^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{v}
is the largest of all leading eigenvalues among all
k\times k
sub-matrices of
\hat{\Sigma}
so that the maximum is indeed attained, though there my be several maximizers. We call
\lambda^{k}_{\max}(\hat{\Sigma})
the
k
-sparse leading eigenvalue of
\hat{\Sigma}
and
\hat{v}
a
k
-sparse leading eigenvector.

Theorem 5.11
.

Let
Y\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be a random vector such that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Y]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[YY^{\top}]=I_{d}
and
Y\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(1)
. Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
independent copies of sub-Gaussian random vector
X=\Sigma^{1/2}Y
so that
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[X]=0,\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma
and
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})
. Assume further that
\Sigma=\theta vv^{\top}+I_{d}
satisfies the spiked covariance model for
v
such that
|v|_{0}=k\leq d/2
. Then, the
k
-sparse largest eigenvector
\hat{v}
of the empirical covariance matrix satisfies,

\min_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}|\varepsilon\hat{v}-v|_{2}\lesssim\frac{1+\theta}{\theta}\Big(\sqrt{\frac{k\log(ed/k)+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\vee\frac{k\log(ed/k)+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\Big)\,.

with probability
1-\delta
.

Proof.

We begin by obtaining an intermediate result of the Davis-Kahan
\sin(\theta)
theorem. Using the same steps as in the proof of Theorem
5.9
, we get

v^{\top}\Sigma v-\hat{v}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{v}\leq\langle\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma,\hat{v}\hat{v}^{\top}-vv^{\top}\rangle

Since both
\hat{v}
and
v
are
k
sparse, there exists a (random) set
S\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that
|S|\leq 2k
and
\{\hat{v}\hat{v}^{\top}-vv^{\top}\}_{ij}=0
if
(i,j)\notin S^{2}
. It yields

\langle\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma,\hat{v}\hat{v}^{\top}-vv^{\top}\rangle=\langle\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S),\hat{v}(S)\hat{v}(S)^{\top}-v(S)v(S)^{\top}\rangle

Where for any
d\times d
matrix
M
, we defined the matrix
M(S)
to be the
|S|\times|S|
sub-matrix of
M
with rows and columns indexed by
S
and for any vector
x\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
,
x(S)\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{|S|}
denotes the sub-vector of
x
with coordinates indexed by
S
. It yields by Hölder’s inequality that

v^{\top}\Sigma v-\hat{v}^{\top}\Sigma\hat{v}\leq\|\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\hat{v}(S)\hat{v}(S)^{\top}-v(S)v(S)^{\top}\|_{1}\,.

Following the same steps as in the proof of Theorem
5.9
, we get now that

\sin\big(\angle(\hat{v},v)\big)\leq\frac{2}{\theta}\sup_{S\,:\,|S|=2k}\|\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,.

To conclude the proof, it remains to control
\sup_{S\,:\,|S|=2k}\|\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
. To that end, observe that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big[\sup_{S\,:\,|S|=2k}

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}>t\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{S\,:\,|S|=2k}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\Big[\sup_{S\,:\,|S|=2k}\|\hat{\Sigma}(S)-\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}>t\|\Sigma(S)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\binom{d}{2k}144^{2k}\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{32}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{32})\Big]\,.

where we used (
5.8
) in the second inequality. Using Lemma
2.7
, we get that the right-hand side above is further bounded by

\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{32}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{32})+2k\log(144)+k\log\big(\frac{ed}{2k}\big)\Big]\,

Choosing now
t
such that

t\geq C\sqrt{\frac{k\log(ed/k)+\log(1/\delta)}{n}}\vee\frac{k\log(ed/k)+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\,,

for large enough
C
ensures that the desired bound holds with probability at least
1-\delta
.
∎

26.3
Minimax lower bound

The last section has established that the spike
v
in the spiked covariance model
\Sigma=\theta vv^{\top}
may be estimated at the rate
\theta^{-1}\sqrt{k\log(d/k)/n}
, assuming that
\theta\leq 1
and
k\log(d/k)\leq n
, which corresponds to the interesting regime. Using the tools from Chapter
4
, we can show that this rate is in fact optimal already in the Gaussian case.

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
be
n
independent copies of the Gaussian random vector
X\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}(0,\Sigma)
where
\Sigma=\theta vv^{\top}+I_{d}
satisfies the spiked covariance model for
v
such that
|v|_{0}=k
and write
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v}
the distribution of
X
under this assumption and by
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{v}
the associated expectation. Then, there exists constants
C,c>0
such that for any
n\geq 2
,
d\geq 9
,
2\leq k\leq(d+7)/8
, it holds

\inf_{\hat{v}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}\\
|v|_{0}=k\end{subarray}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v}^{n}\Big[\min_{\varepsilon\in\{\pm 1\}}|\varepsilon\hat{v}-v|_{2}\geq C\frac{1}{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}\log(ed/k)}\Big]\geq c\,.

Proof.

We use the general technique developed in Chapter
4
. Using this technique, we need to provide the a set
v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
of
k
sparse vectors such that

(i)

\displaystyle\min_{\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}}|\varepsilon v_{j}-v_{k}|_{2}>2\psi
for all
1\leq j<k\leq M
,

(ii)

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v_{j}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v_{k}})\leq c\log(M)/n
,

where
\psi=C\theta^{-1}\sqrt{k\log(d/k)/n}
. Akin to the Gaussian sequence model, our goal is to employ the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert lemma to construct the
v_{j}
’s. However, the
v_{j}
’s have the extra constraint that they need to be of unit norm so we cannot simply rescale the sparse binary vectors output by the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert lemma. Instead, we use the last coordinate to make sure that they have unit norm.

Recall that it follows from the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
4.14
that there exits
\omega_{1},\ldots,\omega_{M}\in\{-1,1\}^{d-1}
such that
|\omega_{j}|_{0}=k-1
,
\rho(\omega_{i},\omega_{j})\geq(k-1)/2
for all
i\neq j
and
\log(M)\geq\frac{k-1}{8}\log(1+\frac{d-1}{2k-2})
. Define
v_{j}=(\gamma\omega_{j},\ell)
where
\gamma<1/\sqrt{k-1}
is to be defined later and
\ell=\sqrt{1-\gamma^{2}(k-1)}
. It is straightforward to check that
|v_{j}|_{0}=k
,
|v_{j}|_{2}=1
and
v_{j}
has only nonnegative coordinates. Moreover, for any
i\neq j
,

\min_{\varepsilon\in{\pm 1}}|\varepsilon v_{i}-v_{j}|^{2}_{2}=|v_{i}-v_{j}|^{2}_{2}=\gamma^{2}\rho(v_{i},v_{j})\geq\gamma^{2}\frac{k-1}{2}\,.

(5.9)

We choose

\gamma^{2}=\frac{C_{\gamma}}{\theta^{2}n}\log\big(\frac{d}{k}\big)\,,

so that
\gamma^{2}(k-1)=\psi
and (i) is verified. It remains to check (ii). To that end, note that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{u},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{u}\Big(X^{\top}\big[(I_{d}+\theta vv^{\top})^{-1}-(I_{d}+\theta vv^{\top})^{-1}\big]X\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{u}\mathsf{Tr}\Big(X^{\top}\big[(I_{d}+\theta vv^{\top})^{-1}-(I_{d}+\theta vv^{\top})^{-1}\big]X\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}\Big(\big[(I_{d}+\theta vv^{\top})^{-1}-(I_{d}+\theta uu^{\top})^{-1}\big](I_{d}+\theta uu^{\top})\Big)

Next, we use the Sherman-Morrison formula
9
9

9

To see this, check that
(I_{d}+\theta uu^{\top})(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{1+\theta})=I_{d}
and that the two matrices on the left-hand side commute.
:

(I_{d}+\theta uu^{\top})^{-1}=I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{1+\theta}uu^{\top}\,,\qquad\forall\,u\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}\,.

It yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{u},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v})

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta}{2(1+\theta)}\mathsf{Tr}\Big((uu^{\top}-vv^{\top})(I_{d}+\theta uu^{\top})\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta^{2}}{2(1+\theta)}(1-(u^{\top}v)^{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta^{2}}{2(1+\theta)}\sin^{2}\big(\angle(u,v)\big)

It yields

\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v_{i}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{v_{j}})=\frac{\theta^{2}}{2(1+\theta)}\sin^{2}\big(\angle(v_{i},v_{j})\big)\,.

Next, note that

\displaystyle\sin^{2}\big(\angle(v_{i},v_{j})\big)

\displaystyle=1-\gamma^{2}\omega_{i}^{\top}\omega_{j}-\ell^{2}\leq\gamma^{2}(k-1)\leq C_{\gamma}\frac{k}{\theta^{2}n}\log\big(\frac{d}{k}\big)\leq\frac{\log M}{\theta^{2}n}\,,

for
C_{\gamma}
small enough. We conclude that (ii) holds, which completes our proof.
∎

Together with the upper bound of Theorem
5.11
, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 5.13
.

Assume that
\theta\leq 1
and
k\log(d/k)\leq n
. Then
\theta^{-1}\sqrt{k\log(d/k)/n}
is the minimax rate of estimation over
\mathcal{B}_{0}(k)
in the spiked covariance model.

27
Graphical models

27.1
Gaussian graphical models

In Section
25
, we noted that thresholding is an appropriate way to gain an advantage from sparsity in the covariance matrix when trying to estimate it.
However, in some cases, it is more natural to assume sparsity in the inverse of the covariance matrix,
\Theta=\Sigma^{-1}
, which is sometimes called
concentration
or
precision matrix
.
In this chapter, we will develop an approach that allows us to get guarantees similar to the lasso for the error between the estimate and the ground truth matrix in Frobenius norm.

We can understand why sparsity in
\Theta
can be appropriate in the context of
undirected graphical models

[
Lau96
]
.
These are models that serve to simplify dependence relations between high-dimensional distributions according to a graph.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph on
d
nodes and to each node
v
, associate a random variable
X_{v}
.
An undirected graphical model or
Markov random field
is a collection of probability distributions over
X_{v}
that factorize according to

p(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\frac{1}{Z}\prod_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\psi_{C}(x_{C}),

(5.10)

where
\mathcal{C}
is the collection of all cliques (completely connected subsets) in
G
,
x_{C}
is the restriction of
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
to the subset
C
, and
\psi_{C}
are non-negative potential functions.

This definition implies certain conditional independence relations, such as the following.

Proposition 5.15
.

A graphical model as in (
5.10
) fulfills the
global Markov property
.
That is, for any triple
(A,B,S)
of disjoint subsets such that
S
separates
A
and
B
,

A\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\displaystyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\textstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptstyle\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\perp$\hss}\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\perp}}}B\mid S.

Proof.

Let
(A,B,S)
a triple as in the proposition statement and define
\tilde{A}
to be the connected component in the induced subgraph
G[V\setminus S]
, as well as
\tilde{B}=G[V\setminus(\tilde{A}\cup S)]
.
By assumption,
A
and
B
have to be in different connected components of
G[V\setminus S]
, hence any clique in
G
has to be a subset of
\tilde{A}\cup S
or
\tilde{B}\cup S
.
Denoting the former cliques by
\mathcal{C}_{A}
, we get

f(x)=\prod_{C\in\mathcal{C}}\psi_{C}(x)=\prod_{C\in\mathcal{C}_{A}}\psi_{C}(x)\prod_{c\in\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{A}}\psi_{C}(x)=h(x_{\tilde{A}\cup S})k(x_{\tilde{B}\cup S}),

which implies the desired conditional independence.
∎

A weaker form of the Markov property is the so-called
pairwise Markov property
, which says that the above holds only for singleton sets of the form
A=\{a\}
,
B=\{b\}
and
S=V\setminus\{a,b\}
if
(a,b)\notin E
.

In the following, we will focus on Gaussian graphical models for
n
i.i.d. samples
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,(\Theta^{\ast})^{-1})
.
By contrast, there is a large body of work on discrete graphical models such as the Ising model (see for example
[
Bre15
]
), which we are not going to discuss here, although links to the following can be established as well
[
LWo12
]
.

For a Gaussian model with mean zero, by considering the density in terms of the concentration matrix
\Theta
,

f_{\Theta}(x)\propto\exp(-x^{\top}\Theta x/2),

it is immediate that the pairs
a,b
for which the pairwise Markov property holds are exactly those for which
\Theta_{i,j}=0
.
Conversely, it can be shown that for a family of distributions with non-negative density, this actually implies the factorization (
5.10
) according to the graph given by the edges
\{(i,j):\Theta_{i,j}\neq 0\}
.
This is the content of the Hammersley-Clifford theorem.

Theorem 5.16

(Hammersley-Clifford)
.

Let
P
be a probability distribution with positive density
f
with respect to a product measure over
V
.
Then
P
factorizes over
G
as in (
5.10
) if and only if it fulfills the pairwise Markov property.

We show the if direction.
The requirement that
f
be positive allows us to take logarithms.
Pick a fixed element
x^{\ast}
.
The idea of the proof is to rewrite the density in a way that allows us to make use of the pairwise Markov property.
The following combinatorial lemma will be used for this purpose.

Lemma 5.17

(Moebius Inversion)
.

Let
\Psi,\Phi:\mathcal{P}(V)\to\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
be functions defined for all subsets of a set
V
.
Then, the following are equivalent:

1.

For all
A\subseteq V
:
\Psi(A)=\sum_{B\subseteq A}\Phi(B)
;

2.

For all
A\subseteq V
:
\Phi(A)=\sum_{B\subseteq A}(-1)^{|A\setminus B}\Psi(B)
.

Proof.

1.\implies 2.
:

\displaystyle\sum_{B\subseteq A}\Phi(B)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{B\subseteq A}\sum_{C\subseteq B}(-1)^{|B\setminus C|}\Psi(C)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\subseteq A}\Psi(C)\left(\sum_{C\subseteq B\subseteq A}(-1)^{|B\setminus C|}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{C\subseteq A}\Psi(C)\left(\sum_{H\subseteq A\setminus C}(-1)^{|H|}\right).

Now,
\sum_{H\subseteq A\setminus C}(-1)^{|H|}=0
unless
A\setminus C=\emptyset
because any non-empty set has the same number of subsets with odd and even cardinality, which can be seen by induction.
∎

Proof.

Define

H_{A}(x)=\log f(x_{A},x^{\ast}_{A^{c}})

and

\Phi_{A}(x)=\sum_{B\subseteq A}(-1)^{|A\setminus B|}H_{B}(x).

Note that both
H_{A}
and
\Phi_{A}
depend on
x
only through
x_{A}
.
By Lemma
5.17
, we get

\log f(x)=H_{V}(x)=\sum_{A\subseteq V}\Phi_{A}(x).

It remains to show that
\Phi_{A}=0
if
A
is not a clique.
Assume
A\subseteq V
with
v,w\in A
,
(v,w)\notin E
, and set
C=V\setminus\{v,w\}
.
By adding all possible subsets of
\{v,w\}
to every subset of
C
, we get every subset of
A
, and hence

\Phi_{A}(x)=\sum_{B\subseteq C}(-1)^{|C\setminus B|}(H_{B}-H_{B\cup\{v\}}-H_{B\cup\{w\}}+H_{B\cup\{v,w\}}).

Writing
D=V\setminus\{v,w\}
, by the pairwise Markov property, we have

\displaystyle H_{B\cup\{v,w\}}(x)-H_{B\cup\{v\}}(x)={}

\displaystyle\log\frac{f(x_{v},x_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B}}{f(x_{v},x_{w}^{\ast},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B}}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\log\frac{f(x_{v}|x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})f(x_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}{f(x_{v}|x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})f(x^{\ast}_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\log\frac{f(x^{\ast}_{v}|x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})f(x_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}{f(x^{\ast}_{v}|x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})f(x^{\ast}_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\log\frac{f(x^{\ast}_{v},x_{w},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}{f(x^{\ast}_{v},x_{w}^{\ast},x_{B},x^{\ast}_{D\setminus B})}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle H_{B\cup\{w\}}(x)-H_{B}(x).

Thus
\Psi_{A}
vanishes if
A
is not a clique.
∎

In the following, we will show how to exploit this kind of sparsity when trying to estimate
\Theta
.
The key insight is that we can set up an optimization problem that has a similar error sensitivity as the lasso, but with respect to
\Theta
.
In fact, it is the penalized version of the maximum likelihood estimator for the Gaussian distribution.
We set

\hat{\Theta}_{\lambda}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\Theta\succ 0}\mathsf{Tr}(\hat{\Sigma}\Theta)-\log\det(\Theta)+\lambda\|\Theta_{D^{c}}\|_{1},

(5.11)

where we wrote
\Theta_{D^{c}}
for the off-diagonal restriction of
\Theta
,
\|A\|_{1}=\sum_{i,j}|A_{i,j}|
for the element-wise
\ell_{1}
norm, and
\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
for the empirical covariance matrix of
n
i.i.d. observations.
We will also make use of the notation

\|A\|_{\infty}=\max_{i,j\in[d]}|A_{i,j}|

for the element-wise
\infty
-norm of a matrix
A\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times d}
and

\|A\|_{\infty,\infty}=\max_{i}\sum_{j}|A_{i,j}|

for the
\infty
operator norm.

We note that the function
\Theta\mapsto-\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta)
has first derivative
-\Theta^{-1}
and second derivative
\Theta^{-1}\otimes\Theta^{-1}
, which means it is convex.
Derivations for this can be found in
[
BV04
, Appendix A.4]
.
This means that in the population case and for
\lambda=0
, the minimizer in (
5.11
) would coincide with
\Theta^{\ast}
.

First, let us derive a bound on the error in
\hat{\Sigma}
that is similar to Theorem
1.14
, with the difference that we are dealing with sub-Exponential distributions.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
are i.i.d. and
\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
denotes the empirical covariance matrix, then

\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\infty}\lesssim\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}},

with probability at least
1-\delta
, if
n\gtrsim\log(d/\delta)
.

Proof.

Start as in the proof of Theorem
5.7
by writing
Y=\Sigma^{-1/2}X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and notice that

\displaystyle|e_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)e_{j}|={}

\displaystyle|(\Sigma^{1/2}e_{i})^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}-I_{d}\right)(\Sigma^{1/2}e_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\|\Sigma^{1/2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{2}|v^{\top}\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}Y_{i}^{\top}-I_{d}\right)w|

with
v,w\in\mathcal{S}^{d-1}
.
Hence, without loss of generality, we can assume
\Sigma=I_{d}
.
The remaining term is sub-Exponential and can be controlled as in (
5.7
)

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(e_{i}^{\top}(\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d})e_{j}>t\big)\leq\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{16}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{16})\Big]\,.

By a union bound, we get

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}\big(\|\hat{\Sigma}-I_{d}\|_{\infty}>t\big)\leq d^{2}\exp\Big[-\frac{n}{2}(\Big(\frac{t}{16}\Big)^{2}\wedge\frac{t}{16})\Big]\,.

Hence, if
n\gtrsim\log(d/\delta)
, then the right-hand side above is at most
\delta\in(0,1)
if

\frac{t}{16}\geq\left(\frac{2\log(d/\delta)}{n}\right)^{1/2}.\qed

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
be i.i.d. and
\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}
denote the empirical covariance matrix.
Assume that
|S|=k
, where
S=\{(i,j):i\neq j,\,\Theta_{i,j}\neq 0\}
.
There exists a constant
c=c(\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}})
such that if

\lambda=c\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}},

and
n\gtrsim\log(d/\delta)
, then the minimizer in (
5.11
) fulfills

\|\hat{\Theta}-\Theta^{\ast}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\frac{(p+k)\log(d/\delta)}{n}

with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.

We start by considering the likelihood

l(\Theta,\Sigma)=\mathsf{Tr}(\Sigma\Theta)-\log\det(\Theta).

Taylor expanding it and the mean value theorem yield

\displaystyle l(\Theta,\Sigma_{n})-l(\Theta^{\ast},\Sigma_{n})={}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}(\Sigma_{n}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}))-\mathsf{Tr}(\Sigma^{\ast}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}))

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast})\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast})),

for some
\tilde{\Theta}=\Theta^{\ast}+t(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast})
,
t\in[0,1]
.
Note that essentially by the convexity of
\log\det
, we have

\mathsf{Tr}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast})\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}))=\|\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast})\|_{F}^{2}\geq\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1})^{2}\|\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}\|_{F}^{2}

and

\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1})=(\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\tilde{\Theta}))^{-1}.

If we write
\Delta=\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}
, then for
\|\Delta\|_{F}\leq 1
,

\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\tilde{\Theta})=\|\tilde{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\|\Theta^{\ast}+\Delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\Theta^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|\Theta^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\Delta\|_{F}\leq\|\Theta^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+1,

and therefore

l(\Theta,\Sigma_{n})-l(\Theta^{\ast},\Sigma_{n})\geq\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma_{n}-\Sigma^{\ast})(\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}))+c\|\Theta-\Theta^{\ast}\|_{F}^{2},

for
c=(\|\Theta^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+1)^{-2}/2
, if
\|\Delta\|_{F}\leq 1
.

This takes care of the case where
\Delta
is small.
To handle the case where it is large, define
g(t)=l(\Theta^{\ast}+t\Delta,\Sigma_{n})-l(\Theta^{\ast},\Sigma_{n})
.
By the convexity of
l
in
\Theta
,

\frac{g(1)-g(0)}{1}\geq\frac{g(t)-g(0)}{t},

so that plugging in
t=\|\Delta\|_{F}^{-1}
gives

\displaystyle l(\Theta,\Sigma_{n})-l(\Theta^{\ast},\Sigma_{n})\geq{}

\displaystyle\|\Delta\|_{F}\left(l(\Theta^{\ast}+\frac{1}{\|\Delta\|_{F}}\Delta,\Sigma_{n})-l(\Theta^{\ast},\Sigma_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\geq{}

\displaystyle\|\Delta\|_{F}\left(\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma_{n}-\Sigma^{\ast})\frac{1}{\|\Delta\|_{F}}\Delta)+c\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma_{n}-\Sigma^{\ast})\Delta)+c\|\Delta\|_{F},

for
\|\Delta\|_{F}\geq 1
.

If we now write
\Delta=\hat{\Theta}-\Theta^{\ast}
and assume
\|\Delta\|_{F}\geq 1
, then by optimality of
\hat{\Theta}
,

\displaystyle\|\Delta\|_{F}\leq{}

\displaystyle C\left[\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})\Delta)+\lambda(\|\Theta^{\ast}_{D^{c}}\|_{1}-\|\Theta_{D^{c}}\|_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\left[\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})\Delta)+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1})\right],

where
S=\{(i,j)\in D^{c}:\Theta^{\ast}_{i,j}\neq 0\}
by triangle inequality.
Now, split the error contributions for the diagonal and off-diagonal elements,

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})\Delta)+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\|(\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})_{D}\|_{F}\|\Delta_{D}\|_{F}+\|(\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})_{D^{c}}\|_{\infty}\|\Delta_{D^{c}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1}).

By Hölder inequality,
\|(\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})_{D}\|_{F}\leq\sqrt{d}\|\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n}\|_{\infty}
, and by Lemma
5.18
,
\|\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq\sqrt{\log(d/\delta)}/\sqrt{n}
for
n\gtrsim\log(d/\delta)
.
Combining these two estimates,

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\mathsf{Tr}((\Sigma^{\ast}-\Sigma_{n})\Delta)+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1})

\displaystyle\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{d\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D}\|_{F}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D^{c}}\|_{1}+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1})

Setting
\lambda=C\sqrt{\log(ep/\delta)/n}
and splitting
\|\Delta_{D^{c}}\|_{1}=\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}+\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1}
yields

\displaystyle\kern-40.00006pt\sqrt{\frac{d\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D}\|_{F}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D^{c}}\|_{1}+\lambda(\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}-\|\Delta_{S^{c}}\|_{1})

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{d\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D}\|_{F}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{S}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{d\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{D}\|_{F}+\sqrt{\frac{k\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta_{S}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{(d+k)\log(d/\delta)}{n}}\|\Delta\|_{F}

Combining this with a left-hand side of
\|\Delta\|_{F}
yields
\|\Delta\|_{F}=0
for
n\gtrsim(d+k)\log(d/\delta)
, a contradiction to
\|\Delta\|_{F}\geq 1
where this bound is effective.
Combining it with a left-hand side of
\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2}
gives us

\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\frac{(d+k)\log(d/\delta)}{n},

as desired.
∎

Write
\Sigma^{\ast}=W^{\ast}\Gamma^{\ast}W^{\ast}
, where
W^{2}=\Sigma^{\ast}_{D}
and similarly define
\hat{W}
by
\hat{W}^{2}=\hat{\Sigma}_{D}
.
Define a slightly modified estimator by replacing
\hat{\Sigma}
in (
5.11
) by
\hat{\Gamma}
to get an estimator
\hat{K}
for
K=(\Gamma^{\ast})^{-1}
.

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma\|_{\infty}={}

\displaystyle\|\hat{W}^{-1}\hat{\Sigma}\hat{W}^{-1}-(W^{\ast})^{-1}\Sigma^{\ast}(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty}

(5.12)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\|\hat{W}^{-1}-(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{W}^{-1}-(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}

\displaystyle+\|\hat{W}^{-1}-(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}\left(\|\hat{\Sigma}\|_{\infty}\|(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty}+\|\Sigma^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\|\hat{W}^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}\right)

\displaystyle+\|\hat{W}^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\|(W^{\ast})^{-1}\|_{\infty,\infty}

(5.13)

Note that
\|A\|_{\infty}\leq\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
, so that if
n\gtrsim\log(d/\delta)
, then
\|\hat{\Gamma}-\Gamma\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\log(d/\delta)}/\sqrt{n}
.

From the above arguments, conclude
\|\hat{K}-K^{\ast}\|_{F}\lesssim\sqrt{k\log(d/\delta)/n}
and with a calculation similar to (
5.13
) that

\|\hat{\Theta}^{\prime}-\Theta^{\ast}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{k\log(d/\delta)}{n}}.

This will not work in Frobenius norm since there, we only have
\|\hat{W}^{2}-W^{2}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim\sqrt{p\log(d/\delta)/n}
.

27.1.1
Lower bounds

Here, we will show that the bounds in Theorem
5.19
are optimal up to log factors.
It will again be based on applying Fano’s inequality, Theorem
4.10
.

In order to calculate the KL divergence between two Gaussian distributions with densities
f_{\Sigma_{1}}
and
f_{\Sigma_{2}}
, we first observe that

\displaystyle\log\frac{f_{\Sigma_{1}}(x)}{f_{\Sigma_{2}}(x)}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}\Theta_{1}x+\frac{1}{2}\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{1})+\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}\Theta_{2}x-\frac{1}{2}\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{2})

,
so that

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\Sigma_{1}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\Sigma_{2}})={}

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}_{\Sigma_{1}}\log\left(\frac{f_{\Sigma_{1}}}{f_{\Sigma_{2}}}(X)\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{1})-\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{2})+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\mathsf{Tr}((\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{1})XX^{\top})]\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{1})-\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{2})+\mathsf{Tr}((\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{1})\Sigma_{1})\right]

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{1})-\log\operatorname{det}(\Theta_{2})+\mathsf{Tr}(\Theta_{2}\Sigma_{1})-p\right].

writing
\Delta=\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{1}
,
\tilde{\Theta}=\Theta_{1}+t\Delta
,
t\in[0,1]
, Taylor expansion then yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\Sigma_{1}},\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\Sigma_{2}})={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[-\mathsf{Tr}(\Delta\Sigma_{1})+\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{Tr}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}^{-1}\Delta)+\mathsf{Tr}(\Delta\Sigma_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{\mathrm{max}}(\tilde{\Theta}^{-1})\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}^{-1}(\tilde{\Theta})\|\Delta\|_{F}^{2}.

This means we are in good shape in applying our standard tricks if we can make sure that
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\tilde{\Theta})
stays large enough among our hypotheses.
To this end, assume
k\leq p^{2}/16
and define the collection of matrices
(B_{j})_{j\in[M_{1}]}
with entries in
\{0,1\}
such that they are symmetric, have zero diagonal and only have non-zero entries in a band of size
\sqrt{k}
around the diagonal, and whose entries are given by a flattening of the vectors obtained by the sparse Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
4.14
.
Moreover, let
(C_{j})_{j\in[M_{2}]}
be diagonal matrices with entries in
\{0,1\}
given by the regular Varshamov-Gilbert Lemma
4.12
.
Then, define

\Theta_{j}=\Theta_{j_{1},j_{2}}=I+\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{n}}(\sqrt{\log(1+d/(2k))}B_{j_{1}}+C_{j_{2}}),\quad j_{1}\in[M_{1}],\,j_{2}\in[M_{2}].

We can ensure that
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}(\tilde{\Theta})
is small by the Gershgorin circle theorem.
For each row of
\tilde{\Theta}-I
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l}|\tilde{\Theta}_{il}|\leq\frac{2\beta(\sqrt{k}+1)}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\log(1+d/(2k)}<\frac{1}{2}

if
n\gtrsim k/\log(1+d/(2k))
.

Hence,

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{j}-\Theta_{l}\|_{F}^{2}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}(\rho(B_{j_{1}},B_{l_{1}})\log(1+d/(2k))+\rho(C_{j_{2}},C_{l_{2}}))

\displaystyle\gtrsim{}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}k}{n}(k\log(1+d/(2k))+d),

and

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{j}-\Theta_{l}\|_{F}^{2}\lesssim{}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}(k\log(1+d/(2k))+d)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta^{2}}{n}\log(M_{1}M_{2}),

which yields the following theorem.

Theorem 5.20
.

Denote by
\mathcal{B}_{k}
the set of positive definite matrices with at most
k
non-zero off-diagonal entries and assume
n\gtrsim k\log(d)
.
Then, if
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}_{\Theta}
denotes a Gaussian distribution with inverse covariance matrix
\Theta
and mean
0
, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\inf_{\hat{\Theta}}\sup_{\Theta^{\ast}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}^{\otimes n}_{\Theta^{\ast}}\left(\|\hat{\Theta}-\Theta^{\ast}\|_{F}^{2}\geq c\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n}(k\log(1+d/(2k))+d)\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha.

27.2
Ising model

Like the Gaussian graphical model, the Ising model is also a model of pairwise interactions but for random variables that take values in
\{-1,1\}^{d}
.

Before developing our general approach, we describe the main ideas in the simplest Markov random field: an Ising model without
10
10

10

The presence of an external field does not change our method. It merely introduces an intercept in the logistic regression problem and comes at the cost of more cumbersome notation. All arguments below follow, potentially after minor modifications and explicit computations are left to the reader.
external field
[
VMLC16
]
. Such models specify the distribution of a random vector
Z=(Z^{(1)},\ldots,Z^{(d)})\in\{-1,1\}^{d}
as follows

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(Z=z)=\exp\big(z^{\top}Wz-\Phi(W)\big)\,,

(5.14)

where
W\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{pd\times d}
is an unknown matrix of interest with null diagonal elements and

\Phi(W)=\log\sum_{z\in\{-1,1\}^{d}}\exp\big(z^{\top}Wz\big)\,,

is a normalization term known as
log-partition function
.

Fix
\beta,\lambda>0
. Our goal in this section is to estimate the parameter
W
subject to the constraint that the
j
th row
e_{j}^{\top}W
of
W
satisfies
|e_{j}^{\top}W|_{1}\leq\lambda
.
To that end, we observe
n
independent copies
Z_{1},\ldots Z_{n}
of
Z\sim\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}
. To that end, estimate the matrix
W
row by row using constrained likelihood maximization. Recall from
[
RWL10
, Eq. (4)]
that for any
j\in[d]
, it holds for any
z^{(j)}\in\{-1,1\}
, that

\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{P}(Z^{(j)}=z^{(j)}|Z^{(\lnot j)}=z^{(\lnot j)})=\frac{\exp(2z^{(j)}e_{j}^{\top}Wz)}{1+\exp(2z^{(j)}e_{j}^{\top}Wz)}\,,

(5.15)

where we used the fact that the diagonal elements
W
are equal to zero.

Therefore, the
j
th row
e_{j}^{\top}W
of
W
may be estimated by performing a logistic regression of
Z^{(j)}
onto
Z^{(\lnot j)}
subject to the constraint that
|e_{j}^{\top}W|_{1}\leq\lambda
and
\|e_{j}^{\top}W\|_{\infty}\leq\beta
. To simplify notation, fix
j
and define
Y=Z^{(j)},X=Z^{(\lnot j)}
and
w^{*}=(e_{j}^{\top}W)^{(\lnot j)}
. Let
(X_{1},Y_{1}),\ldots,(X_{n},Y_{n})
be
n
independent copies of
(X,Y)
. Then the (rescaled) log-likelihood of a candidate parameter
w\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d-1}
is denoted by
\bar{\ell}_{n}(w)
and defined by

\displaystyle\bar{\ell}_{n}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\Big(\frac{\exp(2Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}{1+\exp(2Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\big(1+\exp(2Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)\big)

With this representation, it is easy to see that
w\mapsto\bar{\ell}_{n}(w)
is a concave function. The (constrained) maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)
\hat{w}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d-1}
is defined to be any solution of the following convex optimization problem:

\hat{w}\in\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}_{w\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\lambda)}\bar{\ell}_{n}(w)

(5.16)

This problem can be solved very efficiently using a variety of methods similar to the Lasso.

The following lemma follows from
[
Rig12
]
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Fix
\delta\in(0,1)
. Conditionally on
(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
, the constrained MLE
\hat{w}
defined in (
5.16
) satisfies with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}^{\top}(\hat{w}-w))^{2}\leq 2\lambda e^{\lambda}\sqrt{\frac{\log(2(p-1)/\delta)}{2n}}

Proof.

We begin with some standard manipulations of the log-likelihood. Note that

\displaystyle\log\Big(\frac{\exp(2Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}{1+\exp(2Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\log\Big(\frac{\exp(Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}{\exp(-Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)+\exp(Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}\Big)

\displaystyle=\log\Big(\frac{\exp(Y_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w)}{\exp(-X_{i}^{\top}w)+\exp(X_{i}^{\top}w)}\Big)

\displaystyle=(Y_{i}+1)X_{i}^{\top}w-\log\big(1+\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w)\big)

Therefore, writing
\tilde{Y}_{i}=(Y_{i}+1)/2\in\{0,1\}
, we get that
\hat{w}
is the solution to the following minimization problem:

\hat{w}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{w\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\lambda)}\bar{\kappa}_{n}(w)

where

\bar{\kappa}_{n}(w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{-2\tilde{Y}_{i}X_{i}^{\top}w+\log\big(1+\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w)\big)\big\}

For any
w\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d-1}
, write
\kappa(w)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\bar{\kappa}_{n}(w)]
where here and throughout this proof, all expectations are implicitly taken conditionally on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. Observe that

\kappa(w)=-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\tilde{Y}_{1}]X_{1}^{\top}w+\log\big(1+\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w)\big)\,.

Next, we get from the basic inequality
\bar{\kappa}_{n}(\hat{w})\leq\bar{\kappa}_{n}(w)
for any
w\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\lambda)
that

\displaystyle\kappa(\hat{w})-\kappa(w)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{Y}_{i}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\tilde{Y}_{i}])X_{i}^{\top}(\hat{w}-w)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\lambda}{n}\max_{1\leq j\leq p-1}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{Y}_{i}-\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[\tilde{Y}_{i}])X_{i}^{\top}e_{j}\Big|\,,

where in the second inequality, we used Hölder’s inequality and the fact
|X_{i}|_{\infty}\leq 1
for all
i\in[n]
.
Together with Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound, it yields

\kappa(\hat{w})-\kappa(w)\leq 2\lambda\sqrt{\frac{\log(4(p-1)/\delta)}{2n}}\,,\quad\text{with probability $1-\frac{\delta}{2}$.}

Note that the Hessian of
\kappa
is given by

\nabla^{2}\kappa(w)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{4\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w)}{(1+\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w))^{2}}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\,.

Moreover, for any
w\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(\lambda)
, since
\|X_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, it holds

\frac{4\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w)}{(1+\exp(2X_{i}^{\top}w))^{2}}\geq\frac{4e^{2\lambda}}{(1+e^{2\lambda})^{2}}\geq e^{-2\lambda}

Next, since
w^{*}
is a minimizer of
w\mapsto\kappa(w)
, we get from a second order Taylor expansion that

\kappa(\hat{w})-\kappa(w^{*})\geq e^{-2\lambda}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}^{\top}(\hat{w}-w))^{2}\,.

This completes our proof.
∎

Next, we bound from below the quantity

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}^{\top}(\hat{w}-w))^{2}\,.

To that end, we must exploit the covariance structure of the Ising model. The following lemma is similar to the combination of Lemmas 6 and 7 in
[
VMLC16
]
.

Lemma 5.22
.

Fix
R>0
,
\delta\in(0,1)
and let
Z\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
be distributed according to (
5.14
). The following holds with probability
1-\delta/2
, uniformly in
u\in\mathcal{B}_{1}(2\lambda)
:

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Z_{i}^{\top}u)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|u|_{\infty}^{2}\exp(-2\lambda)-4\lambda\sqrt{\frac{\log(2p(p-1)/\delta)}{2n}}

Proof.

Note that

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Z_{i}^{\top}u)^{2}=u^{\top}S_{n}u\,,

where
S_{n}\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d\times d}
denotes the sample covariance matrix of
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
, defined by

S_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}X_{i}^{\top}\,.

Observe that

u^{\top}S_{n}u\geq u^{\top}\Sigma u-2\lambda|S_{n}-S|_{\infty}\,,

(5.17)

where
S=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[S_{n}]
.
Using Hoeffding’s inequality together with a union bound, we get that

|S_{n}-S|_{\infty}\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{\log(2p(p-1)/\delta)}{2n}}

with probability
1-\delta/2
.

Moreover,
u^{\top}Su=\mathrm{var}(X^{\top}u)
. Assume without loss of generality that
|u^{(1)}|=|u|_{\infty}
and observe that

\mathrm{var}(Z^{\top}u)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[(Z^{\top}u)^{2}]=(u^{(1)})^{2}+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[\big(\sum_{j=2}^{p}u^{(j)}Z^{(j)}\big)^{2}\big]+2\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[u^{(1)}Z^{(1)}\sum_{j=2}^{p}u^{(j)}Z^{(j)}\big]\,.

To control the cross term, let us condition on the neighborhood
Z^{(\lnot 1)}
to get

\displaystyle 2\Big|\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[u^{(1)}Z^{(1)}\sum_{j=2}^{p}u^{(j)}Z^{(j)}\big]\Big|

\displaystyle=2\Big|\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[u^{(1)}Z^{(1)}|Z^{(\lnot 1)}\big]\sum_{j=2}^{p}u^{(j)}Z^{(j)}\big]\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big(\big(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\big[u^{(1)}Z^{(1)}|Z^{(\lnot 1)}\big]\big)^{2}\Big)+\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}\Big(\big(\sum_{j=2}^{p}u^{(j)}Z^{(j)}\big)^{2}\Big)\,,

where we used the fact that
2|ab|\leq a^{2}+b^{2}
for all
a,b\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}
. The above two displays together yield

\mathrm{var}(Z^{\top}u)\geq|u|_{\infty}^{2}(1-\sup_{z\in\{-1,1\}^{p-1}}\big(\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z^{(1)}|Z^{(\lnot 1)}=z]\big)^{2}\big)

To control the above supremum, recall that from (
5.15
), we have

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\{-1,1\}^{p-1}}\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[Z^{(1)}|Z^{(\lnot 1)}=z]

\displaystyle=\sup_{z\in\{-1,1\}^{p}}\frac{\exp(2e_{1}^{\top}Wz)-1}{\exp(2e_{1}^{\top}Wz)+1}=\frac{\exp(2\lambda)-1}{\exp(2\lambda)+1}\,.

Therefore,

\mathrm{var}(Z^{\top}u)\geq\frac{|u|_{\infty}^{2}}{1+\exp(2\lambda)}\geq\frac{1}{2}|u|_{\infty}^{2}\exp(-\lambda)\,.

Together with (
5.17
), it yields the desired result.
∎

Combining the above two lemmas immediately yields the following theorem.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
\hat{w}
be the constrained maximum likelihood estimator (
5.16
) and let
w^{*}=(e_{j}^{\top}W)^{(\lnot j)}
be the
j
th row of
W
with the
j
th entry removed. Then with probability
1-\delta
we have

|\hat{w}-w^{*}|_{\infty}^{2}\leq 9\lambda\exp(3\lambda)\sqrt{\frac{\log(2p^{2}/\delta)}{n}}\,.

r

\mathbb{X}

Problem 5.4
.

In the Markowitz theory of portfolio selection
[
Mar52
]
, a portfolio may be identified to a vector
u\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
such that
u_{j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{d}u_{j}=1
. In this case,
u_{j}
represents the proportion of the portfolio invested in asset
j
. The vector of (random) returns of
d
assets is denoted by
X\in\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{R}^{d}
and we assume that
X\sim\mathsf{subG}_{d}(1)
and
\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[XX^{\top}]=\Sigma
unknown.t

In this theory, the two key characteristics of a portfolio
u
are it’s
reward

\mu(u)=\mathrm{I}\kern-1.79993pt\mathrm{E}[u^{\top}X]
and its
risk

R(u)=\mathrm{var}{X^{\top}u}
. According to this theory one should fix a minimum reward
\lambda>0
and choose the optimal portfolio

u^{*}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{u\,:\mu(u)\geq\lambda}R(u)

when a solution exists for a given. It is the portfolio that has minimum risk among all portfolios with reward at least
\lambda
, provided such portfolios exist.

In practice, the distribution of
X
is unknown. Assume that we observe
n
independent copies
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
of
X
and use them to compute the following estimators of
\mu(u)
and
R(u)
respectively:

\displaystyle\hat{\mu}(u)

\displaystyle=\bar{X}^{\top}u=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\top}u\,,

\displaystyle\hat{R}(u)

\displaystyle=u^{\top}\hat{\Sigma}u,\quad\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(X_{i}-\bar{X})(X_{i}-\bar{X})^{\top}\,.

We use the following estimated portfolio:

\hat{u}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{u\,:\hat{\mu}(u)\geq\lambda}\hat{R}(u)

We assume throughout that
\log d\ll n\ll d
.

1.

Show that for any portfolio
u
, it holds

\big|\hat{\mu}(u)-\mu(u)\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,,

and

\big|\hat{R}(u)-R(u)\big|\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,.

2.

Show that

\hat{R}(\hat{u})-R(\hat{u})\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{\log d}{n}}\,,

with probability .99.

3.

Define the estimator
\tilde{u}
by:

\tilde{u}=\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{u\,:\hat{\mu}(u)\geq\lambda-\varepsilon}\hat{R}(u)

find the smallest
\varepsilon>0
(up to multiplicative constant) such that we have
R(\tilde{u})\leq R(u^{*})
with probability .99.
